The 27 27 geodesic networks in the directed landscape
Duncan Dauvergne

Abstract
The directed landscape is a random directed metric on the plane that arises as the scaling limit of classical metric models in the KPZ universality class. Typical pairs of points in the directed landscape are connected by a unique geodesic. However, there are exceptional pairs of points connected by more complicated geodesic networks. We show that up to isomorphism there are exactly 27 27 geodesic networks that appear in the directed landscape, and find Hausdorff dimensions in a scaling-adapted metric on ℝ ↑ 4 \mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow} for the sets of endpoints of each of these networks.



1
Introduction

The Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) universality class is a collection of one-dimensional random growth models and two-dimensional random metrics that are all expected to exhibit the same limiting behaviour under rescaling. The past thirty years has seen a period of intense research on this class thanks to the discovery of a handful of exactly solvable models, see Section
1.3
for background.

The directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
was constructed in
[
DOV23
]
as the full scaling limit of one particular two-dimensional random metric (Brownian last passage percolation) in the KPZ class. More recently,
\mathcal{L}
has been shown to be the scaling limit of a handful of other exactly solvable random metrics
[
DV21
]
and of the KPZ equation
[
Wu23
]
, see also
[
Vir20
,
QS23
]
. It is conjectured to be the full scaling limit of all KPZ models.

The directed landscape is a random continuous function from

\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}=\{u=(p;q)=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:s<t\}

to
\mathbb{R}
. The value
\mathcal{L}(p;q)
is best thought of as a distance between two points
p
and
q
in the space-time plane. Here
x,y
are spatial coordinates and
s,t
are time coordinates. Unlike with an ordinary metric,
\mathcal{L}
is not symmetric, does not assign distances to every pair of points in the plane, and may take negative values. It also satisfies the triangle inequality backwards:

\mathcal{L}(p;r)\geq\mathcal{L}(p;q)+\mathcal{L}(q;r)\qquad\mbox{ for all }(p;r),(p;q),(q;r)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}.

(1)

Just as in true metric spaces, we can define path lengths in
\mathcal{L}
, see
[
DOV23
, Section 12]
. First, for a continuous function
\pi:[s,t]\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, referred to as a path, let
\bar{\pi}(r)=(\pi(r),r)
. We define the
length
of
\pi
by

\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}}=\inf_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\inf_{s=t_{0}<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{k}=t}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}(t_{i-1});\bar{\pi}(t_{i}))\,.

(2)

By the triangle inequality (
1
)
,
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}}\leq\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}(s);\bar{\pi}(t))
for any path
\pi
.
We say that
\pi
is a
geodesic
from
\bar{\pi}(s)
to
\bar{\pi}(t)
if

\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}}=\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}(s);\bar{\pi}(t))\,.
We write

\mathfrak{g}\pi=\{\bar{\pi}(r):r\in[s,t]\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}

for the image of
\pi
in the space-time plane. By
[
DOV23
]
, Theorem
12.1
, for any
(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, almost surely there exists a unique geodesic
\pi
from
p
to
q
. However, there are exceptional points
(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
where multiple geodesics go from
p
to
q
. The goal of this paper is to fully understand what happens at these points.

For every
(p;q)=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
define the
geodesic network
from
p
to
q
by

\Gamma(u)=\bigcup\{\mathfrak{g}\pi:\pi\text{ is a geodesic from }p\text{ to }q\}.

It turns out that any pair
(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
is connected by only finitely many geodesics. Moreover, from the definition of path length it is not difficult to check that any path
\tau:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}
with
\mathfrak{g}\tau\subset\Gamma(p;q)
is a geodesic from
p
to
q
. Together these two points imply that
\Gamma(p;q)
has the topological structure of a finite directed graph. Indeed, for
(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
we can define the
network graph

G(p;q)
of its geodesic network with the following two rules:

•

The vertices
V
of
G(p;q)
consist of the points
p,q
and all points
v=(z,r)\in\Gamma(p;q)
such that there exist two geodesics
\pi,\tau
from
p
to
q
with
\pi(r)=\tau(r)=z
but
\pi|_{[r-\epsilon,r+\epsilon]}\neq\tau|_{[r-\epsilon,r+\epsilon]}
for all
\epsilon>0
.

•

For two vertices
v_{1}=(z_{1},r_{1}),v_{2}=(z_{2},r_{2})\in V
with
r_{1}<r_{2}
, we add one copy of the directed edge
(v_{1},v_{2})
to the edge set
E
for every distinct path
\pi:[r_{1},r_{2}]\to\mathbb{R}
with
\mathfrak{g}\pi\subset\Gamma(p;q)
and
\mathfrak{g}\pi\cap V=\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
.

See Figure
2
for an example of
\Gamma(p;q),G(p;q)
.
Now, for a finite directed graph
G
, let
G^{T}
denote the transpose of
G
, given by reversing the direction of all the edges of
G
. Since the directed landscape has a time-reversal symmetry and is ergodic (see Section
2
), a graph
G
will appear as a geodesic network in
\mathcal{L}
if and only if
G^{T}
appears. Because of this, we will only distinguish directed graphs
G

up to transpose isomorphism

\sim
(e.g.
G\sim H
if either
G
or
G^{T}
is isomorphic to
H
as a directed graph), see Figure
2
for an example.
Our first main theorem classifies all the network graphs that appear in
\mathcal{L}
.

Figure 2
:
An example of the procedure for taking the geodesic network
\Gamma(p;q)
to the directed graph
G(p;q)
, and an example of two transpose-isomorphic networks.

Theorem 1.1
.

Almost surely, the following holds in the directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
. A directed graph
G=(V,E)
is the network graph of some point
(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
if and only if the following conditions hold:

1.

G
is finite, planar, and loop-free.

2.

There is exactly one
source
vertex
p\in V
(i.e. a vertex with no incoming edges) and exactly one
sink
vertex
q\in V
(i.e. a vertex with no outgoing edges).

3.

The induced graph on
V\setminus\{p,q\}
has no undirected cycles (i.e. the underlying undirected graph is a forest).

4.

Every vertex in
V\setminus\{p,q\}
has degree
3
.

5.

The vertices
p,q
have degree
1,2,
or
3
.

There are exactly
27
graphs that satisfy these rules, enumerated in Section
8
. We call the set of these networks
\mathscr{G}
.

Now, for every
G\in\mathscr{G}
, define

N_{\mathcal{L}}(G)=\{(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}:G(p;q)=G\}.

As mentioned previously, for a typical point
(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
there is unique geodesic from
p
to
q
. All other networks are rare. Our next theorem aims to classify exactly how rare these networks are. We focus on three properties:

•

The size of
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G)
.

•

Whether
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G)
is dense.

•

The Hausdorff dimension
\dim_{1:2:3}(N_{\mathcal{L}}(G))
in the 1:2:3-metric on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}

given by

d_{1:2:3}((x,s;y,t),(x^{\prime},s^{\prime};y^{\prime},t^{\prime}))=|t-t^{\prime}|^{1/3}+|s-s^{\prime}|^{1/3}+|x-x^{\prime}|^{1/2}+|y-y^{\prime}|^{1/2}.

The 1:2:3-metric is set up to take into account the natural KPZ scaling associated with the directed landscape. Indeed, since
\mathcal{L}
is Hölder-
1/2^{-}
in
x,y
and Hölder-
1/3^{-}
in
s,t
, defining Hausdorff dimensions under the
d_{1:2:3}
-metric is a good proxy for defining Hausdorff dimensions using the ‘metric’
\mathcal{L}
. It does not make sense to define Hausdorff dimensions using
\mathcal{L}
directly since it is not a true metric (i.e. it may take on positive or negative values).
We do not address the issue of the Euclidean dimensions of each of the sets
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G)
in this paper.

Theorem 1.2
.

Almost surely, the following assertions hold. Let
G=(V,E)\in\mathscr{G}
, let
p,q
denote the unique source and sink vertices in
G
, and define

d(G):=12-\frac{|V|+\deg^{2}(p)+\deg^{2}(q)}{2}.

Then:

1.

\dim_{1:2:3}(N_{\mathcal{L}}(G))=d(G).

2.

If
d(G)=0
, then
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G)
is countably infinite.

3.

N_{\mathcal{L}}(G)
is dense if there is an edge
e\in E
such that
(V,E\setminus\{e\})
is disconnected as an undirected graph. Otherwise,
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G)
is nowhere dense.

It is not difficult to check that
0\leq d(G)\leq 10=\dim_{1:2:3}(\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow})
for all
G\in\mathscr{G}
, see Lemma
6.1
.
Note that we have chosen not the retain the planar structure when classifying geodesic networks in Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
. A version of these theorems that takes into account the planar structure of networks also follows from the methods in this paper.

Remark 1.3
.

The question of which geodesic networks are dense was studied in
[
AKM17
]
for the Brownian map and in
[
Gwy21
]
for general Liouville quantum gravity for
\gamma\in(0,2)
. It turns out that the types of dense geodesic networks are exactly the same in the directed landscape and for these models. There are six such networks, enumerated in Figure
3
. These networks are uniquely determined by
\deg(p),\deg(q)
, and the fact that they can be disconnected by a single edge. In
[
AKM17
]
and
[
Gwy21
]
, these networks are referred to as
(\deg(p),\deg(q))
-
normal networks
.
The geometric similarities between the directed landscape and the Liouville quantum gravity also go beyond the classification of dense networks; we discuss this more in Section
1.2
.

We can pass from results about geodesic networks to the simpler question of counting points
(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
connected by exactly
k
geodesics.

Corollary 1.4
.

For every
(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, let
T(p;q)
be the number of distinct geodesics from
p
to
q
. Then almost surely, the following statements hold.

1.

T(p;q)\leq 9
for all
(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
.

2.

We have

\displaystyle\dim_{1:2:3}T^{-1}(1)=10,

\displaystyle\dim_{1:2:3}T^{-1}(2)=8,

\displaystyle\dim_{1:2:3}T^{-1}(3)=6,

\displaystyle\dim_{1:2:3}T^{-1}(4)=6,

\displaystyle\dim_{1:2:3}T^{-1}(5)=3,

\displaystyle\dim_{1:2:3}T^{-1}(6)=3

and
T^{-1}(7),T^{-1}(8),T^{-1}(9)
are all countable.

(a)

(b)

(c)

(d)

(e)

(f)

:
The six dense geodesic networks.

1.1
Geometric ideas in the proof

Here we outline the main geometric ideas that underlie the proofs of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
. We do not discuss the technical probabilistic and analytic methods that go into the proofs in order to avoid complicating the geometric picture.

Coalescent geometry.
A key feature of the directed landscape and other models of continuum random geometry that distinguishes these objects from their Euclidean counterparts is geodesic coalescence: if two geodesics are close together, then they will typically coalesce in order to take advantage of certain favourable geodesic highways. Because of this, geodesics in the directed landscape are best thought of as driving routes in a city; geodesics between two points
p
and
q
will follow major geodesic routes away the endpoints
p,q
. The study of coalescence in the context of the KPZ universality class goes back at least to the work of Newman and coauthors on first passage percolation (e.g. see
[
New95
]
and references therein) and is by this point very well understood in the directed landscape, e.g. see
[
BGH22
,
DSV22
,
BSS22
,
Bha23
]
.

One useful perspective on coalescent geometry is the
overlap topology
on geodesics. We say that a sequence of geodesics
\pi_{n}
converges in overlap to
\pi:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}
if the endpoints of
\pi_{n}
converge to the endpoints of
\pi
, and if the set

O^{o}(\pi_{n},\pi):=\{r\in(s,t):\pi_{n}(r)=\pi(r)\}

is an interval whose endpoints converge to
s,t
.
Many useful properties of coalescent geometry can be expressed in terms of overlap. While many results about coalescence are used throughout the paper, for the purposes of this proof sketch, we will only appeal to the following two consequences of coalescent geometry recorded in Lemma
3.3
:

•

Rational overlap approximation
.
\Gamma(\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}\cap\mathbb{Q}^{4})
is dense in
\Gamma(\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow})
. In other words, any geodesic can be approximated in overlap by a sequence of geodesics with rational endpoints.

•

No geodesic bubbles
.   When combined with the fact that typical (i.e. rational) points in
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
are only connected by a single geodesic, rational overlap approximation implies that there are no geodesic bubbles, i.e. if
p,q
are any points on the interior of a common geodesic then
p
and
q
are connected by a unique geodesic.

Just before this paper was posted, Bhatia
[
Bha23
]
developed independent proofs of these two bullet points. His proof methods are quite different from ours.

Geodesic stars.
Consider a collection of geodesics
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots\pi_{k})
defined on a common interval
[s,t]
. We call
\pi
a
forwards
k
-geodesic star
from a point
p
to
(\mathbf{x},t)
where

\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<}:=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:x_{1}<\dots<x_{k}\}

if
\bar{\pi}_{i}(s)=p,\bar{\pi}_{i}(t)=(x_{i},t)
for all
i
, and if
\pi_{i}(r)\neq\pi_{j}(r)
for all
i\neq j,r\in(s,t]
. Similarly,
\pi
is a
reverse
k
-geodesic star
from
(\mathbf{y},s)
to
q
if
\bar{\pi}_{i}(t)=q,\bar{\pi}_{i}(s)=(y_{i},s)
for all
i
and if
\pi_{i}(r)\neq\pi_{j}(r)
for all
i\neq j,r\in[s,t)
. We call
p
a
k
-star point
if it is the starting point of a forwards
k
-geodesic star or the ending point of a reverse geodesic
k
-star.

The study of coalescent geometry in
\mathcal{L}
implies that for any
(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, if we look at the set of all geodesics from
p
to
q
and remove a small ball around
p
and
q
, then we must be left with a typical geodesic forest. Therefore if
G(p;q)
is an exceptional graph (i.e. not the trivial two-point graph) it is because either
p
is a
k
-star point for some
k\geq 2
or
q
is an
\ell
-star point for some
\ell\geq 2
(or both). Because of this, in order to classify geodesic networks it is logical to first classify star points. To this end, in Section
4
we prove the following theorem.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
\operatorname{Star}_{k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the set of all
k
-star points. Then almost surely we have

\dim_{1:2:3}(\operatorname{Star}_{1})=5,\quad\dim_{1:2:3}(\operatorname{Star}_{2})=4,\quad\dim_{1:2:3}(\operatorname{Star}_{3})=2,\quad\operatorname{Star}_{4}=\emptyset.

Here
\dim_{1:2:3}
is Hausdorff dimension with respect to the metric

d_{1:2:3}((x,s),(y,t))=|t-s|^{1/3}+|y-x|^{1/2}.

Cut and paste: from geodesic stars to geodesic networks.
To move from understanding geodesic stars to understanding geodesic networks, the basic idea is as follows.
Fix a candidate network graph
G
whose source and sink
p
and
q
have degrees
k
and
\ell
, respectively, and suppose
(p;q)=(x,s;y,t)\in N_{\mathcal{L}}(G)
. Let
\epsilon>0
be small enough so that no branching in
\Gamma(p;q)
occurs in the intervals
[s,s+\epsilon]
and
[t-\epsilon,t]
. Cut the geodesic network
\Gamma(p;q)
into three pieces defined on the strips

\mathbb{R}\times[s,s+\epsilon],\qquad\mathbb{R}\times[s+\epsilon,t-\epsilon],\qquad\mathbb{R}\times[t-\epsilon,t]

and let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}_{<}
be such that
\{(x_{1},s+\epsilon),\dots,(x_{k},s+\epsilon)\}=\Gamma(p;q)\cap(\mathbb{R}\times\{s+\epsilon\})
and
\{(y_{1},t-\epsilon),\dots,(y_{\ell},t-\epsilon)\}=\Gamma(p;q)\cap(\mathbb{R}\times\{t-\epsilon\})
. This splitting of
\Gamma(p;q)
leaves us with:

•

A forwards geodesic
k
-star
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k})
from
p
to
(\mathbf{x},s+\epsilon)
.

•

An interior network
I
consisting of all geodesics from
(x_{i},s+\epsilon)
to
(y_{j},t-\epsilon)
whenever those two points lie on a common geodesic from
p
to
q
. This network has a graph structure given by taking
G
, splitting its source vertex into
k
degree-one sources and splitting its sink vertex into
\ell
degree-one sinks.

•

A reverse geodesic
\ell
-star
\tau=(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{\ell})
from
(\mathbf{y},t-\epsilon)
to
q
.

Therefore the problem of determining the dimension and size of
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G)
is essentially the problem of determining the dimension and size of the set of all compatible triples
\pi,I,\tau
.

To think about the size of this set, we first consider the possibilities for
I
. By the rational overlap approximation discussed above there are only countably many possibilities for
I
so for all intents and purposes we can focus on determining geodesics whose networks use a single fixed
I
. Moreover,
I
is homeomorphic to a network obtained by shifting
x_{i},y_{j}
to nearby rational points. Because of this, we expect that
I
is generic. Here this means that
I
can only be a forest whose interior branch points all have degree
3
; heuristically, higher degree branch points should be ‘lower dimensional’ and so will not appear in a generic
I
. The network
I
is a forest since there are no geodesic bubbles.

On the other hand, the choice of
I
does put a series of constraints on the lengths of the paths
\pi_{i},\tau_{j}
. Indeed, for all paths through
G(p;q)
to have the same length, we require that all of the lengths

\mathcal{L}(p;x_{i},s+\epsilon)+\mathcal{L}(x_{i},s+\epsilon;t-\epsilon,y_{j})+\mathcal{L}(t-\epsilon,y_{j};q)=\|\pi_{i}\|_{\mathcal{L}}+\mathcal{L}(x_{i},s+\epsilon;t-\epsilon,y_{j})+\|\tau_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}}

are equal whenever
(x_{i},s+\epsilon)
and
(y_{j},t-\epsilon)
are connected in
\Gamma(p;q)\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[s+\epsilon,t-\epsilon])
. Letting
|F^{*}|
denote the number of
bounded faces
in
\Gamma(p;q)
, this puts
|F^{*}|
linearly independent linear constraints on the weight vector

(\mathcal{L}(p;x_{1},s+\epsilon),\dots,\mathcal{L}(p;x_{k},s+\epsilon),\mathcal{L}(t-\epsilon,y_{1};q),\dots,\mathcal{L}(t-\epsilon,y_{\ell};q)),

(3)

see Figure
4
for an example.
Heuristically, if the distribution of the length vector (
3
) for pairs of uniformly chosen geodesic stars is uniformly spread out in
\mathbb{R}^{k+\ell}
, then the set of pairs of geodesic stars that satisfy these linear constraints should have dimension

\dim_{1:2:3}(\operatorname{Star}_{k})+\dim_{1:2:3}(\operatorname{Star}_{\ell})-|F^{*}|.

(4)

In other words, the dimension should reduce by one for every linearly independent linear constraint. The fact that the dimension should decrease by exactly one is coming from the fact that
d_{1:2:3}
is a proxy for the metric dimension, so the function mapping
(p;q)
to the length vector in (
3
) is Hölder-
(1-\epsilon)
for all
\epsilon>0
.

By using Euler’s formula for planar graphs, and the fact that all interior vertices of
G
have degree
3
, we can rewrite (
4
) as

\dim_{1:2:3}(\operatorname{Star}_{k})+\dim_{1:2:3}(\operatorname{Star}_{\ell})+2-\frac{|V|+k+\ell}{2},

(5)

which by Theorem
1.5
equals
d(G)
in Theorem
1.2
.

Figure 4
:
An example of a potential configuration of geodesics, with geodesic stars around the endpoints indicated in red, and lengths labelled by
w_{i},u_{i},v_{i}
. For all paths here to be geodesics, we require that
w_{2}+u_{2}=w_{1}+u_{1}+u_{3}
, and
u_{3}+u_{5}+v_{2}=u_{4}+v_{1}
. In other words, we have two linear restrictions on the weights of the stars at
p
and
q
: one for each of the bounded faces of the candidate network.

Making the above discussion rigorous is delicate. Indeed, we first need a more technical version of Theorem
1.5
that also takes into account the weight vector (
3
). We prove this more technical statement (see Propositions
4.1
and
4.4
) while showing Theorem
1.5
. Next, we need to understand the sizes of candidate triples
(\pi,I,\tau)
. There are many nuances here, but the mains ones involve proving sufficient independence of the weights of
\pi,I,\tau
, and giving methods to ‘cut’ and ‘paste’ that allow us to go back and forth between networks and triples
\pi,I,\tau
.

1.2
The Brownian map and Liouville quantum gravity

As mentioned in Remark
1.3
, there are random surface models that exhibit geodesic geometry that is remarkably similar to that of the directed landscape, and many theorems for
\mathcal{L}
have parallels in these models. Geometric proof techniques for studying geodesics on random surfaces are also of use in studying the directed landscape. For example, we have taken inspiration from the topological ideas in
[
Gwy21
]
in proving bounds in Sections
4.1
,
4.3
and the central role coalescence plays in our proofs is similar to the central role it plays in
[
LG10
,
AKM17
,
MQ20
,
Gwy21
]
. In this section we discuss the similarities between the directed landscape and random surfaces with the goal of developing (conjectural) versions of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
for these models.

The most well-understood random surface model is the Brownian map. The Brownian map is a random metric space that arises as the scaling limit of uniformly chosen random planar maps
[
LG13
,
Mie13
]
, see
[
LG19
]
and references therein for more background.
The Brownian map is part of a family of random surfaces known as Liouville quantum gravity (LQG) indexed by a parameter
\gamma
(the Brownian map corresponds to
\gamma=\sqrt{8/3}
).
Just as the Brownian map arises as the scaling limit of uniformly chosen chosen random planar maps, general LQG models are expected to arise as the scaling limit of random planar maps weighted by a partition function for a statistical mechanics model.

Roughly speaking, Liouville quantum gravity on a domain
U\subset\mathbb{C}
indexed by
\gamma\in(0,2)
is the random Riemannian manifold with Riemannian metric tensor
e^{\gamma h(x+iy)}(dx^{2}+dy^{2})
, where
h
is a Gaussian free field. This is not well-defined since
h
is not a function. However, it is possible to make sense of LQG rigorously using a regularization procedure, see
[
BP21
,
Gwy19
,
DDG21
]
and references therein for more background.

Geodesics in the Brownian map and in LQG for
\gamma\in(0,2)
have been studied in great detail. Notable result include the classification of all geodesic networks starting at a fixed point in the Brownian map
[
LG10
]
; the classification of dense geodesic networks in the Brownian map
[
AKM17
]
and in LQG for
\gamma\in(0,2)

[
Gwy21
]
; and the determination of the Hausdorff dimension of pairs of points in the Brownian map connected by
k
geodesics for all
1\leq k\leq 9
(as in the directed landscape, no pairs are connected by more than
9
geodesics)
[
MQ20
]
. The paper
[
MQ20
]
also goes much further, giving Hausdorff dimension upper bounds that are expected to be sharp and finding restrictions on the types of geodesic networks that appear in the Brownian map.

Our belief is that there are only a few qualitative mechanisms driving the geodesic geometry in these models: coalescence (see
[
LG10
,
GM20
,
MQ20
,
Gwy21
]
for results in the Brownian map and LQG), planarity, and rough independence of the metric in different spatial regions. Because of this, it is reasonable to expect that much of the geometric picture driving Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
goes through for these models.

Indeed, if a graph
G
is a network graph in
\gamma
-LQG then we expect that
G
should satisfy points
1,2,3,
and
4
of Theorem
1.1
.
Point
5
should not necessarily hold since this point relates to the possible dimensions of star points, a quantitative feature which is not the same in the directed landscape and in these other models.
The analogue of
\dim_{1:2:3}
in these models is simply the Hausdorff dimension in the LQG metric, which we will denote by
\dim
. We expect that the formulas (
4
)/(
5
) should also hold in these models with
\dim
in place of
\dim_{1:2:3}
.

For the special case of the Brownian map, the star sets are known to have dimensions

\dim(\operatorname{Star_{k}})=5-k

(6)

for
k=1,2,3,4,5
. No geodesic
6
-star points exist and the question of whether geodesic
5
-star points exist is open. The upper bound here was proven in
[
MQ20
]
and the lower bound in
[
LG22
]
. This suggests the following formula for the Hausdorff dimension of points whose network graph is
G=(V,E)
:

10-k-\ell-|F^{*}|=12-\frac{3(k+\ell)+|V|}{2}.

(7)

Formula (
7
) agrees with the upper bound on the Hausdorff dimension obtained in
[
MQ20
, Theorem 1.5]
. Note that their formula is expressed in terms of a different graph invariant (splitting points).

Formula (
7
) is non-negative whenever
k,\ell\leq 3
and so we expect that each of the
27
network graphs in the directed landscape also occur the Brownian map. We consider the cases when
k\vee\ell\geq 4
. Without loss of generality, assume
k\geq 4
.
Formula (
7
) is negative whenever
k+\ell\geq 8
, so no geodesic networks should exist with these parameters in the Brownian map. Next, if we think about the argument leading to (
4
) carefully, we can observe if a bounded face of
G
is incident to the source vertex
p
but not the sink
q
, then the linear constraint from that face only involves the weight of the
k
-geodesic star at
p
. Therefore if
|F^{*}_{p}|
is the number of bounded faces of
G
incident to
p
but not
q
, we should require
\dim(\operatorname{Star_{k}})-|F^{*}_{p}|\geq 0
for the network to exist. Since
|F^{*}_{p}|\geq k-\ell
, by (
6
) a geodesic network
G
will not exist if
k\geq 4,k-\ell\geq 2
.

Therefore the only new geodesic networks in the Brownian map will have
k=4,\ell=3
, and
|V|\leq 3
. Up to transpose-isomorphism there is exactly one such network, exhibited in Figure
5
(a). In summary, we expect that there are exactly
27+1=28
network graphs that appear in the Brownian map: the
27
network graphs that appear in the directed landscape, along with the single network graph exhibited in Figure
5
(a).

(a)

(b)

Figure 5
:
The two geodesic networks that we expect to appear in certain models of Liouville quantum gravity, but do not appear in the directed landscape.

For general
\gamma
-LQG with
\gamma\in(0,2)
, the picture becomes hazier because star dimensions are not known. However, the set of points joined to the origin by at least three geodesics is known to be countable
[
Gwy21
]
. This fact and a coalescence heuristic strongly suggests that the set of points whose network is that of Figure
3
(f) is countable as well. By formula (
5
), we can then conclude that
\dim(\operatorname{Star_{3}})
should equal
2
, as is the case for the Brownian map and the directed landscape. Now, it is reasonable to expect that for
k\geq 4
we have

\dim(\operatorname{Star_{k}})<\dim(\operatorname{Star_{3}})=2<\dim(\operatorname{Star_{2}})<\dim(\operatorname{Star_{1}}).

(8)

Under assumption (
8
), formula (
5
) will be non-negative for all of the
27
networks in
\mathcal{L}
, suggesting that each of these networks appear in
\gamma
-LQG. Next, as in the Brownian map case we do not expect to see networks with

\dim(\operatorname{Star_{k}})-|F_{p}^{*}|\leq\dim(\operatorname{Star_{k}})-(k-\ell)<0.

By (
8
), this rules out network graphs
G
with with
k\geq 4,\ell\leq 2
. For a graph
G=(V,E)
with
k\geq 4,\ell\geq 3
, under (
8
) and (
5
),
G
cannot be a geodesic network unless
|V|+k+\ell<12
, leaving us with only possibilities when
k=4,\ell=3,|V|\leq 4
or
k=\ell=4,|V|\leq 3
. The two possible networks satisfying these constraints are listed in Figure
5
. In summary, we expect the following.

Conjecture 1.6
.

Fix
\gamma\in(0,2)
. There are either
27,28,
or
29
network graphs that appear in
\gamma
-LQG: the
27
networks that appear in the directed landscape, along with the network in Figure
5
(a) if
\dim(\operatorname{Star}_{4})\geq 1
and the network in Figure
5
(b) if
\dim(\operatorname{Star}_{4})\geq 3/2
. Geodesic networks occur with dimensions given by formula (
4
)/(
5
).

1.3
Previous work

Here we give a brief review of the literature on the KPZ universality class and the directed landscape, focusing on papers most closely related to the present work. For a gentle introduction to KPZ suitable for a newcomer to the area, see
[
Rom15
]
or the introductory articles
[
Cor16
,
Gan21
]
. Review articles and books that go into more depth include
[
FS10
,
Qua11
,
WFS17
,
Zyg22
]
.

Most of the work on the KPZ universality class over the past twenty-five years has been devoted to studying exact formulas for integrable models. This work gives exact formulas for many marginals of the directed landscape.
For example, the Baik-Deift-Johansson theorem
[
BDJ99
]
on the length of the longest increasing subsequence identified the one-point distribution
\mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)
as a GUE Tracy-Widom random variable, see also
[
Joh00
]
. Prähofer and Spohn
[
PS02
]
proved convergence of the multi-layer PNG droplet to the parabolic Airy line ensemble
\mathfrak{A}=\{\mathfrak{A}_{i}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},i\in\mathbb{N}\}
. The function
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
is the marginal
y\mapsto\mathcal{L}(0,0;y,1)
.
More recently, Johansson and Rahman
[
JR21
]
and Liu
[
Liu22a
]
independently found formulas for the joint distribution of
\{\mathcal{L}(p;q_{i}):i\in\{1,\dots,k\}\}
, building on work from
[
Joh17
,
Joh19
,
BL19
]
. Matetski, Quastel, and Remenik
[
MQR21
]
derived a formula for the distribution of
h_{t}(y)=\max_{x\in\mathbb{R}}g(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t)
for a fixed function
g
. The function
h_{t}
is the KPZ fixed point: it is the scaling limit for random growth models in the KPZ universality class.

To answer many questions about the KPZ universality class, it is useful to have probabilistic descriptions of the limit objects. In this direction, Corwin and Hammond
[
CH14
]
rigorously showed that the parabolic Airy line ensemble
\mathfrak{A}
is a system of nonintersecting curves by showing that
\mathfrak{A}
satisfies a certain Brownian Gibbs resampling property, see Section
2.2
. This property turns out to be extremely useful for understanding probabilistic questions about
\mathcal{L}
. Indeed,
[
CH14
]
used it to quickly show that
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
is locally Brownian. Moreover, it can be used to give strong regularity properties and two-point estimates for
\mathcal{L}
e.g. see
[
Ham22
,
Ham19
,
CHH19
,
DB21
,
Dau23b
]
. The Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}(x,y)=\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,1)
was built from
\mathfrak{A}
in
[
DOV23
]
by taking the limit of a certain combinatorial isometry, with a probabilistic analysis of
\mathfrak{A}
used as a key input. We require probabilistic analysis of
\mathfrak{A}
based on results from
[
DZ21
,
Dau23b
]
at various points in this paper, most crucially in the proof of the upper bound in Theorem
1.5
.

Since
[
DOV23
]
first appeared online, there has been a period of intense research on the structure of geodesics in
\mathcal{L}
. The paper
[
DOV23
]
proved basic properties of geodesics: almost sure uniqueness and Hölder continuity. Bates, Ganguly, and Hammond
[
BGH22
]
showed that the Euclidean Hausdorff dimension of the set of pairs
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
for which there are two disjoint geodesics from
(x,0)
to
(y,1)
equals
1/2
. Ganguly and Zhang
[
GZ22
]
showed that the Euclidean Hausdorff dimension of the set of points
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
from which there is a geodesic
2
-star to a fixed pair of points
(x_{1},0),(x_{2},0)
has Hausdorff dimension
5/3
. Bhatia
[
Bha23
]
showed that the set of points
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
that have
3
distinct infinite geodesics in a fixed direction has Euclidean Hausdorff dimension
4/3
, and the set of points
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
that have
2
distinct infinite geodesics in a fixed direction is countable. See also
[
RV21
,
DSV22
,
SS21
,
BSS22
,
Liu22b
,
Bha22
]
for more work on geodesics and
[
GH22
]
for a general review of fractal geometry in the directed landscape.

Organization of the paper.
Section
2
is a preliminary section. Section
3
contains basics about coalescent geometry in
\mathcal{L}
, and contains the weak estimate that every pair of points is connected by at most
121
geodesics. Section
4
is devoted to understanding geodesic stars. Section
5
allows us to conclude that there are at most
27
geodesic networks in
\mathcal{L}
; proof-wise, it is fairly similar to the upper bounds in Section
4
. Section
6
makes rigorous the cut and paste heuristics leading to formula (
4
). This section completes the proof of Theorem
1.1
and parts
1
and
2
of Theorem
1.2
. Section
7
proves Theorem
1.2
.3. The final Section
8
is a bookkeeping section where we enumerate the
27
networks (with justification). It contains figures of the
27
networks that may interest the reader.

Notational conventions.
All Brownian motions and Brownian bridges throughout the paper will have diffusion parameter
2
, as is the convention for KPZ limits. Large constants are typically denoted
c,c^{\prime},c_{1}
etc. and may change from line to line within proofs. We use a subscript
a,k
etc. (i.e.
c_{a,k}
) if a constant depends on parameters
a,k
etc. We write
\llbracket m,n\rrbracket
for the integer interval
\{m,\dots,n\}
and write
\mathcal{C}^{k}(X)
for the space of continuous functions from a topological space
X
to
\mathbb{R}^{k}
with the topology of uniform-on-compact convergence. When
k=1
we omit it from the notation. We write
\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<}=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:x_{1}<\dots<x_{k}\}
and similarly use the notation
\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq},\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\geq}
etc. We often write
a^{k}:=(a,\dots,a)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Acknowledgements.
I would like to thank Bálint Virág for many insightful discussions. Indeed, this paper arose out of an afternoon of conjectural enumeration from mid-2019 resulting in a set of
27
scribbled diagrams we labelled ‘Botany of the directed landscape’. I would also like to thank Manan Bhatia, Ofer Busani, and Ewain Gwynne for helpful comments regarding a previous version of the paper.

2
Preliminaries

In this section we recall the relevant results and background material that we will need
in this paper. This section is rather lengthy, but for understanding all but the most technical inputs in the paper (Lemma
3.1
, Section
4.2
, Section
5
, and Section
6.3
), only Section
2.1
is necessary.

2.1
The directed landscape

We start with the characterization of the directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
from
[
DOV23
]
. The characterization is in terms of its two-dimensional marginal
\mathcal{S}(x,y):=\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,1)
known as the Airy sheet. The Airy sheet itself has a more technical definition that we will give in Section
2.2
. In order to define the directed landscape and for the remainder of the paper we say that
\mathcal{S}_{s}
is an
Airy sheet of scale
s
if

\mathcal{S}_{s}(x,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}s\mathcal{S}(xs^{-2},ys^{-2})

jointly over all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 2.1
.

The directed landscape
\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}\to\mathbb{R}
is the unique, in law, random function in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow})
satisfying

1.

(Airy sheet marginals) For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
s>0
we have

\mathcal{\mathcal{L}}(x,t;y,t+s^{3})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{S}_{s}(x,y)

jointly in all
x,y
.

2.

(Independent increments) For any disjoint time intervals
\{(t_{i},s_{i}):i\in\{1,\dots k\}\}
, the random functions

\mathcal{L}(\cdot,t_{i};\cdot,s_{i}),i\in\{1,\dots,k\}

are independent.

3.

(Metric composition law) Almost surely, for any
r<s<t
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
we have that

\mathcal{L}(x,r;y,t)=\max_{z\in\mathbb{R}}[\mathcal{L}(x,r;z,s)+\mathcal{L}(z,s;y,t)].

The metric composition for
\mathcal{L}
implies the reverse triangle inequality (
1
). The directed landscape also satisfies the well-known
quadrangle inequality
, which states that for all
x_{1}<x_{2}
,
y_{1}<y_{2}
, and
s<t
we have

\mathcal{L}(x_{1},s;y_{2},t)+\mathcal{L}(x_{2},s;y_{1},t)\leq\mathcal{L}(x_{1},s;y_{1},t)+\mathcal{L}(x_{2},s;y_{2},t)

(9)

The quadrangle inequality follows from a straightforward path-crossing argument, e.g. see
[
DOV23
, Lemma 9.1]
.
Note that the reverse triangle inequality (
1
) is an equality at all points along a geodesic. If
\pi:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}
is a geodesic, then for any partition
s=r_{0}<r_{1}<\dots<r_{k}=t
we have

\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}(s);\bar{\pi}(t))=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}(r_{i-1});\bar{\pi}(r_{i})).

(10)

The directed landscape has invariance properties which we use throughout the paper.

Lemma 2.2

(Lemma 10.2,
[
DOV23
]
and Proposition 1.23,
[
DV21
]
)
.

We have the following equalities in distribution as random functions in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow})
. Here
r,c\in\mathbb{R}
, and
q>0
.

1.

Time stationarity.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(x,t;y,t+s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(x,t+r;y,t+s+r).

2.

Spatial stationarity.

\mathcal{L}(x,t;y,t+s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(x+c,t;y+c,t+s).

3.

Flip symmetries.

\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(-x,s;-y,t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(y,-t;x,-s).

4.

Skew stationarity.

\mathcal{L}(x,t;y,t+s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(x+ct,t;y+ct+sc,t+s)+s^{-1}[(x-y)^{2}-(x-y-sc)^{2}].

5.

1:2:3
rescaling.

\mathcal{L}(x,t;y,t+s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}q\mathcal{L}(q^{-2}x,q^{-3}t;q^{-2}y,q^{-3}(t+s)).

Remark 2.3
.

The directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
is strong mixing with respect to the time shift in Lemma
2.2
.1 by the independence of time increments, it is strong mixing with respect to the spatial shift in Lemma
2.2
.2 by
[
Dau22
, Proposition 2.6]
, and it is ergodic with respect to the
1:2:3
rescaling in Lemma
2.2
.5 by
[
Dau22
, Proposition 2.5]
.

We will also need a series of regularity results about
\mathcal{L}
. The following proposition states that the directed landscape is Hölder
1/2^{-}
in space and Hölder
1/3^{-}
in time. This proposition is motivation for the definition of the metric
d_{1:2:3}
.

Proposition 2.4

(Proposition 10.5,
[
DOV23
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}(x,t;y,t+s)=\mathcal{L}(x,t;y,t+s)+(x-y)^{2}/s
and let
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
be a compact set. Then for all
u=(x,s;y,t),v=(x^{\prime},s^{\prime};y^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in K
we have

|\mathcal{R}(u)-\mathcal{R}(v)|\leq C\left(\tau^{1/3}\log^{2/3}(\tau^{-1}+1)+\xi^{1/2}\log^{1/2}(\xi^{-1}+1)\right),

where

\xi=||(x,y)-(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})||_{\infty}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\tau=||(s,t)-(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})||_{\infty}.

Here

C
is a random constant depending on
K
with
\mathbb{E}a^{C^{3/2}}<\infty
for some
a>1
.

The next theorem should be thought of as a
landscape shape theorem
: at first order,
\mathcal{L}(x,t;y,t+s)
is the parabola
-\tfrac{(x-y)^{2}}{s}
.

Theorem 2.5

(Corollary 10.7,
[
DOV23
]
)
.

There exists a random constant
C
satisfying

\mathbb{P}(C>m)\leq ce^{-dm^{3/2}}

for universal constants
c,d>0
and all
m>0
, such that for all
u=(x,t;y,t+s)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, we have

\left|\mathcal{L}(x,t;y,t+s)+\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{s}\right|\leq Cs^{1/3}\log^{4/3}\left(\frac{2(\|u\|_{2}+2)^{3/2}}{s}\right)\log^{2/3}(\|u\|_{2}+2).

(11)

We will frequently use the following corollary of Theorem
2.5
that follows the fact the triangle inequality is an equality along geodesics, (
10
).

Corollary 2.6
.

For every
a\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a random compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that if
\pi
is a geodesic from
p\in[-a,a]^{2}
to
q\in[-a,a]^{2}
then
\mathfrak{g}\pi\subset K
. Moreover, all geodesics from
p\in[-a,a]^{2}
to
q\in[-a,a]^{2}
are Hölder-
1/2
continuous with a common (random) Hölder constant
C_{K}
.

Note that geodesics are typically much smoother than Hölder-
1/2
. Indeed, the geodesic between two fixed points is Hölder-
2/3^{-}
almost surely (see Proposition 12.3,
[
DOV23
]
). The reason for Corollary
2.6
is simply to have a crude equicontinuity bound that applies to all geodesics at once.

To prove coalescence and tightness statements about geodesics we will use almost sure statements about the geodesic structure in
\mathcal{L}
.

Lemma 2.7
.

There exists a set
\Omega
of probability one where the following hold for
\mathcal{L}
:

1.

(Theorem 12.1,
[
DOV23
]
). For every rational
(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}\cap\mathbb{Q}^{4}
, there is a unique
\mathcal{L}
-geodesic from
p
to
q
.

2.

The bound (
11
) holds for a finite constant
C
.

3.

(Lemma 13.2,
[
DOV23
]
). For every
u=(p;q)=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, there exist rightmost and leftmost
\mathcal{L}
-geodesics
\gamma^{R}_{u}
and
\gamma^{L}_{u}
from
p
to
q
. That is, there exist geodesics
\gamma^{R}(u),\gamma^{L}(u)
from
p
to
q
such that for any other geodesic
\gamma^{\prime}
from
p
to
q
, we have
\gamma^{L}(u)\leq\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma^{R}(u)
.
For fixed
s<t
, the functions
(x,y)\mapsto\gamma^{L}(x,s;y,t)
and
(x,y)\mapsto\gamma^{R}(x,s;y,t)
are monotone increasing in
x
and
y
.

4.

(Lemma 2.7,
[
BGH22
]
) For any real numbers
s<t,x_{1}<x_{2},y_{1}<y_{2}
, and any geodesics
\gamma_{i}
from
(x_{i},s)
to
(y_{i},t)
,
i=1,2
, we have
\gamma_{1}(r)\leq\gamma_{2}(r)
for all
r\in[s,t]
.

When working with paths and geodesics throughout the paper, we will frequently appeal to the following basic facts, which fall out immediately from the continuity of
\mathcal{L}
and the definition of path length.

F.1

Path length is upper semi-continuous.
More precisely, if
\pi_{n}:[s_{n},t_{n}]\to\mathbb{R}
is a sequence of paths with
s_{n}\to s,t_{n}\to t
for some
s<t
, and
\pi_{n}(r)\to\pi(r)
for all
r\in(s,t)
,
\pi_{n}(s_{n})\to\pi(s),\pi(t_{n})\to\pi(t)
then

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\pi_{n}\|_{\mathcal{L}}\leq\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}}.

Note that the right-hand side can be defined by (
2
) even if
\pi
is not a path (i.e. a
continuous
function). However, if
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}}\neq-\infty
, then
\pi
is forced to be continuous by Theorem
2.5
.

F.2

Limits of geodesics are geodesics.
In the context of point
1
above, if all the
\pi_{n}
are geodesics, then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\pi_{n}\|_{\mathcal{L}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}_{n}(s_{n});\bar{\pi}_{n}(t_{n}))=\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}(s);\bar{\pi}(t)).

Therefore by upper semi-continuity of path length,
\pi
is also a geodesic.

F.3

Concatenations add path length.
For paths
\pi:[s,r]\to\mathbb{R},\tau:[r,t]\to\mathbb{R}
with
\pi(r)=\tau(r)
, let
\pi\oplus\tau:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}
be equal to
\pi
on
[s,r]
and
\tau
on
[s,t]
. Then

\|\pi\oplus\tau\|_{\mathcal{L}}=\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}}+\|\tau\|_{\mathcal{L}}.

F.4

Switching preserves geodesics.
If
\pi:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R},\tau:[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]\to\mathbb{R}
are geodesics with
\pi(r)=\tau(r),\pi(r^{\prime})=\tau(r^{\prime})
for some
r<r^{\prime}\in[s,t]\cap[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]
, then
\pi|_{[s,r]}\oplus\tau|_{[r,r^{\prime}]}\oplus\pi|_{[r^{\prime},t]}
is a geodesic.

2.2
The Airy line ensemble

The Airy sheet is built from another important object, the parabolic Airy line ensemble.
The
parabolic Airy line ensemble
is random continuous function
\mathfrak{A}:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, where
(i,x)\mapsto\mathfrak{A}_{i}(x)
. It is ordered so that almost surely

for all
i,x
we have
\mathfrak{A}_{i}(x)>\mathfrak{A}_{i+1}(x)
.

(12)

The parabolic Airy line ensemble is defined uniquely via a determinantal formula that we will not require in this paper. The term parabolic comes from the fact that the Airy line ensemble
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
given by
\mathcal{A}_{i}(x)=\mathfrak{A}_{i}(x)+x^{2}
is stationary in
x
. We also have the flip symmetry
\mathfrak{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}
, where
\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}_{i}(x):=\mathfrak{A}_{i}(-x)
. We will explain shortly how the Airy sheet arises from the Airy line ensemble, but for now, the uninitiated reader can motivate their interest in
\mathfrak{A}
by noting that
\mathfrak{A}_{1}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{S}(0,x)=\mathcal{L}(0,0;x,1)
, where the equality in distribution is as functions from
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
.

The ensemble
\mathfrak{A}
arises as the scaling limit at the edge of a sequence of
n
non-intersecting Brownian motions as
n\to\infty
.
Because of this, it is best thought of as a system of countably many non-intersecting Brownian motions. This notion is made rigorous by a probabilistic resampling property called the
Brownian Gibbs property
, which we now introduce.

Lemma 2.8

(The Brownian Gibbs property,
[
CH14
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{k,a,b}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\mathfrak{A}
outside of the set
\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times[a,b]
. Then conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{k,a,b}
, the law of
\mathfrak{A}|_{\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times[a,b]}
is given by a
k
-tuple of Brownian bridges
B=(B_{1},\dots,B_{k})
with
B_{i}(a)=\mathfrak{A}_{i}(a),B_{i}(b)=\mathfrak{A}_{i}(b)
for all
i
, drawn independently of each other and of
\mathfrak{A}|_{(\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times[a,b])^{c}}
and conditioned on the event

B_{1}(x)>B_{2}(x)>\dots>B_{k}(x)>\mathfrak{A}_{k+1}(x)

for all
x\in[a,b]
. This event has positive probability by (
12
).

The Brownian Gibbs property implies that the Airy lines
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
are locally absolutely continuous with respect to Brownian motions. However, it does not say much about exactly how this absolute continuity occurs because of the difficulty in resampling non-intersecting Brownian bridges that avoid a lower boundary. Quantitative versions of absolute continuity were first investigated in
[
Ham22
]
and further studied in
[
CHH19
,
Dau23b
]
.
For our purposes, we require a result from
[
Dau23b
]
that relates the Airy line ensemble to a collection of linearly shifted Brownian bridges.

Theorem 2.9

(part of Theorem 1.8,
[
Dau23b
]
)
.

Fix
t\geq 1,k\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
S=\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times[0,t]
. There is a random continuous function
\mathfrak{L}=\mathfrak{L}^{t,k}:S\to\mathbb{R}
such that:

1.

For any Borel set
A\subset\mathcal{C}(S)
we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{A}|_{S}\in A)\leq e^{c_{k}t^{3}}\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{L}\in A).

2.

We have
\mathfrak{L}=L+B
, where
L=(L_{1},\dots,L_{k})
is a
k
-tuple of affine functions, and
B
is a
k
-tuple of independent Brownian bridges with
B_{i}(0)=B_{i}(t)=0
. Crucially,
B
and
L
are independent.

3.

(Lemma 4.2 and Corollary 4.4,
[
Dau23b
]
) There exists
d_{k,t}>0
such that for all
m>0,r\in\{0,t\},i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
:

\mathbb{P}(|L_{i}(r)|>m)\leq 2e^{-d_{k,t}m^{3/2}}.

Note that Theorem
2.9
is stated on the interval
[0,t]
, but by the shift invariance of
\mathcal{A}(t)=\mathfrak{A}(t)+t^{2}
, also holds on any interval
[a,a+t]
. The difference
\mathfrak{L}_{i}(0)-\mathfrak{L}_{i}(t)
also satisfies strong bounds ensuring that locally, each line
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
is not just absolutely continuous with respect to Brownian motion, but has a uniformly bounded Radon-Nikodym derivative. We will just need this for the line
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
.

Proposition 2.10

(Corollary 1.3,
[
Dau23b
]
)
.

Let
B:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a two sided Brownian motion. Then there exists a constant
c>0
such that for any
t\geq 1
and any Borel set
A\subset\mathcal{C}([-t,t])
we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{A}_{1}|_{[-t,t]}-\mathfrak{A}_{1}(0)\in A)\leq e^{ct^{3}}\mathbb{P}(B\in A).

2.3
The Airy sheet and an isometry

We can define the Airy sheet using last passage across the Airy line ensemble, as follows.
First, consider a continuous function
f\in\mathcal{C}(I\times\mathbb{R})
where
I
is an integer interval, and a non-increasing cadlag function
\pi:[x,y]\to\llbracket m,n\rrbracket\subset I
, henceforth a
path
from
(x,n)
to
(y,m)
. Define the length of
\pi
with respect to the environment
f
by

\|\pi\|_{f}=\sum_{i=m}^{n}f_{i}(\pi_{i})-f_{i}(\pi_{i+1}).

(13)

Here
\pi_{i}=\inf\{t\in[x,y]:\pi(t)<i\}
is the time when
\pi_{i}
jumps off of line
i
, and if this set is empty we set
\pi_{i}=y
. We write

f[(x,n)\to(y,m)]=\sup_{\pi}\|\pi\|_{f},

where the supremum is over all paths from
(x,n)
to
(y,m)
. Last passage percolation is best thought of as a planar directed metric and certain last passage models have a beautiful solvable structure. Indeed, last passage percolation across Brownian motions was the first model shown to converge to the directed landscape, see
[
DOV23
]
. We can now define the Airy sheet.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a parabolic Airy line ensemble, and let
\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}(x)=\mathfrak{A}(-x)
. For
(x,y,z)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
define

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(x,y,z)&=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathfrak{A}[(-\sqrt{k/(2x)},k)\to(y,1)]-\mathfrak{A}[(-\sqrt{k/(2x)},k)\to(z,1)],\\
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(-x,y,z)&=\lim_{k\to\infty}\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}[(-\sqrt{k/(2x)},k)\to(-y,1)]-\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}[(-\sqrt{k/(2x)},k)\to(-z,1)]\end{split}

(14)

and let
\mathcal{S}(0,y)=\mathfrak{A}_{1}(y)
.
For
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Q}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathbb{Q}
, define

\mathcal{S}(x,y)=\mathbb{E}\mathcal{A}_{1}(0)+\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{z=-n}^{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(x,y,z)-(z-x)^{2},

(15)

The
Airy sheet
defined from
\mathfrak{A}
is the unique continuous extension of
\mathcal{S}
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Figure 6
:
An illustration of
\mathcal{S}(x,y,z)
in the definition of the Airy sheet. This quantity is a limit of differences of last passage values from
(-\sqrt{k/(2x)},k)
in
\mathfrak{A}
. As we take
k
to infinity, the portions of the last passage paths illustrated in red and blue above stabilize, and only the tail (illustrated in purple) changes for large
k
. This implies that the limit in (
14
) exists and allows us to define
\mathcal{S}(x,y)-\mathcal{S}(x,z):=\mathcal{S}(x,y,z)
. Equation (
15
) extracts single Airy sheet values from these differences by ergodicity. Note that if we increase
x
, the tail will move from left to right.

See Figure
6
for an illustration of Definition
2.11
.
It is not at all clear that the limits in Definition
2.11
exist, or that the resulting function has a continuous extension to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. However, a.s. these properties do hold and so the construction above is well-defined.
This is shown for
x>\mathbb{Q}^{+}
in
[
DOV23
]
, see also
[
DV21
, Section 1.11]
for the extension to all
x\in\mathbb{Q}\setminus\{0\}
. In this coupling, a.s. we have
\mathcal{S}(0,x)=\mathfrak{A}_{1}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and for any
x>0
,
y,z\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(x,y,z)=\mathcal{S}(x,y)-\mathcal{S}(x,z),\qquad\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(-x,y,z)=\mathcal{S}(-x,y)-\mathcal{S}(-x,z),

(16)

see Definition 8.1 and Remark 8.1 in
[
DOV23
]
.
While Definition
2.11
is somewhat involved, one should think of it as capturing the idea that

``\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,1)=\mathcal{S}(x,y)=\mathfrak{A}[(x,\infty)\to(y,1)]".

(17)

In other words, there is a
partial isometry
between
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathfrak{A}
. The more complex Definition
2.11
is required to rigorously make sense of the right-hand side of (
17
). The exact expression
(-\sqrt{k/(2x)},k)
for the asymptotic direction representing
(x,\infty)
is not particularly intuitive and follows from calculations. See
[
DOV23
]
,
[
DV21
]
for more background.

We can also use Definition
2.11
to define the notion of paths and geodesics from
(x,\infty)
to
(y,1)
across
\mathfrak{A}
, and lengths in
\mathfrak{A}
of these paths.
For
x\in[0,\infty),y\in\mathbb{R},
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, a nonincreasing cadlag function
\pi:(-\infty,y]\to\{n,n+1,\dots\}
is a
parabolic path
from
(x,\infty)
to
(y,n)
if

\lim_{z\to-\infty}\frac{\pi(z)}{2z^{2}}=-x.

(18)

This definition guarantees that for
x>0
, if
z_{k}
is the largest time when
\pi(z)\leq k
, then
z_{k}=-\sqrt{k/(2x+o(1))}
, matching the asymptotic direction from (
14
).

For a parabolic Airy line ensemble
\mathfrak{A}
with corresponding Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}
as in Definition
2.11
, and any parabolic path starting at
x\geq 0
, define its
path length
by

\|\pi\|_{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathcal{S}(x,y)+\lim_{z\to-\infty}\left(\|\pi|_{[z,y]}\|_{\mathfrak{A}}-\mathfrak{A}[(z,\pi(z))\to(y,1)]\right).

(19)

Note that we use
(y,1)
on the right hand side even if the parabolic path ends at a line
n\neq 1
.
The limit above always exists and is nonpositive, see
[
DZ21
, Lemma 4.1]
. Moreover, by (
16
), the formula (
19
) also holds if
\mathcal{S}(x,y)
is replaced by
\mathcal{S}(x,w)
and
\mathfrak{A}[(z,\pi(z))\to(y,1)]
is replaced by
\mathfrak{A}[(z,\pi(z))\to(w,1)]
for any
w\in\mathbb{R}
.

The idea of definition (
19
) is that the best possible path length for
\pi
should be the Airy sheet value
\mathcal{S}(x,y)
. This accounts for the first term in (
19
). For fixed
z
, the second term in (
19
) measures the discrepancy between the length of
\pi
and the optimal length on the compact interval
[z,y]
; taking
z\to-\infty
then gives the discrepancy between
\|\pi\|_{\mathfrak{A}}
and
\mathcal{S}(x,y)
. Now, for
(y,n)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{N}
we define the
parabolic last passage value

\mathfrak{A}[(x,\infty)\to(y,n)]=\sup_{\pi}\|\pi\|_{\mathfrak{A}},

(20)

where the supremum is over all parabolic paths from
(x,\infty)
to
(y,n)
. We call a path a geodesic if it achieves this supremum. The next lemma gives a few basics about paths and geodesics in
\mathfrak{A}
.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{S}
be coupled as in Definition
2.11
. Then we have the following.

(i)

(Lemma 4.1(ii),
[
DZ21
]
)
\mathcal{S}(x,y)=\mathfrak{A}[(x,\infty)\to(y,1)]
for all
x\geq 0,y\in\mathbb{R}
. This makes sense of the isometry (
17
).

(ii)

(Lemma 4.1(i, ii, v),
[
DZ21
]
) Almost surely, for every
(x,y)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, there exists a geodesic
\pi
in
\mathfrak{A}
from
(x,\infty)
to
(y,1)
. Moreover, almost surely for every
(x,y)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, there is a geodesic
\pi_{R}\{x,y\}
from
x
to
y
satisfying
\pi(t)\leq\pi_{R}\{x,y\}(t)
for any geodesic
\pi
from
x
to
y
and all
t\in(-\infty,y]
. We call
\pi_{R}\{x,y\}
the
rightmost geodesic
from
(x,\infty)
to
(y,1)
. We have the monotonicity
\pi_{R}\{x,y\}(t)\leq\pi_{R}\{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\}(t)
whenever
x\leq x^{\prime},y\leq y^{\prime}
and
t\leq y
.

(iii)

(Lemma 3.9,
[
Dau23a
]
) For any
x\in[0,\infty),y<z\in\mathbb{R}
and any geodesic
\pi
from
(x,\infty)
to
(z,1)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}[(x,\infty)\to(z,1)]

\displaystyle=\max_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{A}[(x,\infty)\to(y,i)]+\mathfrak{A}[(y,i)\to(z,1)]

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{A}[(x,\infty)\to(y,\pi(y))]+\mathfrak{A}[(y,\pi(y))\to(z,1)].

Here the last passage values starting at
(x,\infty)
are parabolic last passage values, and the others are standard last passage values.

2.4
The extended landscape

We extend the definition of
\mathcal{L}
to measure optimal lengths of disjoint paths. Here and throughout the paper, we say that two paths
\pi,\tau:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}
are
disjoint
if
\pi(r)\neq\tau(r)
for all
r\in(s,t)
.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
be the space of all points
(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)
, where
s<t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
lie in the same space
\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:x_{1}\leq\dots\leq x_{k}\}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. For
\mathbf{u}=(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)\in\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
, define

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{u})=\sup_{\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\pi_{i}\|_{\mathcal{L}}.

(21)

Here and throughout we use the convention that
k
is such that
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
. The supremum is over all
k
-tuples of paths
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k})
where each
\pi_{i}
is a path from
(x_{i},s)
to
(y_{i},t)
, and the paths
\pi_{i}
are disjoint. We call such a collection
\pi
a
disjoint
k
-tuple
from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
. We call the extension of
\mathcal{L}
from
\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
the
extended landscape
.

Geometrically, the space
\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}=\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathfrak{X}^{k}_{\uparrow}
where

\mathfrak{X}^{k}_{\uparrow}=\{(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t):s<t,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}\}

inherits the Euclidean metric and topology.
With this setup, the extended landscape is continuous on
\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
by
[
DZ21
, Theorem 1.4/1.6]
. In fact, as with the standard landscape we have a modulus of continuity bound, see the comment immediately after
[
DZ21
, Lemma 6.5]
.

Lemma 2.14
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N},\epsilon>0
and a compact subset
K\subset\mathfrak{X}^{k}_{\uparrow}
. Then there exists a random
C>0
such that for all
\mathbf{u}=(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t),\mathbf{u}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},s^{\prime};\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in K
we have

|\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{u})-\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})|\leq C\left(\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\|^{1/2-\epsilon}_{2}+\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\|^{1/2-\epsilon}_{2}+|s-s^{\prime}|^{1/3-\epsilon}+|t-t^{\prime}|^{1/3-\epsilon}\right).

Moreover, by
[
DZ21
, Theorem 1.7]
, almost surely the supremum in (
21
) is attained by a disjoint
k
-tuple for all
\mathbf{u}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
. We call a disjoint
k
-tuple that attains this supremum a
disjoint optimizer
.
Defining
\mathfrak{X}=\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, we call the function
\mathcal{S}:\mathfrak{X}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y},1)

the
extended Airy sheet
.

We can give a similar definition for last passage across the Airy line ensemble. We say parabolic paths
\pi_{i}:(-\infty,y_{i}]\to\mathbb{N},i=1,2
with
y_{1}<y_{2}
are disjoint if
\pi_{1}(z)<\pi_{2}(z)
for
z<y_{1}
.
For
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathfrak{X}
with
x_{1}\geq 0
, let

\mathfrak{A}[(\mathbf{x},\infty)\to(\mathbf{y},1)]=\sup_{\pi}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\pi\|_{\mathfrak{A}},

where the supremum is over all
k
-tuple of disjoint parabolic paths
\pi_{i}
from
(x_{i},\infty)
. Again, this supremum is always attained, see
[
DZ21
, Proposition 5.8]
.

Remarkably, the isometry in Lemma
2.12
(i) extends to
\mathfrak{X}
, and the parabolic Airy line ensemble can actually be defined from the extended landscape. For this definition and throughout the paper, we write
x^{k}=(x,\dots,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Theorem 2.15
.

Let
\mathcal{S},\mathcal{L}
be as in Definition
2.13
, and define
\mathfrak{A}:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
so that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathfrak{A}_{i}(x)=\mathcal{S}(0^{k},x^{k})
. Then:

1.

(Equation (9),
[
DZ21
]
)
\mathfrak{A}
is a parabolic Airy line ensemble.

2.

(Theorem 1.3,
[
DZ21
]
)
\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{S}
are coupled as in Definition
2.11
. Moreover, almost surely,
\mathfrak{A}[(\mathbf{x},\infty)\to(\mathbf{y},1)]=\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
for all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathfrak{X}
with
x_{1}\geq 0
.

Hopefully the relevance of the extended landscape to the study of geodesic networks is clear. Indeed, interesting geodesic networks arise when certain points are connected by disjoint geodesics in
\mathcal{L}
, which is equivalent to an extended landscape value equalling a sum of usual landscape values.

We end this section by recording a simpler form for last passage across
\mathfrak{A}
starting at
(0^{k},\infty)
. For this definition, for
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
we define the disjoint last passage value

\mathfrak{A}[(z,\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket)\to(\mathbf{y},1)]

to be the maximum weight
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\pi_{i}\|_{\mathfrak{A}}
of a
k
-tuple of paths
\pi_{i}
from
(z,i)
to
(y_{i},1)
that satisfy
\pi_{i}>\pi_{i+1}
on
(z,y_{i})
.

Proposition 2.16

(Proposition 5.9,
[
DZ21
]
)
.

Almost surely the following holds. For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
there is a disjoint optimizer
\pi
in
\mathfrak{A}
from
(0^{k},\infty)
to
(\mathbf{y},1)
that only uses the top
k
lines. In particular, for any
z<y_{1}
we have

\mathfrak{A}[(0^{k},\infty)\to(\mathbf{y},1)]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathfrak{A}_{i}(z)+\mathfrak{A}[(z,\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket)\to(\mathbf{y},1)].

(22)

3
Coalescent planar geometry

As discussed in Section
1.1
, geodesics in the directed landscape typically coalesce. The main goal of this section is to establish results related to this coalescence. Along the way we will show that the network graph
G(p;q)
of any pair
(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
satisfies point 1-3 of Theorem
1.1
; this follows from Proposition
3.4
(ii) together with Lemma
3.3
.3.
We start the following lemma, which bounds the number locations geodesics can have at a fixed time.

Lemma 3.1
.

The following claims hold almost surely for
\mathcal{L}
.

(i)

Fix rational times
s<r<t
and any interval
[a,b]
. There exists a random finite set of points
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{N}\}\subset\mathbb{R}
such that if
\pi:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}
is any geodesic with
\pi(s),\pi(t)\in[a,b]
, then

\pi(r)\in X
.

(ii)

For every point
u=(p;q)=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
and
r\in(s,t)
there is a random set of points
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{11}\}\subset\mathbb{R}
such that if
\pi
is a geodesic from
p
to
q
then

\pi(r)\in X
.

The proof of Lemma
3.1
requires analysis of the Airy line ensemble.

Proof of Lemma
3.1
(i).

By rescaling (Lemma
2.2
), it is enough to prove the lemma when
s=0,r=1,
and
t\geq 2
. Define
\mathcal{S}_{1}(x,y)=\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,1)
,
\mathcal{S}_{2}(z,y)=\mathcal{L}(y,1,z,2)
. These are both Airy sheets by the symmetries in Lemma
2.2
. Let

F_{x,z}(y)=\mathcal{S}_{1}(x,y)+\mathcal{S}_{2}(z,y).

By Corollary
2.6
, any geodesic
\pi:[0,t]\to\mathbb{R}
with
\pi(s),\pi(t)\in[a,b]
satisfies
\pi(0),\pi(1),\pi(2)\in[-C,C]
for some random constant
C<\infty
. Therefore since
\pi(1)
is a maximizer of the function
F_{\pi(0),\pi(2)}
on the interval
[-C,C]
, it suffices to show that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, almost surely there are at most finitely many
y\in[-n,n]
that maximize
F_{x,z}
for some
x,z\in[-n,n]
. By shift invariance of
\mathcal{S}
(Lemma
2.2
.2) rather than working on the interval
[-n,n]
we can work on the interval
[0,2n]
.

Let
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
be Airy line ensembles coupled to
\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{2}
as in Definition
2.11
.
By Lemma
2.12
(i, iii), for
x,z\in[0,2n],y\in\mathbb{R}
we can write

\displaystyle F_{x,z}(y)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{A}[(x,\infty)\to(y,1)]+\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}[(z,\infty)\to(y,1)]

\displaystyle=\max_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{A}[(x,\infty)\to(-1,i)]+\mathfrak{A}[(-1,i)\to(y,1)]

(23)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}[(z,\infty)\to(-1,j)]+\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}[(-1,j)\to(y,1)],

(24)

Moreover, if we let
\pi_{R},\pi_{R}^{\prime}
be the rightmost geodesics in
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
from
(2n,\infty)
to
(2n,1)
, then by the monotonicity in Lemma
2.12
(ii) and the second equality in Lemma
2.12
(iii),
letting
M=\max(\pi_{R}(-1),\pi^{\prime}_{R}(-1)),
we can take the maximum in (
23
) over
i,j\in\llbracket 1,M\rrbracket
. Therefore if
y\in[0,2n]
maximizes
F_{x,z}
for some
x,z\in[0,2n]
, then
y
must maximize the function

H_{i,j}[\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}](y):=g_{i}[\mathfrak{A}](y)+g_{j}[\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}](y),\qquad\text{where}\quad g_{\ell}[A](y)=A[(-1,\ell)\to(y,1)].

for some
i,j\in\llbracket 1,M\rrbracket
where
H_{i,j}[\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}]:[-1,2n+1]\to\mathbb{R}
. To finish the proof, it is enough to show that almost surely
H_{i,j}[\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}]
has at most one maximizer in
[0,2n]
for all fixed
i,j
. Now,

\mathfrak{A}|_{\llbracket 1,i\rrbracket\times[-1,2n+1]}-\mathfrak{A}(0),\qquad\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}|_{\llbracket 1,j\rrbracket\times[-1,2n+1]}-\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}(0)

are jointly absolutely continuous with respect to the law of
i+j
independent Brownian motions
B=(B_{1},\dots,B_{i}),B^{\prime}=(B_{1}^{\prime},\dots,B_{j}^{\prime})
by Lemma
2.8
. Therefore it suffices to show that
H_{i,j}[B,B^{\prime}]
has at most one maximizer in
[0,2n]
. For this, we note that the law of
g_{i}[B]
restricted to
[0,2n]
is absolutely continuous with respect to the law of a Brownian motion
W
with
W(-1)=0
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
. This is well-known: for example, it follows from the fact that the map
g_{i}[B]
is the top line
A_{1}
in an ensemble of
i
nonintersecting Brownian motions
A_{1}>\dots>A_{i}
started from the initial condition
A(-1)=0
, see
[
O’C03
]
. Therefore
g_{i}[B]+g_{j}[B^{\prime}]
has a unique maximizer on
[0,2n]
almost surely, as desired.
∎

To prove Lemma
3.1
.(ii), we first need a lemma about Brownian motion.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
I
be a real interval, let
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
, and for
\epsilon,m>0
let
E_{m,\epsilon}(f)
be the maximum size of the largest set of points
\{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\}\subset I
with
|z_{i}-z_{j}|>m
for all
i\neq j
and

f(z_{i})\geq\max_{z\in I}f(z)-\epsilon

for all
i
. Then if
B:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is a Brownian motion we have

\mathbb{P}(E_{m,\epsilon}(B)\geq k)\leq c_{m,k}\epsilon^{k-1}.

Proof.

For
i\in\{-2m,\dots,2m-1\}
, let
M_{i}=\max_{x\in[i/(2m),(i+1)/2m]}B(x)
. Let
\mathcal{I}
be the set of
k
-tuples of indices
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})\in\{-2m,\dots,2m-1\}^{k}
such that
|i_{j}-i_{j^{\prime}}|\geq 2
for all
j\neq j^{\prime}
. If
E_{m,\epsilon}(B)\geq k
then there exists
\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{I}
such that
|M_{i_{j}}-M_{i_{j^{\prime}}}|\leq\epsilon
for all
j\neq j
.

To complete the proof it just remains to take a union bound over
\mathcal{I}
and observe that for any
\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{I}
the vector
(M_{i_{1}},\dots,M_{i_{k}})
has a Lebesgue density on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
bounded above by a constant depending only on
m
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.1
(ii).

Setting some notation, for
u=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
and
r\in(s,t)
define

Y_{u,r}(z)=\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,r)+\mathcal{L}(z,r;y,t),

and let
T(u,r)=\argmax Y_{u,r}(z)
. The set
T(u,r)
is the set of all spatial locations along geodesics from
(x,s)
to
(y,t)
at time
r
. Our aim is to show that
\#T(u,r)\leq 11
for all
u,r
. First, observe that by continuity of geodesics in
\mathcal{L}
, if
\#T(u,r^{\prime})\geq 12
for some point
(u,r^{\prime})
, then
\#T(u,r)\geq 12
for all
r
in an open interval containing
r^{\prime}
; in particular, this holds at some rational
r
.

Next, fix
r
rational and
\gamma>0
and let
K_{\gamma}
be the set of all
u=(x,s;y,t;r)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}\times\mathbb{R}
where
|x|,|s|,|y|,|t|\leq\gamma^{-1}
and
s+\gamma<r<t-\gamma
. By Theorem
2.5
, for every
\gamma>0
there is a random
D>0
such that for all
(u,r)\in K_{\gamma}
, we have
T(u,r)=T_{D}(u,r)
where
T_{d}(u,r):=T(u,r)\cap[-d,d]
.
Therefore it is enough to show that for every fixed
\gamma>0
and
d>0
, almost surely
T_{d}(u,r)\leq 11
for all
(u,r)\in K_{\gamma}
.
For every
\epsilon>0
, let

K_{\gamma,\epsilon}=K_{\gamma}\cap[\mathbb{Z}/(\epsilon^{2}\log\epsilon)]\times[\mathbb{Z}/(\epsilon^{3}\log\epsilon)]\times[\mathbb{Z}/(\epsilon^{2}\log\epsilon)]\times[\mathbb{Z}/(\epsilon^{3}\log\epsilon)].

By the modulus of continuity for
\mathcal{L}
(Proposition
2.4
), if
T_{d}(u,r)\geq 12
for some
u\in K_{\gamma}
then using the notation of Lemma
3.2
, there exists an
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all small enough
\epsilon>0
we have

\max_{u\in K_{\gamma,\epsilon}}E_{1/m,\epsilon}(Y_{u,r}|_{[-d,d]})\geq 12.

(25)

Therefore it is enough to show that the probability of (
25
) goes to
0
with
\epsilon
for every fixed
\gamma,d,m
. We do this with a union bound over
K_{\gamma,\epsilon}
, using that
\#K_{\gamma,\epsilon}\leq c_{\gamma}\epsilon^{-10}\log^{8}(\epsilon^{-1})
. By Proposition
2.10
and Lemma
3.2
, letting
B,B^{\prime}
denote two independent two-sided Brownian motions defined on
[-d,d]
, for all
(u,r)\in K_{\gamma}
we have

\mathbb{P}(E_{1/m,\epsilon}(Y_{u,r}|_{[-d,d]})\geq 12)\leq c_{d,\gamma}\mathbb{P}(E_{1/m,\epsilon}(B+B^{\prime})\geq 12)\leq c_{m,d,\gamma}\epsilon^{11}.

The fact that the first bound is uniform over all choices of
(u,r)\in K_{\gamma,\epsilon}
follows from the symmetries in Lemma
2.2
and the fact that we are working on a compact set. Taking a union bound yields the result.
∎

3.1
Coalescence via Lemma
3.1

Given Lemma
3.1
, all coalescence results in this section are based on essentially topological arguments. Throughout this section we work on the almost sure event
\Omega
defined in Lemma
2.7
, and on the almost sure set where Lemma
3.1
holds. All results will be deterministic while on this set.

To state coalescence results in this section, define the
overlap

O(\pi,\tau)
of two geodesics
\pi:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R},\tau:[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]\to\mathbb{R}
as the closure of the set

\{r\in(s,t)\cap(s^{\prime},t^{\prime}):\pi(r)=\tau(r)\}.

Note that
O(\pi,\tau)
can contain endpoints of the open interval
(s,t)\cap(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})
if
\pi
and
\tau
overlap on a dense set near these endpoints.
Our first result on coalescence in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
(p;q)=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, and let
\gamma
be any geodesic from
p
to
q
. Then:

1.

There exists a sequence of rational points
(p_{n},q_{n})\in(x_{n},s_{n};y_{n},t_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
with
p_{n}\to p
and
q_{n}\to q
such that letting
\gamma_{n}
be the unique geodesic from
p_{n}
to
q_{n}
, then
O(\gamma_{n},\gamma)
is a closed interval whose endpoints converge to
s
and
t
, respectively.

2.

For any other geodesic
\pi
, the overlap
O(\gamma,\pi)
is a (possibly empty) closed interval.

3.

For any
[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]\subset(s,t)
,
\gamma|_{[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]}
is the unique geodesic from
\bar{\gamma}(s^{\prime})
to
\bar{\gamma}(t^{\prime})
.

Shortly before this paper was first posted, Bhatia
[
Bha23
, Theorem 1]
developed a different proof of this result. When
\gamma
is a rightmost geodesic, Lemma
3.3
.1 also follows from the proof of
[
DSV22
, Corollary 3.5]
.

Proof.

First, it is enough to show that there is a set of pairs
S:=\{(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Q}^{2}\cap(s,t):s^{\prime}<t^{\prime}\}
such that
(s,t)
is in the closure of
S
and such that the three points of the lemma hold when
\gamma
is replaced by
\gamma|_{[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]}
for
(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in S
. This is clear for part
3
. The rational approximation in part
1
follows by a diagonalization argument from the existence of rational approximations for all
\gamma|_{[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]}
, and finally if
O(\gamma,\pi)
is not a closed interval, then
O(\gamma|_{[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]},\pi)
will not be a closed interval for some
(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})
close to
(s,t)
.

To define the set
S
, first define
f:[s,t]\to\mathbb{N}
by

f(r)=\#\{\pi(r):\pi\text{ is a geodesic from $p$ to $q$}\}.

By Lemma
3.1
(ii),
f(r)\leq 11
for all
r\in(s,t)
.
The boundedness of
f
implies that
f
has a dense set of local maxima. Noting also that
f
is lower semi-continuous,
f
must be constant in a region around each of its local maxima, so
f
has a dense set of rational local maxima
M\subset\mathbb{Q}
. Define

S:=\{(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in M^{2}\cap(s,t):s^{\prime}<t^{\prime}\},

and for every
s\in M
, let
[a_{s},b_{s}]
be a closed interval containing
s
in its interior on which
f
is constant.

Proof of 1 for
\gamma|_{[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]},(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in S
.
Since
f
is constant on
[s^{\prime},b_{s^{\prime}}],[a_{t^{\prime}},t^{\prime}]
, all geodesics from
\bar{\gamma}(s^{\prime})
to
\bar{\gamma}(t^{\prime})
must coincide on
[s^{\prime},b_{s^{\prime}}],[a_{t^{\prime}},t^{\prime}]
. In particular, the geodesic
\gamma
coincides with the rightmost geodesic
\pi
from
\bar{\gamma}(s^{\prime})
to
\bar{\gamma}(t^{\prime})
on
[s^{\prime},b_{s^{\prime}}],[a_{t^{\prime}},t^{\prime}]
.

Let
x^{\prime}=\gamma(s^{\prime}),y^{\prime}=\gamma(t^{\prime})
, let
x_{n},y_{n}
be rational sequences with
x_{n}\downarrow x^{\prime},y_{n}\downarrow y^{\prime}
, and let
\gamma_{n}
be the geodesics from
(x_{n},s^{\prime})
to
(y_{n},t^{\prime})
. By the monotone ordering on rightmost geodesics (Lemma
2.7
.3) and the fact that pointwise limits of geodesics are geodesics (Fact
F.2
),
\gamma_{n}\downarrow\pi^{\prime}
pointwise, where
\pi^{\prime}
is a geodesic from
\bar{\gamma}(s^{\prime})
to
\bar{\gamma}(t^{\prime})
. Since
\gamma_{n}(r)\geq\pi(r)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N},r\in[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]
and
\pi
is the rightmost geodesic we have
\pi^{\prime}=\pi
.

Therefore for any rational
\epsilon>0
, either there are infinitely many values in the set
\{\gamma_{n}(s^{\prime}+\epsilon),\gamma_{n}(t^{\prime}-\epsilon)\}
or else
\gamma_{n}(s^{\prime}+\epsilon)=\pi(s^{\prime}+\epsilon),\gamma_{n}(t^{\prime}+\epsilon)=\pi(t^{\prime}+\epsilon)
for all small enough
\epsilon
. The former possibility cannot occur by Lemma
3.1
(i). The latter implies that
\{s^{\prime}+\epsilon,t^{\prime}-\epsilon\}\subset O(\gamma_{n},\pi)
for large enough
n
. Since
[s^{\prime},b_{s^{\prime}}]\cup[a_{t^{\prime}},t^{\prime}]\subset O(\gamma|_{[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]},\pi)
we have
\{s^{\prime}+\epsilon,t^{\prime}-\epsilon\}\subset O(\gamma_{n},\gamma|_{[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]})
for small enough
\epsilon>0
and large enough
n
. Finally,
O(\gamma_{n},\gamma)
is connected since otherwise we could contradict the uniqueness of the rational geodesics
\gamma_{n}
by appealing to geodesic switching (Fact
F.4
). Therefore
O(\gamma_{n},\gamma|_{[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]})
is an interval converging to
[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]
.

Proof of 2, 3 for
\gamma|_{[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]},(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in S
.
We use statement
1
for
\gamma|_{[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]}
with approximating sequence
\gamma_{n}
. Since
\gamma_{n}
is the unique geodesic from
p_{n}
to
q_{n}
, the set
O(\gamma_{n},\pi)
is connected for any geodesic
\pi
by appealing to geodesic switching.
Therefore statement
2
follows from
1
, since if
O(\gamma,\pi)
is disconnected, then

O(\gamma_{n},\pi)

must be disconnected for large enough
n
. Statement
3
also follows from
1
since otherwise we could contradict uniqueness of rational geodesics by again using a geodesic switching argument.
∎

We can use Lemma
3.3
to upgrade Lemma
3.1
. To state the upgrade, let
K_{1}=[x_{1},y_{1}]\times[s_{1},t_{1}],K_{2}=[x_{2},y_{2}]\times[s_{2},t_{2}]
with
t_{1}<s_{2}
be two compact boxes, let
I=[r,r^{\prime}]\subset[t_{1},s_{2}]
, define

H(K_{1},K_{2},I)=\#\{\pi|_{I}:\pi\text{ is a geodesic from some $p\in K_{1}$ to $q\in K_{2}$}\}.

In words,
H(K_{1},K_{2},I)
counts the number of paths defined on the interval
I
that are segments of geodesics from
K_{1}
to
K_{2}
.

Proposition 3.4
.

The following claims hold almost surely for
\mathcal{L}
.

(i)

For any compact sets
K_{1}=[x_{1},y_{1}]\times[s_{1},t_{1}],K_{2}=[x_{2},y_{2}]\times[s_{2},t_{2}]
with
t_{1}<s_{2}
and
I=[r,r^{\prime}]\subset(t_{1},s_{2})
, we have
H(K_{1},K_{2},I)<\infty
. Note that this no longer holds if
I
contains either
t_{1}
or
s_{2}
.

(ii)

For every point
(p;q)=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, we have
H(p,q,[s,t])\leq 121
. That is, there are at most
121
geodesics between any pair of points in
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
.

Proof.

By Corollary
2.6
, there exists a random compact interval
[-A,A]
and rational times
r_{1}\in(t_{1},r),r_{2}\in(r^{\prime},s_{2})
such that any geodesic from
K_{1}
to
K_{2}
lives in the interval
[-A,A]
at times
r_{1},r_{2}
. Therefore by Lemma
3.1
(i), we have

H(K_{1},K_{2},\{r_{1}\})<\infty,\qquad H(K_{1},K_{2},\{r_{2}\})<\infty.

(26)

Next, by Lemma
3.3
.3, if
p
is an interior geodesic point at time
r_{1}
and
q
is an interior geodesic point at time
r_{2}
, then there is a unique geodesic from
p
to
q
. Therefore

H(K_{1},K_{2},I)\leq H(K_{1},K_{2},[r_{1},r_{2}])\leq H(K_{1},K_{2},\{r_{1}\})H(K_{1},K_{2},\{r_{2}\}),

(27)

which is finite by (
26
).

For part (ii), using (
27
) with
K_{1}=\{p\},K_{2}=\{q\},I=[s+\epsilon,t-\epsilon]
along with Lemma
3.1
(ii) gives that

H(p,q,[s+\epsilon,t-\epsilon])\leq 121

for all
\epsilon>0
. Now,
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}H(p,q,[s+\epsilon,t-\epsilon])=H(p,q,[s,t])
, yielding part (ii).
∎

3.2
The overlap metric

We can use overlap to define a tractable metric on the space of geodesics.
Let
\gamma,\pi
be two geodesics with domains
I,I^{\prime}
, and let
\lambda
denote Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
. Define the
overlap distance
between
\gamma
and
\pi
as

d_{o}(\gamma,\pi)=\lambda(I)+\lambda(I^{\prime})-2\lambda(O(\pi,\gamma)),

and write
\to_{o}
for convergence in this metric.
For this next proposition we let
\Gamma(S)
denote the set of geodesics with endpoints in a set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
.

Proposition 3.5
.

The overlap distance
d_{o}
is a metric on the space of all geodesics. Under this metric:

1.

The countable set
\Gamma(\mathbb{Q}^{4}_{\uparrow})
is dense in
\Gamma(\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow})
.

2.

For any compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, the overlap metric makes
\Gamma(K)
a compact Polish space and
\gamma_{n}\to_{o}\gamma
in
\Gamma(K)
if and only if
\mathfrak{g}\gamma_{n}\to\mathfrak{g}\gamma
in the Hausdorff topology.

Proof.

It is easy to check that
d_{o}
defines a metric. Indeed, for geodesics
\pi,\gamma,\tau
where
\gamma
has domain
I
, the set containment
O(\pi,\gamma)\cap O(\gamma,\tau)\subset O(\pi,\tau)
implies that

\lambda(O(\pi,\gamma))+\lambda(O(\gamma,\tau))\leq\lambda(O(\pi,\tau))+\lambda(I),

which yields the triangle inequality. The density of
\Gamma(\mathbb{Q}^{4}_{\uparrow})
in
\Gamma(\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow})
follows from Lemma
3.3
.1.

For the second point, first observe that by Corollary
2.6
and the fact that pointwise limits of geodesics are geodesics (Fact
F.2
), the space
\Gamma(K)
is both complete and compact in the topology of Hausdorff convergence of the graphs
\mathfrak{g}\gamma
. Therefore we just need to verify that the Hausdorff and overlap metrics induce the same topology on
\Gamma(K)
. If
\mathfrak{g}\gamma_{n}\to\mathfrak{g}\gamma
, then we must have that
\gamma_{n}\to_{o}\gamma
as otherwise we contradict Proposition
3.4
(i). On the other hand, if
\gamma_{n}\to_{o}\gamma
then the only potential limit point for
\mathfrak{g}\gamma_{n}
in the space
\{\mathfrak{g}\pi:\pi\in\Gamma(K)\}
is
\mathfrak{g}\gamma
. Therefore by compactness of the graph space
\{\mathfrak{g}\pi:\pi\in\Gamma(K)\}
, we have
\mathfrak{g}\gamma_{n}\to\mathfrak{g}\gamma
.
∎

We end the section with one more overlap approximation lemma.

Lemma 3.6
.

For any
(p;q)=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
and any geodesic
\gamma
from
p
to
q
we can find a sequence of rational points
q_{n}\to q
and a sequence of geodesics
\pi_{n}
from
p
to
q_{n}
such that the overlap
O(\pi_{n},\gamma)=[s,r_{n}]
for a sequence
r_{n}\to t
as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

Since there are only finitely many geodesics from
p
to
q
(Proposition
3.4
(ii)) for every
\epsilon>0
we can find a point
r\in(t-\epsilon,t)
and
\delta>0
such that every geodesic
\sigma
from
p
to
q
with
\sigma(r)=\gamma(r)
has
[r-\delta,r]\subset O(\sigma,\gamma)
. If we can prove the claim in lemma with
\gamma
replaced by
\gamma|_{[s,r]}
, then the claim for
\gamma
follows by taking
\epsilon\to 0
and applying a diagonalization argument.

Consider any sequence of rational points
q_{n}=(x_{n},t_{n})\to\bar{\gamma}(r)
and let
\sigma_{n}
be a sequence of geodesics from
p
to
q_{n}
. By Proposition
3.5
, after possibly passing to a subsequence we may assume that
\sigma_{n}\to_{o}\sigma
for some geodesic
\sigma
to
p
to
\bar{\gamma}_{n}
. Therefore
\sigma_{n}(r-\delta)=\sigma(r-\delta)=\gamma(r-\delta)
for all large enough
n
, so the concatenation
\pi_{n}:=\gamma|_{[s,r-\delta]}\oplus\sigma_{n}|_{[r-\delta,t_{n}]}
is a geodesic from
p
to
q_{n}
. The sequence
\pi_{n}
satisfies the claim in the lemma.
∎

4
Geodesic stars

As discussed in Section
1.1
one of the main components in the proof of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
is a careful study of weighted geodesic stars. This is the subject of the present section. Along the way, we will prove Theorem
1.5
.
First, for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<},s\in\mathbb{R}
, and
p\in\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,s),q\in\mathbb{R}\times(s,\infty)
define the
weight
vectors

W^{-}_{\mathbf{x},s}(p)=(\mathcal{L}(p;x_{1},s),\dots,\mathcal{L}(p;x_{k},s)),\qquad W^{+}_{\mathbf{x},s}(q)=(\mathcal{L}(x_{1},s;q),\dots,\mathcal{L}(x_{k},s;q)).

Next, for
W\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
we let

\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;W)

be the set of points
p\in\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,s)
such that
W^{-}_{\mathbf{x},s}(p)\in W
and such that there is a forwards
k
-geodesic star from
p
to
(\mathbf{x},s)
. We similarly let
\operatorname{Star}_{R}(\mathbf{x},s;W)
be the set of points
q\in\mathbb{R}\times(s,\infty)
such that
W^{+}_{\mathbf{x},s}(q)\in W

and such that
there is a reverse
k
-geodesic star from
(\mathbf{x},t)
to
q
.
For
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}_{<}
,
s<t
, and
W\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
we define

\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;W)=\bigcup_{(w_{1},w_{2})\in W}\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;\{w_{1}\})\times\operatorname{Star}_{R}(\mathbf{y},y;\{w_{2}\}).

The goal of the section is to bound the sizes of the sets
\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;W)
when
W\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
is an affine set satisfying
W=W+(a^{k},b^{\ell})
