for every
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. These are the sets that will appear when we apply cut and paste operations to geodesic networks. We call a set
W
satisfying
W=W+(a^{k},b^{\ell})
for every
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
a
slide-invariant set
.

4.1
The lower bound

The lower bound is easier so we start there.
The lower bound on the sizes of the sets
\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;W)
takes a strong form, in that we will get an almost sure lower bound for all quintuples
(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;W)
simultaneously.

First, for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{>},t\in\mathbb{R}
define the sets

\displaystyle S^{-}(\mathbf{x},t):=\{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\mathcal{L}(0,t-1;x_{i},t)-w_{i}>\mathcal{L}(0,t-1;x_{2},t)-w_{2},i=1,3\},

\displaystyle S^{+}(\mathbf{x},t):=\{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\mathcal{L}(x_{i},t;0,t+1)-w_{i}>\mathcal{L}(x_{2},t;0,t+1)-w_{2},i=1,3\}.

Also, for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
for
k=1,2
let
S^{\pm}(\mathbf{x},t)=\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Almost surely, the following assertion holds for every
k,\ell\in\{1,2,3\},\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}_{<}
,
s<t
, and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

There exist compact sets
K_{1}=K_{1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},n)\subset\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,s),K_{2}=K_{2}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},n)\subset\mathbb{R}\times(t,\infty)
such that the following holds. Consider any slide-invariant affine set
W\subset\mathbb{R}^{k+\ell}
with

W^{\prime}=W\cap[S^{-}(\mathbf{x},s)\times S^{+}(\mathbf{y},t)]\cap(-n,n)^{k+\ell}\neq\emptyset,

(28)

and let
g(W)
be the smallest slide-invariant subset of
\mathbb{R}^{k+\ell}
containing
W^{\prime}
. Then the set
\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;g(W))\cap(K_{1}\times K_{2})
is non-empty and

\displaystyle\dim_{1:2:3}[\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;g(W))\cap(K_{1}\times K_{2})]\geq\dim W+10-\frac{k(k+1)}{2}-\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{2}.

Here
\dim W
is the linear dimension.

The main part of the proof of Proposition
4.1
is the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

Almost surely, the following statements hold.

1.

Let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{<}
and
s<t\in\mathbb{R}
, and define the function
F=F[\mathbf{x},s,t]:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by

F(y)=\mathcal{L}(y,s;x_{1},t)-\mathcal{L}(y,s;x_{2},t).

Then
F
is a continuous, non-increasing function with
\operatorname{Range}(F)=\mathbb{R}
. In particular, the set
F^{-1}(u)
is a nonempty closed interval for all
u\in\mathbb{R}
. Letting
a<b
denote the endpoints of this interval, there are forward geodesic stars from
(a,s)
to
(\mathbf{x},t)
and from
(b,s)
to
(\mathbf{x},t)
.

2.

Let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{<},t\in\mathbb{R}
and recall the definition of
S^{-}(\mathbf{x},t)
above.
Then for any
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},0,u_{3})\in S^{-}(\mathbf{x},t)
there exists a point
p\in\mathbb{R}\times(t-1,t)
for which there is a forwards geodesic star
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3})
from
p
to
(\mathbf{x},t)
with

(\|\pi_{1}\|_{\mathcal{L}}-\|\pi_{2}\|_{\mathcal{L}},\|\pi_{3}\|_{\mathcal{L}}-\|\pi_{2}\|_{\mathcal{L}})=(u_{1},u_{3}).

The proof of Lemma
4.2
is essentially topological, using only planarity and properties of the coalescent geometry for
\mathcal{L}
. We take inspiration from previous proofs.
A version of Lemma
4.2
.1 where the almost sure claim is only for fixed
s,t
in
\mathbb{R}
was shown as part of
[
BGH22
, Theorem 1.9]
using related ideas. Moreover, similar topological ideas for both parts
1
and
2
have been used in
[
Gwy21
]
to prove the existence of certain geodesic networks in Liouville quantum gravity.

Proof.

Proof of 1.
The fact that
F
is a non-increasing function of
y
follows from the quadrangle inequality (
9
). The asymptotics in Theorem
2.5
guarantee that
F(y)\to-\infty
as
y\to-\infty
and
F(y)\to\infty
as
y\to\infty
so
\operatorname{Range}(f)=\mathbb{R}
. Fixing
u\in\mathbb{R}
and letting
F^{-1}(u)=[a,b]
, it just remains to check that there are
2
-geodesic stars from
(a,s)
and
(b,s)
to
(\mathbf{x},t)
.

Suppose first that there is not a geodesic star from
(a,s)
to
(\mathbf{x},t)
. Then the leftmost geodesics
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
from
(a,s)
to
(x_{1},t),(x_{2},t)
satisfy
\pi_{1}(r)=\pi_{2}(r)
for some
r\in(s,t)
. Since
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
are leftmost we necessarily have
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}
on
[s,r]
. Next, let
\pi^{n}
be the sequence of leftmost geodesics from
(a-1/n,s)
to
(x_{1},t)
. The sequence
\pi^{n}
is tight in the overlap metric (Proposition
3.5
) and any subsequential limit must be a geodesic from
(a,s)
to
(x_{1},t)
. The ordering on leftmost geodesics (Lemma
2.7
.3) then implies that
\pi^{n}\to_{o}\pi
.
Therefore for some
n
we have
\pi^{n}(r)=\pi_{1}(r)=\pi_{2}(r)
and
\pi^{n}|_{[r,t]}=\pi_{1}|_{[r,t]}
, and so

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(a-1/n,s;x_{1},t)-\mathcal{L}(a-1/n,s;x_{2},t)

\displaystyle\leq\|\pi^{n}\|_{\mathcal{L}}-\|\pi^{n}|_{[s,r]}\oplus\pi_{2}|_{[r,t]}\|_{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle=\|\pi_{1}|_{[r,t]}\|-\|\pi_{2}|_{[r,t]}\|

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(a,s;x_{1},t)-\mathcal{L}(a,s;x_{2},t)=u.

Since
F
is non-increasing, this must be an equality, contradicting that
F^{-1}(c)=[a,b]
. A similar argument shows that there is a geodesic star from
(b,s)
to
(\mathbf{x},t)
.

Proof of 2.
Fix
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},0,u_{3})\in S^{-}(\mathbf{x},t)
, and set
u_{2}=0
. Define three regions
R^{\mathbf{u}}_{1},R^{\mathbf{u}}_{2},R^{\mathbf{u}}_{3}\subset\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,t)
by

R^{\mathbf{u}}_{i}=\{p\in\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,t):\mathcal{L}(p;x_{i},t)-u_{i}\geq\mathcal{L}(p;x_{j},t)-u_{j},j\in\llbracket 1,3\rrbracket\setminus\{i\}\}.

We can make a few observations about the sets
R^{\mathbf{u}}_{i}
.

1.

By Theorem
2.5
, each
R^{\mathbf{u}}_{i}
contains the intersection of an open ball around the point
(x_{i},t)
with
\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,t)
and the set
R_{2}^{\mathbf{u}}\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[t-1,t))
is bounded.

2.

By continuity of
\mathcal{L}
, each of the sets
R^{\mathbf{u}}_{i}
is closed in
\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,t)
.

3.

By the quadrangle inequality (
9
)
and the definition of
S^{-}(\mathbf{x},t)
we have that
R_{2}^{\mathbf{u}}\cap(\mathbb{R}\times\{t-1\})=\emptyset
.

We claim that in this case the set of
P
of points
p\in\mathbb{R}\times(t-1,t)
where

\mathcal{L}(p;x_{1},t)-u_{1}=\mathcal{L}(p;x_{2},t)=\mathcal{L}(p;x_{3},t)-u_{3}

(29)

is nonempty. The three points above guarantee that
R_{2}^{\mathbf{u}}\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[t-1,t))
is a closed, bounded, nonempty subset which is disjoint from
\mathbb{R}\times\{t-1\}
. Therefore there is a minimal time
s_{0}\in(t-1,t)
for which

R_{2}^{\mathbf{u}}\cap(\mathbb{R}\times\{s_{0}\})\neq\emptyset.

By the quadrangle inequality (
9
) and continuity of
\mathcal{L}
, there are points
y_{1}\leq y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

[y_{1},y_{2}]\times\{s_{0}\}=R_{2}^{\mathbf{u}}\cap(\mathbb{R}\times\{s_{0}\}),\quad y_{1}\in R_{1}^{\mathbf{u}},\quad y_{2}\in R_{3}^{\mathbf{u}}.

Moreover, since
\mathbb{R}\times[t-1,s_{0})
does not intersect
R_{2}^{\mathbf{u}}
, and
R^{\mathbf{u}}_{1}\cup R^{\mathbf{u}}_{3}
is a closed set containing the complement of
R_{2}^{\mathbf{u}}
, we have

[y_{1},y_{2}]\times\{s\}\subset R_{1}^{\mathbf{u}}\cup R_{3}^{\mathbf{u}}.

Using this, along with the fact that
y_{1}\in R_{1}^{\mathbf{u}},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{3}^{\mathbf{u}}
and
R^{\mathbf{u}}_{1}
and
R^{\mathbf{u}}_{3}
are closed yields a point
(y,s_{0})\in R_{1}^{\mathbf{u}}\cap R_{3}^{\mathbf{u}}
with
y\in[y_{1},y_{2}]
. This point is in
P
.

Now,
P
is closed since
\mathcal{L}
is continuous, and by point
1
above it is separated from the line
\mathbb{R}\times\{t\}
. Therefore there is a point
p=(y,s)
in
P
with maximal time coordinate. If there is a geodesic star from
p
to
(\mathbf{x},t)
, then by (
29
),
p
satisfies the conditions of part
2
. We will show that the maximality of
s
guarantees the existence of such a geodesic star.

Let
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3})
be the three leftmost geodesics from
p
to
(x_{i},t),i=1,2,3
. We have
\pi_{1}\leq\pi_{2}\leq\pi_{3}
by Lemma
2.7
.3 and the overlaps
O(\pi_{i},\pi_{j})
are always intervals of the form
[s,r^{\prime}]
for some
r^{\prime}\geq s
since otherwise we could perform a geodesic switching argument (Fact
F.4
) to contradict that one of the
\pi_{i}
is leftmost.

If there is an
r\in(s,t)
with
\pi_{1}(r)=\pi_{2}(r)=\pi_{3}(r)
, then
(\pi_{1}(r),r)\in P
contradicting the maximality of
s
. Therefore either
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are disjoint or
\pi_{2}
and
\pi_{3}
are disjoint. Without loss of generality, assume
\pi_{2}
and
\pi_{3}
are disjoint, and that
O(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})=[s,r^{\prime}]
with
s<r^{\prime}
.
Now for every
r\in(s,r^{\prime}]
, define

[a_{r},b_{r}]=\{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}:\mathcal{L}(y^{\prime},r;x_{1},t)-u_{1}=\mathcal{L}(y^{\prime},r;x_{2},t)\}.

The fact that the right-hand side above is an interval follows from part
1
, and
[a_{r},b_{r}]
always contains
\pi_{1}(r)
.

Claim:

b_{r}\in[\pi_{1}(r),\pi_{3}(r))
. In particular,
b_{r}\to y
as
r\downarrow s
.

Proof:
Set

d_{r}=\min\{x\in\mathbb{R}:(x,r)\in R^{\mathbf{u}}_{3}\}.

At the point
d_{r}
, we have that

(\mathcal{L}(d_{r},r;x_{1},t)-u_{1})\vee\mathcal{L}(d_{r},r;x_{2},t)=\mathcal{L}(d_{r},r;x_{3},t)-u_{3}.

Therefore if
d_{r}\in[a_{r},b_{r}]
then the point
(d_{r},r)\in P
which contradicts the maximality of
s
. On the other hand, if
d_{r}\leq\pi_{1}(r)
, then

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}(p;\bar{\pi}_{1}(r))+\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}_{1}(r),x_{3},t)&\geq\mathcal{L}(p;\bar{\pi}_{1}(r))+\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}_{1}(r),x_{2},t)+u_{2}\\
&=\mathcal{L}(p;x_{2},t)+u_{2}\\
&=\mathcal{L}(p;x_{3},t).\end{split}

Here the inequality uses that
d_{r}\leq\pi_{1}(r)
, the first equality uses that
\pi_{2}
is a geodesic from
p
to
(x_{2},s)
going through the point
\bar{\pi}_{1}(r)
, and the second equality uses that
p\in P
. This calculation implies that there is a geodesic from
p
to
(x_{3},s)
going through the point
\bar{\pi}_{1}(r)
. Since
\pi_{3}(r)>\pi_{1}(r)
, this contradicts that
\pi_{3}
is the leftmost geodesic from
p
to
(x_{3},s)
. Hence
d_{r}>\pi_{1}(r)
.

Next, if
\pi_{3}(r)<d_{r}
, then by similar reasoning we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}(p;\bar{\pi}_{3}(r))&+(\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}_{3}(r);x_{1},t)-u_{1})\vee\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}_{3}(r);x_{2},t)\\
&>\mathcal{L}(p;\bar{\pi}_{3}(r))+\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}_{3}(r);x_{3},t)-u_{3}\\
&=\mathcal{L}(p;x_{3},s)-u_{3}\\
&=(\mathcal{L}(p;x_{1},t)-u_{1})\vee\mathcal{L}(p;x_{2},t),\end{split}

which contradicts the triangle inequality for
\mathcal{L}
. Therefore
d_{r}\in(\pi_{1}(r),\pi_{3}(r)]\cap[a_{r},b_{r}]^{c}
. Since
\pi_{1}(r)\in[a_{r},b_{r}]
this implies
b_{r}<d_{r}\leq\pi_{3}(r)
, completing the proof of the claim.

\blacksquare

Now, by part
1
of the lemma, at the point
b_{r}
, there are disjoint geodesics
\pi^{1,r},\pi^{2,r}
from
(b_{r},r)
to
(x_{1},t)
and
(x_{2},t)
. Since
(b_{r},r)\to p
, these geodesic sequences are precompact in the overlap topology with limits that are geodesics from
p
to
(x_{1},t)
and
(x_{2},t)
(Proposition
3.5
). Therefore since disjointness is preserved by overlap convergence there exist disjoint geodesics
\tilde{\pi}_{1},\tilde{\pi}_{2}
from
p
to
(x_{1},t),(x_{2},t)
.

If
\tilde{\pi}_{2}
is disjoint from the rightmost geodesic
\tau_{3}
from
p
to
(x_{3},t)
, then
(\tilde{\pi}_{1},\tilde{\pi}_{2},\tau_{3})
gives a desired geodesic star. If not, then letting
\tau_{2}
be the rightmost geodesic from
p
to
(x_{2},t)
, there exists an interval
[s,r^{\prime\prime}]\subset[s,r^{\prime}]
along which
\tau_{2}=\tau_{3}
. For every
r\in(s,r^{\prime\prime}]
, define

[a_{r}^{\prime},b_{r}^{\prime}]=\{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}:\mathcal{L}(y^{\prime},r;x_{2},t)=\mathcal{L}(y^{\prime},r;x_{3},t)-u_{3}\},

and note that
\pi_{3}(r)\in[a_{r}^{\prime},b_{r}^{\prime}]
.
Now, if
a_{r}^{\prime}\leq b_{r}
for any
r\in(s,r^{\prime\prime}]
, then since
b_{r}<\pi_{3}(r)
, we have that
b_{r}\in[a_{r}^{\prime},b_{r}^{\prime}]
and so
(b_{r},r)
is necessarily in
P
. This contradicts the maximality of
s
. Therefore we may assume
b_{r}<a_{r}^{\prime}
for all
r\in(s,r^{\prime\prime}]
.

Again using part
1
of the lemma, the leftmost geodesic from
(a_{r}^{\prime},r)
to
(x_{2},t)
is disjoint the rightmost geodesic from
(a_{r}^{\prime},r)
to
(x_{3},t)
. Therefore since
b_{r}<a_{r}^{\prime}
, the rightmost geodesic from
(b_{r},r)
to
(x_{2},t)
is disjoint from
\tau_{3}
; this uses the monotonicity in Lemma
2.7
.4.
Therefore
\pi^{2,r},\pi^{1,r}
are disjoint from
\tau_{3}
for all
r
, and since overlap convergence preserves disjointness, so are
\tilde{\pi}_{1},\tilde{\pi}_{2}
. This is a contradiction.
∎

From Lemma
4.2
and the modulus of continuity for
\mathcal{L}
we can lower bound the Hausdorff dimension of the sets
\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;W)
and prove Proposition
4.1
.
We require a simple lemma relating Hausdorff dimension and Hölder continuous maps. This lemma is well-known, but we could not locate a reference for the exact generality we need here, so include a short proof. Here we let
\dim_{d}
denote Hausdorff dimension with respect to a metric
d
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
(X,d_{1}),(Y,d_{2})
be two metric spaces, each of which can be written as a countable union of compact sets. Let
f:X\to Y
be a function and suppose that
f
is locally Hölder-
\alpha
continuous (i.e. Hölder-
\alpha
continuous on every compact subset of
X
). Then for any set
U\subset X
,

\dim_{d_{2}}(f(U))\leq\alpha^{-1}\dim_{d_{1}}(U).

(30)

Proof.

Let
X=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}K_{n}
, where the
K_{n}
are compact. By countable stability of Hausdorff dimension, it suffices to prove (
30
) for
U\cap K_{n}
for all
n
. Now suppose that
\dim_{d_{1}}(U\cap K_{n})<\beta<\infty
. Then there exists
m>0
such that for all
\epsilon>0
, there exists a cover
V_{i},i\in\mathbb{N}
of
U\cap K_{n}
with
\operatorname{diam}(V_{i})<\epsilon
and
\sum_{i}\operatorname{diam}(V_{i})^{\beta}<m
. Then
f(V_{i}),i\in\mathbb{N}
is a cover for
f(U\cap K_{n})
, and letting
c_{n}
be the Hölder-
\alpha
constant for
f
on
K_{n}
, we have that

\operatorname{diam}(f(V_{i}))\leq c_{n}\operatorname{diam}(V_{i})^{\alpha}\leq c_{n}\epsilon^{\alpha},\quad\sum_{i}\operatorname{diam}(f(V_{i}))^{\beta/\alpha}\leq c_{n}^{\beta/\alpha}\sum_{i}\operatorname{diam}(V_{i})^{\beta}<c_{n}^{\beta/\alpha}m

and so
\dim_{d_{2}}(f(U\cap K_{n}))\leq\beta/\alpha
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

We prove Proposition
4.1
by finding a locally Hölder continuous map relating the star-sets and the set
W
, and then applying Lemma
4.3
. Let
(\mathbf{x},s)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R},(\mathbf{y},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{R}
. The definition of the map depends on the choice of
k,\ell
, and by time-reversal symmetry of
\mathcal{L}
(Lemma
2.2
.3) we may assume
k\leq\ell
. First, define
L_{3}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2},L_{2}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
by

L_{3}(\mathbf{w})=(w_{1}-w_{2},w_{3}-w_{2}),\qquad L_{2}(\mathbf{w})=w_{1}-w_{2}.

Next we define the compact set
K_{1}
. If
k=1
, set
K_{1}=[-1,1]\times[s-1,s-1/2]
. If
k=2
, then by Theorem
2.5
there is a random constant
C>0
such that

|\mathcal{L}(p;x_{1},s)-\mathcal{L}(p;x_{2},s)|>2n\qquad\text{ for all }p\in[-C,C]^{c}\times[s-1,s).

(31)

In this case we set
K_{1}=[-C,C]\times[s-1,s-1/2]
. Finally, when
k=3
we keep the definition of
C
above. We can also find a random
\epsilon>0
such that whenever
p\in[-C,C]\times[s-\epsilon,s)
we have

|\mathcal{L}(p;x_{1},s)-\mathcal{L}(p;x_{2},s)|\vee|\mathcal{L}(p;x_{3},s)-\mathcal{L}(p;x_{2},s)|>2n.

(32)

In this final case, set
K_{2}=[-C,C]\times[s-1,s-\epsilon].
We can define
K_{2}
analogously.

Finally, define maps
f_{k,\ell}:K_{1}\times K_{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{4}
for the six choices of
k\leq\ell
as follows. For each of these maps, we equip the domain with the
d_{1:2:3}
metric and the range with a metric
e_{k,\ell}
defined below.

•

f_{1,1}
is the identity and
e_{1,1}=d_{1:2:3}
.

•

f_{1,2}(z,r;z^{\prime},r^{\prime})=(z,r,r^{\prime},L_{2}\circ W^{+}_{\mathbf{y},t}(z^{\prime},r^{\prime}))
and

e_{1,2}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=|u_{1}-v_{1}|^{1/2}+|u_{2}-v_{2}|^{1/3}+|u_{3}-v_{3}|^{1/3}+|u_{4}-v_{4}|.

•

f_{1,3}(z,r;z^{\prime},r^{\prime})=(z,r,L_{3}\circ W^{+}_{\mathbf{y},t}(z^{\prime},r^{\prime}))
and

e_{1,3}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=|u_{1}-v_{1}|^{1/2}+|u_{2}-v_{2}|^{1/3}+|u_{3}-v_{3}|+|u_{4}-v_{4}|.

•

f_{2,2}(z,r;z^{\prime},r^{\prime})=(r,r^{\prime},L_{2}\circ W^{-}_{\mathbf{x},s}(z,r),L_{2}\circ W^{+}_{\mathbf{y},t}(z^{\prime},r^{\prime}))
and

e_{2,2}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=|u_{1}-v_{1}|^{1/3}+|u_{2}-v_{2}|^{1/3}+|u_{3}-v_{3}|+|u_{4}-v_{4}|.

•

f_{2,3}(z,r;z^{\prime},r^{\prime})=(r,L_{2}\circ W^{-}_{\mathbf{x},s}(z,r);L_{3}\circ W^{+}_{\mathbf{y},t}(z^{\prime},r^{\prime}))
and

e_{2,3}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=|u_{1}-v_{1}|^{1/3}+|u_{2}-v_{2}|+|u_{3}-v_{3}|+|u_{4}-v_{4}|.

•

f_{3,3}(z,r;z^{\prime},r^{\prime})=(L_{3}\circ W^{-}_{\mathbf{x},s}(z,r),L_{3}\circ W^{+}_{\mathbf{y},t}(z^{\prime},r^{\prime}))
and let
e_{3,3}
be the usual Euclidean metric.

By the modulus of continuity for
\mathcal{L}
(Proposition
2.4
) all of the maps
f_{k,\ell}
are locally Hölder-
(1-\epsilon)
continuous for all
\epsilon>0
with the prescribed codomain metrics. Now, let
L_{k,\ell}:=(L_{k},L_{\ell}):\mathbb{R}^{k+\ell}\to\mathbb{R}^{k-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{\ell-1}
. We claim that

B_{k,\ell}\times L_{k,\ell}(g(W))\subset f_{k,\ell}(\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;g(W)))

(33)

where
B_{k,\ell}
is a
6-k-\ell
dimensional product of positive-length intervals.
It suffices to check (
33
) when
W=L_{k,\ell}^{-1}(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})
for some
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k-1},\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell-1}
, since every other slide-invariant set
W
is a disjoint union of sets of this form. We may assume
g(W)\neq\emptyset
since otherwise the claim is trivial. In this case
L_{k,\ell}(g(W))=(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;W)=\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;L_{k}^{-1}(\mathbf{a}))\times\operatorname{Star}_{R}(\mathbf{y},t;L_{\ell}^{-1}(\mathbf{b})),

and
L_{k}^{-1}(\mathbf{a})\cap(-n,n)^{k}\neq\emptyset,L^{-1}_{\ell}(\mathbf{b})\cap(-n,n)^{\ell}\neq\emptyset
.

Now, if
k=1
, then
L_{k}^{-1}(\mathbf{a})=\mathbb{R}
and so
\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;L_{k}^{-1}(\mathbf{a}))\cap K_{1}=K_{1}
since there are geodesics between any pair of points. If
k=2
, then by Lemma
4.2
.1,
\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;L_{k}^{-1}(\mathbf{a}))
contains a point of the form
(y,r)
for all
r\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, since
L_{2}^{-1}(\mathbf{a})\cap(-n,n)^{2}\neq\emptyset
, if
(y,r)\in\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;L_{k}^{-1}(\mathbf{a}))
for
r\in[s-1,s-1/2]
then by (
31
) we must have
y\in[-C,C]
. Therefore
\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;L_{k}^{-1}(\mathbf{a}))
contains a point in
K_{1}\cap(\mathbb{R}\times\{r\})
for all
r\in[s-1,s-1/2]
. If
k=3
, then by Lemma
4.2
.2 and the fact that
g(W)\neq\emptyset
, the set
\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;L_{k}^{-1}(\mathbf{a}))
contains a point
p\in\mathbb{R}\times(s-1,s)
. Moreover, since
L_{3}^{-1}(\mathbf{a})\cap(-n,n)^{3}\neq\emptyset
, by (
31
) and (
32
) we must have
p\in K_{1}
.
This analysis, along with similar reasoning applied to
\operatorname{Star}_{R}(\mathbf{y},t;L_{\ell}^{-1}(\mathbf{b}))\cap K_{2}
, yields (
33
) for
W=L_{k}^{-1}(\mathbf{a})\times L^{-1}_{\ell}(\mathbf{b})
.

The fact that the left-hand side of (
33
) is non-empty implies that
\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;g(W))
is non-empty. Moreover, Lemma
4.3
and the fact that
f_{k,\ell}
is locally Hölder-
(1-\epsilon)
continuous for all
\epsilon>0
implies that

\dim_{1:2:3}\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;g(W))\geq\dim_{e_{k,\ell}}(B_{k,\ell}\times L_{k,\ell}(g(W))).

It is straightforward to calculate the Hausdorff dimension of the right-hand side above. Indeed, since
W
is a slide-invariant affine set,
L_{k,\ell}(g(W))
is a non-empty intersection of a
\dim W-2
dimensional affine set and an open set. The set
B_{k,\ell}
is simply a product of positive-length intervals. Going through the cases for each
k,\ell
, we get that

\dim_{e_{k,\ell}}(B_{k,\ell}\times L_{k,\ell}(g(W)))\geq 12-\frac{k(k+1)}{2}-\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{2}+\dim W-2,

yielding the bound in the proposition.
∎

4.2
Upper bounds on dimension

The upper bound on the dimension of
\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;W)
is more difficult, and leans heavily on an analysis of a last passage problem in the Airy line ensemble arising via Proposition
2.16
. The upper bound we achieve is not as strong as the lower bound: it only holds almost surely for a fixed quintuple
(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;W)
, rather than for all quintuples simultaneously.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
s<t
, let
\ell,k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}_{<}
, and let
W\subset\mathbb{R}^{k+\ell}
be any affine set. Then setting

d(k,\ell,W)=\dim W+10-\frac{k(k+1)}{2}-\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{2}

almost surely we have

\dim_{1:2:3}[\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;W)]\leq d(k,\ell,W)\vee 0.

Moreover if
d(k,\ell,W)<0
then the set
\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;W)
is almost surely empty.

Before proceeding with the proof of Proposition
4.4
we show how when combined with the lower bound in Proposition
4.1
, it implies Theorem
1.5
.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

The
k=1
case is immediate since
\operatorname{Star}_{1}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\dim_{1:2:3}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=5
. Let
k\in\{2,3,4\}
. Let
\operatorname{Star}_{k,1}
be the set of all points
p
that start a forwards
k
-geodesic star. By time-reversal symmetry of
\mathcal{L}
, it suffices to prove the theorem with
\operatorname{Star}_{k,1}
in place of the larger set
\operatorname{Star}_{k}
.

First, by Lemma
3.6
, if
p\in\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},t,\mathbb{R}^{k})
then
p\in\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},t^{\prime},\mathbb{R}^{k})
for some rational
\mathbf{x}^{\prime},t^{\prime}
close to
\mathbf{x},t
. Therefore

\operatorname{Star}_{k,1}=\bigcup_{(\mathbf{x},t)\in\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{<}\times\mathbb{Q}}\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},t,\mathbb{R}^{k}).

Now, almost surely for all
(\mathbf{x},t)\in\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{<}\times\mathbb{Q}
we have

\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},t;0,t+1;\mathbb{R}^{k+1})=\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},t,\mathbb{R}^{k})\times\mathbb{R}\times(t+1,\infty).

If
k=4
, then Proposition
4.4
implies that almost surely

\dim_{1:2:3}[\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},t,\mathbb{R}^{k})\times\mathbb{R}\times(t+1,\infty)]\leq 4.

This can only hold if
\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},t,\mathbb{R}^{k})
is empty, and so almost surely
\operatorname{Star}_{4,1}
is also empty.
If
k=2,3
, then Proposition
4.1
and Proposition
4.4
together imply that

\dim_{1:2:3}[\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},t,\mathbb{R}^{k})\times\mathbb{R}\times(t+1,\infty)]=10-\frac{k(k-1)}{2}.

Now, standard results about the Hausdorff dimension of Cartesian products imply that the Hausdorff dimension of a Cartesian product
A\times B
equals the sum of the Hausdorff dimensions when the Hausdorff dimension of
B
coincides with its upper packing dimension (e.g. see
[
Mat99
, Corollary 8.11]
for the result in Euclidean space; the proof goes through verbatim for dimensions defined with the metric
d_{1:2:3}
). Therefore

\displaystyle\dim_{1:2:3}[\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},t,\mathbb{R}^{k})\times\mathbb{R}\times(t+1,\infty)]

\displaystyle=\dim_{1:2:3}[\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},t,\mathbb{R}^{k})]+\dim_{1:2:3}[\mathbb{R}\times(t+1,\infty)]

\displaystyle=\dim_{1:2:3}[\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},t,\mathbb{R}^{k})]+5,

which implies that
\dim_{1:2:3}(\operatorname{Star}_{2,1})=4,\dim_{1:2:3}(\operatorname{Star}_{3,1})=2
.
∎

The proof of Proposition
4.4
is based on an application of Theorem
2.15
and Proposition
2.16
. These results are connected to the study of geodesic stars by the following lemma.

For this lemma and throughout this section, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
let
T_{k}=\{(i,j)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}:i+j\leq k+1\}
. We say that
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{T_{k}}
is a
k
-level
Gelfand-Tsetlin pattern
if it satisfies the interlacing inequalities

w_{i,j}\leq w_{i+1,j}\leq w_{i,j+1}

for all
i+j\leq k
, and let
\operatorname{GT}_{k}
be the space of
k
-level Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns. For
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}_{\leq}
we let
\operatorname{GT}_{k}(\mathbf{x})
be the set of all
\mathbf{w}\in\operatorname{GT}_{k}
such that
(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x})\in\operatorname{GT}_{k+1}
where
(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x})_{i,j}=w_{i-1,j}
for
i\geq 2
and
(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x})_{1,j}=x_{j}
.

Lemma 4.5
.

For every point
p=(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
s>t
define a sequence of continuous functions
\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{2}\dots,
from
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by the formula

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{i}(y)=\mathcal{L}(p^{k};y^{k},s).

Then almost surely, for all
k\in\{2,3,4\},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<},s\in\mathbb{R}
, and
p=(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,s)
, the following two statements are equivalent:

A.

There is a
k
-geodesic star from
p
to
(\mathbf{y},s)
and
W^{-}_{\mathbf{y},s}(p)=\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

B.

We have
\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{1}(y_{i})=w_{i}
for all
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
and there exists
\mathbf{z}\in\operatorname{GT}_{k-1}(\mathbf{y})
with
\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{i}(z_{i,j})=\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{i+1}(z_{i,j})
for all
(i,j)\in T_{k}
.

Proof.

First, if there is a
k
-geodesic star
\pi
from
p=(x,t)
to
(\mathbf{y},s)
with
w(\pi)=\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, then

\mathcal{L}(x^{k},t;\mathbf{y},s)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}(x,t;y_{i},s).

(34)

Moreover, as discussed after Definition
2.13
, almost surely the extended landscape value
\mathcal{L}(x^{k},t;\mathbf{y},s)
is attained by a disjoint
k
-tuple for all
p=(x,t),k,\mathbf{y},s
. Therefore almost surely for all choices of
x,k,t,\mathbf{y},s
, statement
A
holds if and only if both (
34
) holds and

\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{1}(y_{i})=\mathcal{L}(p;y_{i},s)=w_{i}

for all
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
.

On the other hand, almost surely we have
\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{1}\geq\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{2}\geq\dots
for all
p,s
. Indeed, this holds for a fixed
p,s
since in this case
\mathcal{L}^{p,s}
is a rescaled parabolic Airy line ensemble (Theorem
2.15
.1). The extension to all
p,s
follows by continuity. Therefore almost surely for all
p,s,k,\mathbf{y}
, there exists
\mathbf{z}\in\operatorname{GT}_{k-1}(\mathbf{y})
with
\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{i}(z_{i,j})=\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{i+1}(z_{i,j})
for all
(i,j)\in T_{k-1}
if and only if

\mathcal{L}^{p,s}[((y_{1}-1)^{k},k)\to(\mathbf{y},1)]+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}_{i}^{p,s}(y_{1}-1)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{1}(y_{i}),

(35)

see Figure
7
. Therefore to prove the lemma it suffices to show that almost surely, for all
k,\mathbf{y},s,p
the statements (
35
) and (
34
) are equivalent. The right-hand sides of (
34
) and (
35
) are equal by definition. Also, for a fixed choice of
k,\mathbf{y},s,p
the left-hand sides are equal almost surely by Theorem
2.15
.2 and Proposition
2.16
. This extends to all rational
k,\mathbf{y},s,p
by countable additivity, and then almost surely to all
k,\mathbf{y},s,p
since the left-hand sides of both (
34
) and (
35
) are continuous by continuity of the extended landscape. Therefore almost surely, for all
k,\mathbf{y},s,p
the statements (
35
) and (
34
) are equivalent.
∎

Figure 7
:
An example of an ensemble
\mathcal{L}^{p,s}
where (
35
) holds with
k=3
. The left-hand side illustrates the Gelfand-Tsetlin pattern
\mathbf{z}
and the right-hand side illustrates the disjoint optimizer for
\mathcal{L}^{p,s}[((y_{1}-1)^{k},k)\to(\mathbf{y},1)]
.

To upper bound the dimension of
\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;W)
, by Lemma
4.5
and a discretization argument it will be enough to upper bound the probability that at a fixed
p
, the claim in Lemma
4.5
.B approximately holds. For fixed
p,s
, Theorem
2.15
.1 gives that
\mathcal{L}^{p,s}
is a rescaled parabolic Airy line ensemble. Moreover, Theorem
2.9
implies that on any compact set,
\mathcal{L}^{p,s}
has bounded Radon-Nikodym derivative with respect to a collection of independent Brownian bridges, suggesting we first try to analyze the probability that Lemma
4.5
.B holds for such bridges. This is the goal of Lemma
4.6
, Lemma
4.7
, and Corollary
4.8
. Note that all Brownian bridges in this section variance
2
.

For this next suite of lemmas, we say that
B=(B_{1},\dots,B_{k})\in\mathscr{C}^{k}([a,b])
is a
k
-tuple of independent Brownian bridges from
(a,\mathbf{x})
to
(b,\mathbf{y})
if each
B_{i}:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
is an independent Brownian bridge with
B_{i}(a)=x_{i},B_{i}(b)=y_{i}
. We write
\mathbb{P}_{a,b}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y};\cdot)
for the law of
B
. We define the set
\operatorname{NI}\subset\mathcal{C}^{k}([a,b])
to be the set of all functions
f
with
f_{1}>f_{2}>\dots f_{k}
.

Throughout the remainder of this section, for a Gelfand-Tsetlin pattern
\mathbf{w}\in\operatorname{GT}_{k}
we let
\#\mathbf{w}
be the number of distinct values in
\mathbf{w}
and set

\Theta(\mathbf{w})=\min\{|w_{i,j}-w_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}|:w_{i,j}\neq w_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}},(i,j),(i^{\prime},j)\in T_{k}\}.

Lemma 4.6
.

Fix
k\in\{2,3,4\},m\geq 1,\epsilon\in(0,1/100)
. Let
B
be a
k
-tuple of Brownian bridges from
(0,\mathbf{x})
to
(1,\mathbf{y})
for vectors
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in[-m,m]^{k}_{>}
, and let
\mathbf{w}
be a
(k-1)
-level Gelfand-Tsetlin pattern satisfying
\Theta(\mathbf{w})\geq\epsilon
and with
w_{i,j}\in(\epsilon,1-\epsilon)
for all
(i,j)\in T_{k-1}
.

For
\delta>0
, let
V_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{w},\delta)
be the event where

B\in\operatorname{NI},\quad|B_{i}(w_{i,j})-B_{i+1}(w_{i,j})|\leq\delta\quad\text{ for all }(i,j)\in T_{k}.

Then

\mathbb{P}(V_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{w},\delta))\leq e^{c_{\epsilon}m}\delta^{k(k-1)/2+2\#\mathbf{w}}.

The main step in the proof of Lemma
4.6
is the following basic bound on the probability that a collection of Brownian bridges
B
is nonintersecting (i.e.
B\in\operatorname{NI}
).

Lemma 4.7
.

Fix
t\in(0,1),k\in\{2,3,\dots\},m\geq 1
, and let
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in[-m,m]^{k}_{>}
. Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
, and let
G_{\delta}(\mathbf{x})
be the graph on
\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
where
(i,j)
is an edge exactly when
|x_{i}-x_{j}|<\delta
. Define

E_{\delta}(\mathbf{x})=\#\{(i,j)\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket^{2}:\text{$i<j$ and $i,j$ are in the same component of }G_{\delta}(\mathbf{x})\}.

Similarly define
G_{\delta}(\mathbf{y}),E_{\delta}(\mathbf{y}),
. Then

\mathbb{P}_{0,t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y};\operatorname{NI})\leq\delta^{E_{\delta}(\mathbf{x})+E_{\delta}(\mathbf{y})}\exp(cmk^{4}/t),

where
c>0
is an absolute constant.

Proof.

First, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{0,t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y};\operatorname{NI})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{>}}\mathbb{P}_{0,t/2}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z};\operatorname{NI})\mathbb{P}_{t/2,t}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y};\operatorname{NI})\rho(\mathbf{z})d\mathbf{z},

(36)

where

\rho(\mathbf{z})=\frac{1}{(\pi t)^{k/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\mathbf{z}-(\mathbf{y}+\mathbf{x})/2\|^{2}}{t}\right)

is the Gaussian density. Next, we estimate
\mathbb{P}_{0,t/2}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z};\operatorname{NI})
using the Karlin-McGregor formula. Consider a component
H
of
G_{\delta}(\mathbf{x})
and note that
H
is an integer interval
H=\llbracket a_{H},b_{H}\rrbracket
. Let
\mathbf{x}^{H}=(x_{a_{H}},\dots,x_{b_{H}})
. We can write

\mathbb{P}_{0,t/2}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z};\operatorname{NI})\leq\prod_{H}\mathbb{P}_{0,t/2}(\mathbf{x}^{H},\mathbf{z}^{H};\operatorname{NI}).

where the product is over components of
G_{\delta}(\mathbf{x})
. Next, let
\iota^{H}=(|H|-1,|H|-2,\dots,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{|H|}
and let
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{H}=(z_{a_{H}}-z_{b_{H}},z_{a_{H}+1}-z_{b_{H}},\dots,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{|H|}
. Since the spacing between the coordinates of
\delta\iota^{H}
is greater than the spacing for
\mathbf{x}^{H}
by the definition of
G_{\delta}(\mathbf{x})
, and Brownian bridge law commutes with linear shifts, we have

\mathbb{P}_{0,t/2}(\mathbf{x}^{H},\mathbf{z}^{H};\operatorname{NI})\leq\mathbb{P}_{0,t/2}(\delta\iota^{H},\mathbf{z}^{H};\operatorname{NI})=\mathbb{P}_{0,t/2}(\delta\iota^{H},\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{H};\operatorname{NI}).

We focus on bounding the latter probability. By the Karlin-McGregor formula (see p. 48 in
[
Ham22
]
for the form used below) we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{0,t/2}(\delta\iota_{H},\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{H};\operatorname{NI})

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(-t^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{|H|}\delta\iota^{H}_{i}\tilde{z}^{H}_{i}\Big)\det\Big(e^{\delta\iota^{H}_{i}\tilde{z}^{H}_{j}/t}\Big)_{1\leq i,j\leq|H|}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq|H|}(e^{\delta\tilde{z}^{H}_{j}/t}-e^{\delta\tilde{z}^{H}_{i}/t})

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{|H|(|H|-1)/2}\exp\left(\frac{c|H|(|H|-1)\tilde{z}^{H}_{1}}{t}\right)

for some constant
c>0
.
In the first inequality, we have used that the determinant is a Vandermonde by the form of
\iota_{H}
, and that
\tilde{z}_{i}\geq 0
for all
i
to bound the first term by
1
. Now, using that
\sum_{H}|H|(|H|-1)/2=E_{\delta}(\mathbf{x})
and that
\tilde{z}^{H}_{1}\leq z_{1}-z_{k}
for any
H
, we get that

\mathbb{P}_{0,t/2}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z};\operatorname{NI})\leq\delta^{E_{\delta}(\mathbf{x})}\exp\left(\frac{ck^{2}(z_{1}-z_{k})}{t}\right).

A similar bound on
\mathbb{P}_{t/2,t}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y};\operatorname{NI})
implies that (
36
) is bounded above by

\delta^{E_{\delta}(\mathbf{x})+E_{\delta}(\mathbf{x})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{>}}e^{\frac{2ck^{2}|z_{1}-z_{k}|}{t}}\rho(\mathbf{z})dz.

The integral above converges, and since
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in[-m,m]^{k}_{>}
, it is bounded above by
\exp(c^{\prime}mk^{4}/t)
for some
c^{\prime}>0
, giving the desired result.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.6
.

To simplify notation, we write
\ell=\#\mathbf{w}
throughout.
First, let
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}_{<}
denote the unique vector whose coordinates are equal to the entries of
\mathbf{w}
and let
v_{0}=0,v_{\ell+1}=1
.
Letting
\mathbf{a}^{0}:=\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a}^{\ell+1}:=\mathbf{y},
we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(V_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{w},\delta))=\int_{\Delta}\prod_{i=0}^{\ell}\mathbb{P}_{v_{i},v_{i+1}}(\mathbf{a}^{i},\mathbf{a}^{i+1};\operatorname{NI})\rho(\mathbf{a}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}^{\ell})d\mathbf{a}^{1}\cdots d\mathbf{a}^{\ell}.

(37)

where

\rho(\mathbf{a}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}^{\ell})=\sqrt{4\pi}\exp\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{a}^{0}-\mathbf{a}^{\ell+1}\|^{2}}{4}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi(v_{i+1}-v_{i})}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\mathbf{a}^{i}-\mathbf{a}^{i+1}\|^{2}}{4[v_{i}-v_{i+1}]}\right)

is the Gaussian density.
Here the region
\Delta=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\Delta_{i}
, and
\Delta_{i}
is the set of points
\mathbf{a}^{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{>}
where
a^{i}_{i^{\prime}}-a^{i}_{i^{\prime}+1}\leq\delta
whenever
v_{i}=w_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
for some
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in T_{k-1}
. Next we use Lemma
4.7
to estimate
\mathbb{P}_{v_{i},v_{i+1}}(\mathbf{a}^{i},\mathbf{a}^{i+1};\operatorname{NI})
. First,
for every
i\in\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket
, let

n_{i}=\#\{i^{\prime}:\text{ $v_{i}=w_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}$ for some $j^{\prime}$ with $(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in T_{k-1}$}\}.

Also let
n_{0}=n_{\ell+1}=0
.
Observe that if
\mathbf{a}^{i}\in\Delta_{i}
, then with notation as in Lemma
4.7
we have that
E_{\delta}(\mathbf{a}^{i})\geq n_{i}
, and so by Lemma
4.7
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{v_{i},v_{i+1}}(\mathbf{a}^{i},\mathbf{a}^{i+1};\operatorname{NI})\leq\delta^{n_{i}+n_{i+1}}\exp(ck^{4}\|\mathbf{a}\|_{\infty}/(v_{i+1}-v_{i}))\leq\delta^{n_{i}+n_{i+1}}\exp(c_{\epsilon}\|\mathbf{a}\|_{\infty}).

Here
\|\mathbf{a}\|_{\infty}=\|(\mathbf{a}^{0},\dots,\mathbf{a}^{\ell+1})\|_{\infty}
, and the second inequality follows since
k\in\{2,3,4\}
and
v_{i}-v_{i+1}\geq\epsilon
. Plugging this back into (
37
) we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(V_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{w},\delta))\leq\delta^{2\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}n_{i}}\int_{\Delta}\exp((\ell+1)c_{\epsilon}\|\mathbf{a}\|_{\infty})\rho(\mathbf{a}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}^{\ell})d\mathbf{a}^{1}\cdots d\mathbf{a}^{\ell}

(38)

Now,

\displaystyle\int_{\Delta}\exp

\displaystyle((\ell+1)c_{\epsilon}\|\mathbf{a}\|_{\infty})\rho(\mathbf{a}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}^{\ell})d\mathbf{a}^{1}\cdots d\mathbf{a}^{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq e^{c_{\epsilon}m}\int_{\Delta}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\mathbf{a}^{i}\|^{2}}{c_{\epsilon}}\right)d\mathbf{a}^{1}\cdots d\mathbf{a}^{\ell}

\displaystyle=e^{c_{\epsilon}m}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\int_{\Delta_{i}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\mathbf{a}^{i}\|^{2}}{c_{\epsilon}}\right)d\mathbf{a}^{i}\leq e^{c_{\epsilon}m}\delta^{\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}},

where in the final line we have used the definition of
\Delta_{i}
. Together with (
38
) we get that

\mathbb{P}(V_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{w},\delta))\leq e^{c_{\epsilon}m}\delta^{3\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}n_{i}}.

To complete the proof, we just need to prove the inequality
k(k-1)/2+2\ell\leq 3\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}n_{i}
for any choice of the Gelfand-Tsetlin pattern
\mathbf{w}
. This can be checked by routine casework since we have
k\in\llbracket 2,3,4\rrbracket
so
\mathbf{w}
has at most
k(k-1)/2
points. Note that this is an equality if all the
w_{i}
are distinct.
∎

For this next corollary, we also build in a statement about trivial Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns. For this, we let
T_{0}=\emptyset,\operatorname{GT}_{0}=\{\emptyset\}
be the trivial space containing only the empty set. We set
\Theta(\emptyset)=\infty,\#\emptyset=0
.

Corollary 4.8
.

Fix
k\in\{1,2,3,4\},m\geq 1,\epsilon\in(0,1/100)
. Consider the following data:

•

A
k
-tuple
B
of Brownian bridges from
(0,\mathbf{x})
to
(1,\mathbf{y})
for vectors
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in[-m,m]^{k}_{>}
.

•

A vector
\mathbf{z}\in(\epsilon,1-\epsilon)^{k}_{<}
such that
z_{i}-z_{i-1}>\epsilon
for all
i\in\llbracket 2,k\rrbracket
, and a vector
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

•

A
(k-1)
-level Gelfand-Tsetlin pattern
\mathbf{w}\in\operatorname{GT}_{k-1}(\mathbf{z})
with
\Theta(\mathbf{w})\geq\epsilon
.

For
\delta\in(0,1)
, let
U_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z},\alpha,\delta)
be the event where

|B_{1}(z_{j})-\alpha_{j}|\leq\delta,\forall j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket;\qquad B\in\operatorname{NI};\qquad|B_{i}(w_{i,j})-B_{i+1}(w_{i,j})|\leq\delta,\forall(i,j)\in T_{k-1}.

Then

\mathbb{P}(U_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z},\alpha,\delta))\leq e^{c_{\epsilon}m}\delta^{k(k+1)/2+2\#\mathbf{w}}

where
c_{\epsilon}>0
is an
\epsilon
-dependent constant.

Proof.

In the case
k=1
, the set
U_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z},\alpha,\delta)
is simply the set where
|B_{1}(z_{1})-\alpha_{1}|\leq\delta
, which has probability at most
c_{\epsilon}\delta
by an elementary computation, yielding the corollary. For the remainder of the corollary, we assume
k\in\{2,3,4\}
.

Let
U^{*}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{z},\alpha,\delta)
be the event where

|B_{1}(z_{j})-\alpha_{j}|\leq\delta
for all
j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket.

With notation as in Lemma
4.6
, we can write

U_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z},\alpha,\delta)=U^{*}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{z},\alpha,\delta)\cap V_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{w},\delta).

Therefore by that lemma, to prove the corollary it suffices to show that

\mathbb{P}(U^{*}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{z},\alpha,\delta)\mid V_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{w},\delta))\leq c_{\epsilon}\delta^{k}.

The event
V_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{w},\delta)
is measurable with respect to the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}
generated by
W_{1}:=B_{1}-B_{2},W_{2}:=B_{2}-B_{3},\dots,W_{k-1}:=B_{k-1}-B_{k}
. On the other hand, the sum
W_{k}=B_{1}+\dots+B_{k}
is a Brownian bridge of variance
2k
, independent of
\mathcal{F}
, and we can write
U^{*}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{z},\alpha,\delta)
in terms of the bridges
W_{i},i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
as

|W_{k}(z_{j})+\beta_{j}|\leq k\delta\quad\text{ for all }j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,

where
\beta_{j}=(k-1)W_{1}(z_{j})+(k-2)W_{2}(z_{j})+\dots+W_{k-1}(z_{j})-k\alpha_{j}.
Therefore

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(U^{*}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{z},\alpha,\delta)\mid V_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{w},\delta))

\displaystyle\leq\sup\mathbb{P}(U^{*}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{z},\alpha,\delta)\mid\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}(|W_{k}(z_{j})+\beta_{j}|\leq k\delta\quad\text{ for all }j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket)\leq c_{\epsilon}\delta^{k},

where the final bound follows since
\epsilon<z_{1}
,
1-\epsilon<z_{k}
, and
z_{i}-z_{i-1}>\epsilon
for all
i\in\llbracket 2,k\rrbracket
. This gives the result.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.4
.

Step 1: Reduction to pinches in the Airy line ensemble.
For
p=(z,r)
and
t<r<s
we define random functions
\mathcal{L}^{p,s},\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{p,t}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R})
as follows: write

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{i}(x)=\mathcal{L}(p^{k};x^{k},s)\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{k}\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{p,t}_{i}(x)=\mathcal{L}(x^{k},t;p^{k}).

By Lemma
4.5
and time-reversal symmetry of
\mathcal{L}
, almost surely
\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;W)
equals the set of all
(p;q)
for which there exists
(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\in W
such that the following claims hold:

1.

\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{1}(x_{i})=a_{i}
for all
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
and there exists
\mathbf{z}\in\operatorname{GT}_{k-1}(\mathbf{x})
with
\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{i}(z_{i,j})=\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{i+1}(z_{i,j})
for all
(i,j)\in T_{k-1}
.

2.

\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{q,t}_{1}(y_{i})=b_{i}
for all
i\in\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket
and there exists
\mathbf{w}\in\operatorname{GT}_{k-1}(\mathbf{y})
with
\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{q,t}_{i}(w_{i,j})=\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{q,t}_{i+1}(w_{i,j})
for all
(i,j)\in T_{\ell-1}
.

We can write

\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;W)=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigcup_{m_{1}=0}^{k(k-1)/2}\bigcup_{m_{2}=0}^{\ell(\ell-1)/2}\operatorname{Star}_{n,m_{1},m_{2}}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;W)

where
(p;q)\in\operatorname{Star}_{n,m_{1},m_{2}}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;W)
if

•

p\in K_{n}:=[-n,n]\times[s-n,s-1/n]
and
q\in K_{n}^{\prime}:=[-n,n]\times[t+1/n,t+n]
.

•

Points
1
and
2
above hold for some
\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w}
with
\Theta(\mathbf{z})>1/n,\#\mathbf{z}=m_{1},\Theta(\mathbf{w})>1/n,\#\mathbf{w}=m_{2}
.

•

(\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{1}(x_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{1}(x_{k}),\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{1}^{q,t}(y_{1}),\dots,\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{1}^{q,t}(y_{\ell}))\in W\cap[-n,n]^{k}
.

By countable stability of Hausdorff dimension, it is enough to prove the proposition for

\dim_{1:2:3}[\operatorname{Star}_{n,m_{1},m_{2}}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;W)]

(39)

for all fixed
n,m_{1},m_{2}
.

Step 2: Discretization.
For a point
u=(p;q)=(x^{\prime},s^{\prime};y^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, define the box

\Delta_{\epsilon}(u)=[x^{\prime}-\epsilon^{2},x^{\prime}+\epsilon^{2}]\times[s^{\prime}-\epsilon^{3},s^{\prime}+\epsilon^{3}]\times[y^{\prime}-\epsilon^{2},y^{\prime}+\epsilon^{2}]\times[t^{\prime}-\epsilon^{3},t^{\prime}+\epsilon^{3}],

and for a set
S
, let
\Delta_{\epsilon}(S)=\bigcup_{u\in S}\Delta_{\epsilon}(u)
.
Consider the grid

\mathfrak{G}_{n,\epsilon}:=(\epsilon^{3}\mathbb{Z}\times\epsilon^{2}\mathbb{Z})^{2}\cap(K_{n+1}\times K_{n+1}^{\prime})

and set

S_{n,\epsilon}:=\{(p;q)\in\mathfrak{G}_{n,\epsilon}:\Delta_{\epsilon}(p;q)\cap\operatorname{Star}_{n,m_{1},m_{2}}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t;W)\neq\emptyset\}.

To prove the proposition it is enough to show that

|S_{n,\epsilon}|=o(\epsilon^{-d(k,\ell,W)-\eta})

(40)

for every
\eta>0
, almost surely as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
.

We require one more discretization step before analyzing the probability of (
40
). Let
\mathfrak{D}_{n,\epsilon}
be the set of all
(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w})\in\operatorname{GT}_{k-1}(\mathbf{x})\times\operatorname{GT}_{k-1}(\mathbf{y})
such that
z_{i,j},w_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\in\epsilon^{2}\mathbb{Z}
for all choices of
(i,j)\in T_{k-1},(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in T_{\ell-1}
.
Also, for all
\epsilon>0
we can cover
W\cap[-n,n]^{k}
by
c_{n}\epsilon^{-\dim W}
balls of radius
\epsilon
, where
c_{n}>0
is an
n
-dependent constant. Let
\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}\subset[-n-1,n+1]^{3}
be the set of centres of these balls.

Now, for
\delta>0
and
(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w},\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{D}_{n,\epsilon}\times\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}
let
\mathcal{X}_{n,\epsilon,\delta}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w},\mathbf{a},\mathcal{L}^{p,s},\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\hat{q},-t})
be the event where

\displaystyle|\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{i}(z_{i,j})-\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{i+1}(z_{i,j})|\leq\epsilon^{1-\delta},\qquad|\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{q,t}_{i}(w_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}})-\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{q,t}_{i+1}(w_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}})|\leq\epsilon^{1-\delta}

(41)

for all
(i,j)\in T_{k-1},(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in T_{\ell-1}
, and

\|(\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{1}(x_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{L}^{p,s}_{1}(x_{k}),\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{1}^{q,t}(y_{1}),\dots,\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{1}^{q,t}(y_{\ell}))-\mathbf{a}\|_{2}\leq\epsilon^{1-\delta}.

By the modulus of continuity for the extended landscape
\mathcal{L}
(Lemma
2.14
), letting

S^{*}_{n,\epsilon,\delta}:=\{(p;q)\in\mathfrak{G}_{n,\epsilon}:\bigcup_{(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w},\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{D}_{n,\epsilon}\times\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}}\mathcal{X}_{n,\epsilon,\delta}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w},\mathbf{a},\mathcal{L}^{p,s},\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{q,-t})\text{ holds}\}

we have that

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{P}(S_{n,\epsilon}\subset S^{*}_{n,\epsilon,\delta})=1

(42)

for every fixed
\delta>0
. Hence to prove (
40
) it suffices to control
|S^{*}_{n,\epsilon,\delta}|
.

Step 3: Bounding
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}_{n,\epsilon,\delta}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w},\mathbf{a},\mathcal{L}^{p,s},\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\hat{q},-t}))
.
By Theorem
2.15
.1, Theorem
2.9
, and the symmetries of
\mathcal{L}
(Lemma
2.2
), for any fixed
(p;q)\in\mathfrak{G}_{n,\epsilon}
the law
\mu_{p,q}
of the process

(\mathcal{L}^{p,s}|_{\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times[-x_{1}-1,x_{k}+1]},\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{q,t}|_{\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times[-y_{1}-1,y_{\ell}+1]})

is absolutely continuous with respect to the law
\nu_{p,q}
of
(B,B^{\prime})
, where

•

B
is a
k
-tuple of independent Brownian bridges from
(x_{1}-1,\mathbf{u})
to
(x_{k}+1,\mathbf{u}^{\prime})
where
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}(p,s),\mathbf{u}^{\prime}=\mathbf{u}^{\prime}(p,s)
are random endpoints, independent of
B
.

•

B^{\prime}
is an
\ell
-tuple of independent Brownian bridges from
(x_{1}-1,\mathbf{v})
to
(x_{k}+1,\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
where
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}(q,t),\mathbf{v}^{\prime}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}(q,t)
are random endpoints, independent of
B^{\prime}
.

•

B
and
B^{\prime}
are independent.

•

There exists
c^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},n}>0
such that for all
a>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\|(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})\|_{\infty}>a)\leq c^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},n}e^{-a^{3/2}}.

Moreover, by Theorem
2.9
.1, the Radon-Nikodym derivative
\tfrac{d\mu_{p,q}}{d\nu_{p,q}}
is bounded above by a constant
c_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},n}>0
. Since
all points in
\mathfrak{G}_{n,\epsilon},\epsilon>0
are contained in the compact set
K_{n+1}\times K_{n+1}^{\prime}
we can take this constant to be independent of
p,q,\epsilon
.
Finally, recall that almost surely we have

\mathcal{L}^{p,s}|_{\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times[-x_{1}-1,x_{k}+1]}\in\operatorname{NI},\qquad\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{q,t}|_{\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times[-y_{1}-1,y_{\ell}+1]}\in\operatorname{NI}

for any fixed
p,q
. Therefore for
(p;q)\in\mathfrak{G}_{n,\epsilon}
and
(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w},\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{D}_{n,\epsilon}\times\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}_{n,\epsilon,\delta}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w},\mathbf{a},\mathcal{L}^{p,s},\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\hat{q},-t}))\leq c_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},n}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}_{n,\epsilon,\delta}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w},\mathbf{a},B,B^{\prime})\cap\{B\in\operatorname{NI},B^{\prime}\in\operatorname{NI}\}).

Since
B,B^{\prime}
are independent, we can bound the right-hand side above by Corollary
4.8
. With notation as in Corollary
4.8
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}_{n,\epsilon,\delta}

\displaystyle(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w},\mathbf{a},B,B^{\prime})\cap\{B\in\operatorname{NI},B^{\prime}\in\operatorname{NI}\})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{P}(U_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{x},(a_{1},\dots,a_{k}),\epsilon^{1-\delta}))\mathbb{P}(U_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{y},(a_{k+1},\dots,a_{k+\ell}),\epsilon^{1-\delta}))]

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{(1-\delta)[k(k+1)/2+\ell(\ell+1)/2+2m_{1}+2m_{2}]}\mathbb{E}e^{c_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},n}\|(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})\|_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},n}\epsilon^{(1-\delta)[k(k+1)/2+\ell(\ell+1)/2+2m_{1}+2m_{2}]}

In the above calculation, in the expectation the only randomness involves the endpoints
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
. The first inequality is by Corollary
4.8
and the second inequality is by the fourth bullet above.
Therefore by a union bound over the
c_{n}\epsilon^{-10}
points in
\mathfrak{G}_{n,\epsilon}
, the
c_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},n}\epsilon^{-2(m_{1}+m_{2})}
points in
\mathfrak{D}_{n,\epsilon}
, and the
c_{n}\epsilon^{-\dim W}
points in
\mathfrak{E}_{n,\epsilon}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|S^{*}_{n,\epsilon,\delta}|\leq

\displaystyle c^{\prime\prime}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},n}\epsilon^{-10-\dim W+k(k+1)/2+\ell(\ell+1)/2-\delta[k(k+1)/2+\ell(\ell+1)/2+2m_{1}+2m_{2}]}

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle c^{\prime\prime}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},n}\epsilon^{-d(k,\ell,W)-1000\delta}.

Taking
\delta>0
small enough and applying Markov’s inequality and (
42
) yields (
40
) for any fixed
\eta>0
, as desired.
∎

4.3
Upper bound on set size for
3
-geodesic stars

We finish this section with a lemma that gives a converse to Lemma
4.2
by showing that certain sets of geodesic stars with weight in particular sets are finite.
This lemma can be used to refine Proposition
4.4
in the
d(k,\ell,W)=0
case when
k=\ell=3
, and
W
is a slide-invariant affine set of dimension
2
. For this lemma, define
L_{3}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
by
L_{3}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=(x_{1}-x_{2},x_{3}-x_{2})
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Almost surely, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{<},t\in\mathbb{R},
and
m\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
we have

\#\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},t;L_{3}^{-1}(m))\leq 135.

The bounds here is not optimal; the key point is just that it is finite. The proof of Lemma
4.9
is topological.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we write
X=\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},t;L_{3}^{-1}(m))
.
We start with two claims.

Claim 1:
Suppose that
p_{1}=(y_{1},s_{1}),p_{2}=(y_{2},s_{2})\in X
and that
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
are two geodesics starting at points
p_{i(1)},p_{i(2)}
and ending at points
(x_{i^{\prime}(1)},t),(x_{i^{\prime}(2)},t)
where
i(1),i(2)\in\{1,2\}
and
i^{\prime}(1),i^{\prime}(2)\in\{1,2,3\}
. Suppose additionally that there exists a time
r\in[s_{1}\vee s_{2},t)
such that
\pi_{1}(r)=\pi_{2}(r)
.
Then the paths

\pi_{1}|_{[s_{i(1)},r]}\oplus\pi_{2}|_{[r,t]}\qquad\;\text{and}\;\qquad\pi_{2}|_{[s_{i(2)},r]}\oplus\pi_{1}|_{[r,t]}

(43)

are geodesics.

Proof:
Observe that

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}(p_{i(1)};x_{i(1)},t)&+\mathcal{L}(p_{i(2)};x_{i(2)},t)=\|\pi_{1}\|_{\mathcal{L}}+\|\pi_{2}\|_{\mathcal{L}}\\
&=\|\pi_{1}|_{[s_{i(1)},r]}\oplus\pi_{2}|_{[r,t]}\|_{\mathcal{L}}+\|\pi_{2}|_{[s_{i(2)},r]}\oplus\pi_{1}|_{[r,t]}\|_{\mathcal{L}}.\end{split}

(44)

Moreover, we have

\mathcal{L}(p_{i(1)};x_{i^{\prime}(1)},t)+\mathcal{L}(p_{i(2)};x_{i^{\prime}(2)},t)=\mathcal{L}(p_{i(2)};x_{i^{\prime}(1)},t)+\mathcal{L}(p_{i^{\prime}(1)};x_{i(2)},t).

(45)

Indeed, this is a tautology if either
i(1)=i(2)
or
i^{\prime}(1)=i^{\prime}(2)
. If both
i(1)\neq i(2)
and
i^{\prime}(1)\neq i^{\prime}(2)
then this holds since
p_{1},p_{2}\in X
. Combining (
44
) with (
45
) gives that the paths in (
43
) are geodesics.

\blacksquare

Claim 2:
Suppose that
|X|\geq 136
. Then we can find three points

\{p_{i}=(y_{i},s_{i}),i=1,2,3\}\subset X

with either
s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq s_{3}
or
s_{1}\geq s_{2}\geq s_{3}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\{(x_{1},x_{2}),(x_{2},x_{3})\}
, and geodesic
2
-stars
\tau^{1},\tau^{2},\tau^{3}
from
p_{i}
to
(\tilde{\mathbf{x}},t)
such that

•

\tau^{1}=\tau^{2}=\tau^{3}
on some interval
[t-\delta,t]
.

•

\tau^{i}_{2}
and
\tau^{j}_{1}
are not disjoint for all
i<j\in\llbracket 1,3\rrbracket
.

Proof:
Suppose that we can find points
p_{1}=(y_{1},s_{1}),\dots,p_{136}=(y_{136},s_{136})\in X
and let
\pi^{i},i\in\llbracket 1,136\rrbracket
be geodesic
3
-stars from
p_{i}
to
(\mathbf{x},t)
. We can further assume that there exists
\delta>0
such that for every
i\in\llbracket 1,136\rrbracket
, on
[t-\delta,t]
, each path
\pi^{i}_{j}
follows a leftmost geodesic, since by the landscape shape theorem (Theorem
2.5
), the leftmost geodesics from
(\pi^{i}_{j}(t-\delta),t-\delta)
to
(x_{j},t)
,
j=1,2,3
are necessarily disjoint for small enough
\delta
.

We define an equivalence relation on the set
\llbracket 1,136\rrbracket
by saying that
i\sim_{1}j
if
\pi^{i}_{1}|_{[t-\epsilon,t]}=\pi^{j}_{1}|_{[t-\epsilon,t]}
for some
\epsilon>0
. This splits
\llbracket 1,136\rrbracket
into at most
3
equivalence classes. Indeed, if
\pi^{i},\pi^{j}
are in different equivalence classes then
\pi^{i}_{1},\pi^{j}_{1}
cannot intersect on the interval
[t-\delta,t)
, since they are both leftmost geodesics on this interval. Therefore if there were at least four equivalence classes, we could construct a
4
-geodesic star to
(x_{1},t)
, contradicting Theorem
1.5
. Similarly defining
\sim_{2},\sim_{3}
and letting
i\sim j
if
i\sim_{1}j,i\sim_{2}j,i\sim_{3}j
, the relation
\sim
splits
\llbracket 1,136\rrbracket
into at most
27
equivalence classes. One of these must have at least
6
elements. After relabeling, we may assume
1,\dots,6
belong to this class, and assume that
p_{1},\dots,p_{6}
are listed in order of increasing time coordinate (breaking ties arbitrarily).

For every pair
i<j\in\llbracket 1,6\rrbracket
, by planarity there must exist a time
s_{i,j}
with
\pi^{i}_{2}(s_{i,j})\in\{\pi^{j}_{1}(s_{i,j}),\pi^{j}_{3}(s_{i,j})\}
. Therefore (i.e. since the Ramsey number
R(3,3)=6
) we can find
m\in\{1,3\}
and a subset
S\subset\llbracket 1,6\rrbracket
with
|S|=3
such that if
i<j\in S
then
\pi^{i}_{2}(s_{i,j})=\pi^{j}_{m}(s_{i,j})
. Without loss of generality,
S=\llbracket 1,3\rrbracket
, Then setting
\tau^{i}=(\pi^{i}_{1},\pi^{i}_{2})
and
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2})
if
m=1
or
\tau^{i}=(\pi^{i}_{2},\pi^{i}_{3})
and
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{2},x_{3})
if
m=3
gives the desired construction.

\blacksquare

We can use the two claims along with a planarity argument to prove the existence of geodesic bubbles if
|X|\geq 136
, contradicting Lemma
3.3
. The details are as follows.

Proof of the lemma given the claims.
Let all notation be as in Claim 2. We assume
s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq s_{3}
; the case when
s_{1}\geq s_{2}\geq s_{3}
follows a symmetric argument. We first show that the ordering of the time coordinates
s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq s_{3}
implies that

\tau^{1}|_{[s_{2},t]}\leq\tau^{2},\qquad\tau^{2}|_{[s_{3},t]}\leq\tau^{3}.

(46)

We just show that
\tau^{1}|_{[s_{2},t]}\leq\tau^{2}
as the second assertion in (
46
) follows by an identical argument. Suppose first that
s^{\prime}\in[s_{2},t)
is such that
\tau^{1}_{1}(s^{\prime})=\tau^{2}_{1}(s^{\prime})
. Note that
\bar{\tau}^{1}_{1}(s^{\prime})
is an interior point on a geodesic since
p_{1}=\bar{\tau}^{1}_{1}(s_{1})
cannot lie on
\tau^{2}_{1}
by construction. Now, by the first bullet point in Claim 2, we can find
s^{\prime\prime}\in(s^{\prime},t)
with
\tau^{1}_{1}(s^{\prime\prime})=\tau^{2}_{1}(s^{\prime\prime})
. The point
\bar{\tau}^{1}_{1}(s^{\prime\prime})
is also in the interior of a geodesic and so by Lemma
3.3
.3 (no geodesic bubbles) we have
\tau^{1}_{1}|_{[s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime}]}=\tau^{2}_{1}|_{[s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime}]}
. Therefore the set where
\tau^{1}_{1}=\tau^{2}_{1}
is a closed interval containing
t
. By a similar argument, the set where
\tau^{1}_{2}=\tau^{2}_{2}
is also a closed interval containing
t
.

Therefore by continuity, either
\tau^{1}_{1}|_{[s_{2},t]}\leq\tau^{2}_{1}
or else
\tau^{1}_{1}|_{[s_{2},t]}\geq\tau^{2}_{1}
and
\tau^{1}_{1}(s_{2})>\tau^{2}_{1}(s_{2})
. Similarly, either
\tau^{1}_{2}|_{[s_{2},t]}\leq\tau^{2}_{2}
or else
\tau^{1}_{2}|_{[s_{2},t]}\geq\tau^{2}_{2}
and
\tau^{1}_{2}(s_{2})>\tau^{2}_{2}(s_{2})
.
If either
\tau^{1}_{1}|_{[s_{2},t]}\geq\tau^{2}_{1}
and
\tau^{1}_{1}(s_{2})>\tau^{2}_{1}(s_{2})
, or
\tau^{1}_{2}|_{[s_{2},t]}\geq\tau^{2}_{2}
and
\tau^{1}_{2}(s_{2})>\tau^{2}_{2}(s_{2})
, then necessarily
\tau^{1}_{2}(r)>\tau^{2}_{1}(r)
for all
r\in[s_{2},t]
by the ordering and disjointness between
\tau^{1}_{1},\tau^{1}_{2}
in the former case and between
\tau^{2}_{1},\tau^{2}_{2}
in the latter. This contradicts the second bullet point in Claim 2, completing the proof of (
46
).

Now, for
i<j
define

\displaystyle X^{i,j}=\sup O(\tau^{i}_{2},\tau^{j}_{1}),\qquad C_{1}^{i,j}=\inf O(\tau^{i}_{1},\tau^{j}_{1}),\qquad C_{2}^{i,j}=\inf O(\tau^{i}_{2},\tau^{j}_{2}).

The ‘X’ coordinates are crossing times for paths to different endpoints are defined for
i<j
by the second bullet in Claim 2, whereas the ‘
C
’ coordinates are coalescence times for paths to the same endpoint. Note that points of the form

\bar{\tau}^{i}_{2}(X^{i,j}),\qquad\bar{\tau}^{i}_{k}(C_{k}^{i,j})

(47)

are always interior points on geodesics. Indeed, neither of these points equal
p_{i}
because
p_{i}\neq p_{j}
and
s_{i}\leq s_{j}
, we have
X^{i,j}<t
by continuity, and
C_{k}^{i,j}\leq t-\delta
by the first bullet point in Claim
2
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 8
:
Geodesic bubbles in the proof of Lemma
4.9
. Figure (A) covers the case when
X^{1,3}<C^{2,3}_{1}
and figure (B) covers the case when
X^{1,3}\geq C^{2,3}_{1}
.

First suppose that
X^{1,3}<C^{2,3}_{1}
, see Figure
8
(A).
Now,
\tau^{2}_{1}\leq\tau^{3}_{1}
on
[s_{3},t]
by (
46
) and
\tau^{3}_{1}(r)<\tau^{1}_{2}(r)
for
r\in(X^{1,3},t]
. Therefore
\tau^{2}_{1}(r)<\tau^{1}_{2}(r)
for
r\in(X^{1,3},t]
and so
X^{1,2}\leq X^{1,3}
. Moreover, we cannot have
X^{1,2}=X^{1,3}
since this would imply
C^{2,3}_{1}\leq X^{1,3}
and so
X^{1,2}<X^{1,3}
.
Therefore by Claim 2,

\tau^{1}_{2}|_{[X^{1,2},X^{1,3}]}\oplus\tau^{3}_{1}|_{[X^{1,3},C^{2,3}_{1}]}\qquad\;\text{and}\;\qquad\tau^{2}_{1}|_{[X^{1,2},C^{2,3}_{1}]}

are both geodesics, which are distinct since
\tau^{1}_{2}(r)>\tau^{2}_{1}(r)
for
r>X^{1,2}
. These geodesic go between the same interior geodesic points of the form in (
47
). This creates a geodesic bubble, contradicting Lemma
3.3
.3.

Next, suppose that
X^{1,3}\geq C^{2,3}_{1}
, see Figure
8
(B). Note that
s_{2}<C^{2,3}_{1}
since
p_{2}\neq p_{3}
. In this case, at time
X^{1,3}>s_{2}
the paths
\tau^{3}_{1},\tau^{2}_{1}
, and
\tau^{1}_{2}
all overlap, and so
X^{1,3}<C^{1,2}_{2}
since otherwise

\tau^{2}_{2}(X^{1,3})=\tau^{2}_{1}(X^{1,3})

which would contradict the disjointness of
\tau^{2}_{1},\tau^{2}_{2}
on
(s_{2},t]
.
Also,
X^{2,3}<C^{2,3}_{1}
since otherwise

\tau^{2}_{2}(X^{2,3})=\tau^{3}_{1}(X^{2,3})=\tau^{2}_{1}(X^{2,3}),

again contradicting disjointness of
\tau^{2}_{1},\tau^{2}_{2}
on
(s_{2},t]
. Therefore

X^{2,3}<C^{2,3}_{1}\leq X^{1,3}<C^{1,2}_{2}

and so the paths

\tau^{2}_{2}|_{[X^{2,3},C^{1,2}_{2}]}\qquad\;\text{and}\;\qquad\tau^{3}_{1}|_{[X^{2,3},X^{1,3}]}\oplus\tau^{1}_{2}|_{[X^{1,3},C^{1,2}_{2}]}

form two geodesics between interior geodesic points. These geodesics are distinct by the definition of
C^{1,2}_{2}
, contradicting Lemma
3.3
.3.
∎

5
There are at most
27
networks

In this section we prove the ‘only if’ direction in Theorem
1.1
, as the only remaining part of the proof is closely related to the proof of Proposition
4.4
. Indeed, in Section
3
we showed that the network graph
G(p;q)
of any pair
(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
satisfies points 1-3 of Theorem
1.1
. Theorem
1.5
shows that
G(p;q)
satisfies point
5
, so it remains to show point
4
: namely, that all interior vertices of
G(p;q)=(V,E)
have total degree equal to
3
. By construction, each of these vertices has total degree at least
3
, so to prove point
3
we need to show that no such vertex can have degree
4
or more. There are two possibilities we need to rule out:

1.

A vertex
v\in V\setminus\{p,q\}
has in-degree and out-degree at least
2
. This is ruled out by Lemma
5.1
.

2.

A vertex
v\in V\setminus\{p,q\}
has out-degree at least
3
(or in-degree at least
3
). This is ruled out by Lemma
5.5
and time-reversal symmetry of
\mathcal{L}
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Almost surely, the following holds. Consider any geodesics
\gamma,\pi
. Then the overlap
O(\gamma,\pi)
is not a single point.

We prove Lemma
5.1
by contradiction. The first step is to show that the negation of Lemma
5.1
implies the existence of rational ‘figure eights’.

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose that Lemma
5.1
fails. Then with positive probability, we can find
u=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
with
s,t
rational such that the graph
\Gamma(\gamma^{R}(u);\gamma^{L}(u))
contains a vertex of degree
4
.

In the statement above recall that
\gamma^{R}(p;q),\gamma^{L}(p;q)
denote the rightmost and leftmost geodesics from
p
to
q
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma:[s_{1},t_{1}]\to\mathbb{R},\pi:[s_{2},t_{2}]\to\mathbb{R}
. If
O(\gamma,\pi)
consists of a single point
r
, then
r\in(s,t)\subset(s_{1},t_{1})\cap(s_{2},t_{2})
for a rational interval
(s,t)
. Without loss of generality,
x^{-}:=\gamma(s)<x^{+}:=\pi(s)
, and we can assume that
\gamma|_{(s,r)}
is the leftmost geodesic from
(x^{-},s)
to
\bar{\gamma}(r)
and
\pi|_{(s,r)}
is the rightmost geodesic from
(x^{+},s)
to
\bar{\gamma}(r)
.
Next, define

z^{+}=\inf\{z\in[x^{-},x^{+}]:O(\gamma^{R}(z,s;\bar{\gamma}(r)),\gamma|_{(s,r)})=\emptyset\}.

For every fixed
r^{\prime}\in[r,s]
, the function
z\mapsto\gamma^{R}(z,s;\bar{\gamma}(r))(r^{\prime})
is right-continuous by the monotonicity in Lemma
2.7
.3 and since limits of geodesics are geodesics. Therefore the function
z\mapsto\gamma^{R}(z,s;\bar{\gamma}(r))
is continuous in the overlap topology by Proposition
3.5
.2, so
O(\gamma^{R}(z^{+},s;\bar{\gamma}(r)),\gamma|_{(s,r)})=\emptyset
. Next, let

z^{-}=\sup\{x\in[x^{-},z^{+}]:O(\gamma^{L}(x,s;\bar{\gamma}(r)),\gamma^{R}(z^{+},s;\bar{\gamma}(r)))=\emptyset\}.

Again by Proposition
3.5
.2, we have that
O(\gamma^{L}(z^{-},s;\bar{\gamma}(r)),\gamma^{R}(z^{+},s;\bar{\gamma}(r)))=\emptyset
. We claim that
z^{-}=z^{+}
. Indeed, if not then we can find
x\in(z^{-},z^{+})
. By the definition of
z^{+}
, the geodesic
\gamma^{L}(x,s;\bar{\gamma}(r))
must have non-empty overlap with
\gamma|_{(s,r)}
and hence also with
\gamma^{L}(z^{-},s;\bar{\gamma}(r))
by monotonicity (Lemma
2.7
.3). By the definition of
z^{-}
the geodesic
\gamma^{L}(x,s;\bar{\gamma}(r))
must also have non-empty overlap with
\gamma^{R}(z^{+},s;\bar{\gamma}(r))
. Let

\displaystyle r^{-}

\displaystyle=\inf O(\gamma^{L}(x,s;\bar{\gamma}(r)),\gamma^{L}(z^{-},s;\bar{\gamma}(r))),

\displaystyle\qquad r^{+}

\displaystyle=\inf O(\gamma^{L}(x,s;\bar{\gamma}(r)),\gamma^{R}(z^{+},s;\bar{\gamma}(r)))

If
r^{+}<r^{-}
, then the geodesic

\gamma^{L}(x,s;\bar{\gamma}(r))|_{[s,r^{+}]}\oplus\gamma^{R}(z^{+},s;\bar{\gamma}(r))|_{[r^{+},r]}

is disjoint from
\gamma^{L}(z^{-},s;\bar{\gamma}(r))
, and hence also from
\gamma|_{(s,r)}
, which contradicts the minimality of
z^{+}
. If
r^{+}>r^{-}
, then the geodesic

\gamma^{L}(x,s;\bar{\gamma}(r))|_{[s,r^{-}]}\oplus\gamma^{L}(z^{-},s;\bar{\gamma}(r))|_{[r^{-},r]}

is to the left of
\gamma^{L}(x,s;\bar{\gamma}(r))
contradicting the fact that
\gamma^{L}(x,s;\bar{\gamma}(r))
is a leftmost geodesic. Hence
z^{-}=z^{+}
.

Similarly, we can find a point
y\in(\gamma(t),\pi(t))
such that the leftmost and rightmost geodesics from
\bar{\gamma}(r)
to
(y,t)
are disjoint. Let
u=(z^{-},s;y,t)
. To finish the proof, we claim that
O(\gamma^{R}(u),\gamma^{L}(u))=\{r\}
. Indeed, by geodesic ordering (Lemma
2.7
.3),

\gamma|_{(s,t)}\leq\gamma^{L}(u)\leq\gamma^{R}(u)\leq\pi|_{(s,t)},

so
\{r\}\subset O(\gamma^{R}(u),\gamma^{L}(u))
and
\gamma(r)=\gamma^{R}(u)=\gamma^{L}(u)
. On the other hand, we have exhibited pairs of disjoint geodesics from
(z^{-},s)
to
\bar{\gamma}(r)
and from
\bar{\gamma}(r)
to
(y,t)
, so
O(\gamma^{R}(u),\gamma^{L}(u))=\{r\}
.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

Almost surely, there are no points
u=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
with
s,t
rational such that
O(\gamma^{R}(u);\gamma^{L}(u))
is a single point.

Just as with the proof of Proposition
4.4
, the proof of Lemma
5.3
is based on the analysis of a problem involving rare events for the Airy line ensemble. As in the proof of Proposition
4.4
, we require a lemma about the rarity of a certain Brownian event.

Lemma 5.4
.

Fix
\epsilon>0,m\geq 1
, and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
a+2\epsilon<b
.
Let
\bar{B}=(B,B^{\prime})=(B_{1},B_{2},B_{1}^{\prime},B_{2}^{\prime})
be a
4
-tuple of Brownian bridges from
(a,\mathbf{x})
to
(b,\mathbf{y})
for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in[-m,m]^{4}
. Fix
z\in(a+\epsilon,b-\epsilon)
, and let
T_{a,b}(B,B^{\prime};z,\delta)
be the event where

\displaystyle B_{1}(z)-B_{2}(z)

\displaystyle<\delta,\qquad\qquad B_{1}^{\prime}(z)-B_{2}^{\prime}(z)<\delta,\text{ and }

\displaystyle B_{1}(z)+B_{1}^{\prime}(z)+\delta

\displaystyle>B_{1}(w)+B_{1}^{\prime}(w)\text{ for all }w\in[a,b].

Then

\mathbb{P}(T_{a,b}(B,B^{\prime};z,\delta),B\in\operatorname{NI},B^{\prime}\in\operatorname{NI})\leq e^{c_{\epsilon,a,b}m}\delta^{14}.

Proof.

Consider the
4
-tuple of functions

W=(W_{1},W_{2},W_{3},W_{4})=(-B_{2}+\delta,-B_{1}+\delta,B_{1}^{\prime}-[B_{1}(z)+B_{1}^{\prime}(z)],B_{2}^{\prime}-[B_{1}(z)+B_{1}^{\prime}(z)]).

Then
T_{a,b}(B,B^{\prime};z,\delta)\cap\{B\in\operatorname{NI}\}\cap\{B^{\prime}\in\operatorname{NI}\}
is the same the event
T^{*}
given by

W\in\operatorname{NI},\quad W_{i}(z)-W_{i+1}(z)<\delta\quad\text{ for }i=1,3.

To estimate
\mathbb{P}(T^{*})
, observe that conditional on
\bar{B}(z)
,
W|_{[a,z]}
and
W_{[z,b]}
are two independent
4
-tuples of Brownian bridges from
(0,g(\mathbf{x}))
to
(z,g(\bar{B}(z)))
and from
(z,g(\bar{B}(z)))
to
(1,g(\mathbf{y}))
, where

g(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})=(-w_{2}+\delta,-w_{1}+\delta,w_{3}-[B_{1}(z)+B_{1}^{\prime}(z)],w_{4}-[B_{1}(z)+B_{1}^{\prime}(z)]).

Therefore

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(T^{*})=\int_{\Delta}\mathbb{P}_{0,z}(g(\mathbf{x}),g(\mathbf{a});\operatorname{NI})\mathbb{P}_{z,1}(g(\mathbf{a}),g(\mathbf{y});\operatorname{NI})\rho(\mathbf{a})d\mathbf{a},

(48)

where
\rho
is the (Gaussian) density of
\bar{B}(z)
, and
\Delta
is the set of all
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
with
0<a_{1}-a_{2}<\delta
and
0<a_{3}-a_{4}<\delta
. By Lemma
4.7
,

\mathbb{P}_{0,z}(g(\mathbf{x}),g(\mathbf{a});\operatorname{NI})\leq e^{c_{a,b,\epsilon}(m+\|\mathbf{a}\|_{\infty})}\delta^{6},\qquad\mathbb{P}_{z,1}(g(\mathbf{a}),g(\mathbf{y});\operatorname{NI})\leq e^{c_{a,b,\epsilon}(m+\|\mathbf{a}\|_{\infty})}\delta^{6},

for
\mathbf{a}\in\Delta
, where we have used that
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in[-m,m]^{4}
to remove the dependence on these parameters. Therefore by (
48
) we have the desired bound:

\mathbb{P}(T^{*})\leq\int_{\Delta}e^{2c_{a,b,\epsilon}(m+\|\mathbf{a}\|_{\infty})}\delta^{12}\rho(\mathbf{a})d\mathbf{a}\leq e^{c_{a,b,\epsilon}m}\delta^{14}.\qed

For the proof of Lemma
5.3
, we use both the
\mathcal{L}^{p,t},\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{q,t}
notation used in the proof of Proposition
4.4
and the notation of Lemma
5.4
.

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

By landscape rescaling and a countable additivity argument, it suffices to show that almost surely, for every fixed
m>0
there are no such points
u=(x,0;y,1)
with
x,y\in[-m,m]
such that the overlap
O(\gamma^{R}(u);\gamma^{L}(u))
is a single point
p=(z,t)
with
z\in[-m,m]
and
t\in[1/m,1-1/m]
. Suppose that there is a triple
(x,y,p)
for which this is indeed the case. Then the following three equations hold:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(x,0;z,t)+\mathcal{L}(z,t;y,1)

\displaystyle=\max_{w\in[-m-1,m+1]}\mathcal{L}(x,0;w,t)+\mathcal{L}(w,t;y,1),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}((x,x),0;(z,z),t)

\displaystyle=2\mathcal{L}(x,0;z,t),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}((z,z),t;(y,y),1)

\displaystyle=2\mathcal{L}(z,t;y,1).

We can equivalently write these equations in terms of the line ensembles
\mathcal{L}^{x,t}:=\mathcal{L}^{(x,0),t},\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{y,t}:=\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(y,1),t}
:

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}^{x,t}_{1}(z)+\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{y,t}_{1}(z)&=\max_{w\in[-m-1,m+1]}\mathcal{L}^{x,t}_{1}(w)+\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{y,t}_{1}(w),\\
\quad\mathcal{L}^{x,t}_{1}(z)&=\mathcal{L}^{x,t}_{2}(z),\quad\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{y,t}_{1}(z)=\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{y,t}_{2}(z).\end{split}

(49)

Now fix
\delta>0
. By the modulus of continuity for the extended landscape (Lemma
2.14
), if (
49
) holds for some
x,y,z\in[-m,m]
and
t\in[1/m,1-1/m]
then
for all small enough
\epsilon>0
the event

\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,z\in\epsilon^{2+\delta}\mathbb{Z}\cap[-m-1/2,m+1/2],\\
t\in\epsilon^{3+\delta}\mathbb{Z}\cap[1/(2m),1-1/(2m)]\end{subarray}}T_{-m-1,m+1}(\mathcal{L}^{x,t},\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{y,t};z,\epsilon)

(50)

holds. By Theorem
2.9
, for all choices of
x,y,z,t
in the union in (
50
) there are random vectors
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}(x,y,t),\mathbf{w}^{\prime}=\mathbf{w}^{\prime}(x,y,t)
and a constant
c_{m}>0
such that letting
B,B^{\prime}
be independent Brownian bridges from
(-m-1,\mathbf{w})
to
(m+1,\mathbf{w}^{\prime})
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(T_{-m-1,m+1}(\mathcal{L}^{x,t},\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{y,t};z,\epsilon))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(T_{-m-1,m+1}(\mathcal{L}^{x,t},\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{y,t};z,\epsilon),\mathcal{L}^{x,t}\in\operatorname{NI},\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{y,t}\in\operatorname{NI})

\displaystyle\leq c_{m}\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{P}(T_{-m-1,m+1}(B,B^{\prime};z,\epsilon),B\in\operatorname{NI},B^{\prime}\in\operatorname{NI}\mid\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime})]

\displaystyle\leq c_{m}\epsilon^{14}\mathbb{E}e^{c_{m}\|(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\|_{\infty}}.

Here the fact that the constant in the first inequality only depends on
m
uses the fact that
x,y\in[-m-1,m+1],t\in[1/(2m),1-1/(2m)]
and the landscape symmetries in Lemma
2.2
, and the second inequality is Lemma
5.4
. Finally,
\mathbb{E}e^{c_{m}\|(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\|_{\infty}}\leq c_{m}^{\prime}
by the tail bounds in Theorem
2.9
.3, so the probability of (
50
) is
O(\epsilon^{5-4\delta})
as
\epsilon\to 0
. Therefore the probability that there is some
x,y,z,t
for which (
49
) holds is
0
.
∎

Lemma
5.1
is an immediate consequence of Lemmas
5.2
and
5.3
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Almost surely, we cannot find three geodesics
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3}
that start and end at common points
p=(x,s)
and
q=(y,t)
, coincide on an interval
[s,r]
with
s<r<t
and are mutually disjoint on the interval
(r,t)
.

The strategy for proving Lemma
5.5
is similar to that of Lemma
5.1
, except we do not require an initial rational reduction step (a version of Lemma
5.2
). We start with a Brownian lemma, the analogue of Lemma
5.4
in the present setting.

Lemma 5.6
.

Fix
\epsilon>0,m\geq 1
, and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
a+2\epsilon<b
. Let
\bar{B}=(B,B^{\prime})=(B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},B_{1}^{\prime})
be a
4
-tuple of Brownian bridges from
(a,\mathbf{x})
to
(b,\mathbf{y})
for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in[-m,m]^{4}
. Fix
z\in(a+\epsilon,b-\epsilon)
, and let
S_{a,b}(B,B^{\prime},z,\delta)
be the event where

\displaystyle B_{1}(z)-B_{3}(z)<\delta,\qquad

\displaystyle B_{1}(z)+B_{1}^{\prime}(z)+\delta>B_{1}(w)+B_{1}^{\prime}(w)\quad\forall w\in[a,b].

Then

\mathbb{P}(S_{a,b}(B,B^{\prime},z,\delta),B\in\operatorname{NI})\leq e^{c_{\epsilon,a,b}m}\delta^{14}.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma
5.4
.
Consider the
4
-tuple of functions

W=(W_{1},W_{2},W_{3},W_{4})=(-B_{3}+\delta,-B_{2}+\delta,-B_{1}+\delta,B_{1}^{\prime}-[B_{1}(z)+B_{1}^{\prime}(z)]).

Then
S_{a,b}(B,B^{\prime},z,\delta)\cap\{B\in\operatorname{NI}\}
is contained in the event
S^{*}
where

W\in\operatorname{NI},\quad W_{1}(z)-W_{3}(z)<\delta.

To estimate
\mathbb{P}(S^{*})
, observe that conditional on
\bar{B}(z)
,
W|_{[0,z]}
and
W|_{[z,1]}
are two independent
4
-tuples of Brownian bridges from
(0,g(\mathbf{x}))
to
(z,g(\bar{B}(z)))
and from
(z,g(\bar{B}(z)))
to
(1,g(\mathbf{y}))
, where

g(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})=(-w_{3}+\delta,-w_{2}+\delta,-w_{1}+\delta,w_{4}-[B_{1}(z)+B_{1}^{\prime}(z)]).

Therefore

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(S^{*})=\int_{\Delta}\mathbb{P}_{0,z}(g(\mathbf{x}),g(\mathbf{a});\operatorname{NI})\mathbb{P}_{z,1}(g(\mathbf{a}),g(\mathbf{y});\operatorname{NI})\rho(\mathbf{a})d\mathbf{a},

(51)

where
\rho
is the (Gaussian) density of
\bar{B}(z)
, and
\Delta
is the set of all
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
with
0<a_{1}-a_{3}<\delta
and
a_{1}>a_{2}>a_{3}
. From here the proof goes through as in the proof of Lemma
5.4
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.5
.

First note that if such geodesics
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3}
exist, then by possibly restricting
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3}
to an interval
[s^{\prime},t]
with
s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(s,r)
we may assume that
s
is rational. Let
\mathcal{X}_{m,s}
denote the event where three geodesics
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3}
as in the statement of the lemma exist, with

x,y,t\in[-m,m],\qquad s\leq r-1/m<r+1/m\leq t,\qquad z:=\pi_{1}(r)\in[-m,m].

(52)

By countable additivity, it suffices to show that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}_{m,s})=0
for all fixed
m\in\mathbb{N},s\in\mathbb{Q}
. Suppose that such a triple
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3}
does exist. Then the following two equations hold:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,r)+\mathcal{L}(z,r;y,t)

\displaystyle=\max_{w\in[-m-1,m+1]}\mathcal{L}(x,s;w,r)+\mathcal{L}(w,r;y,t)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(z^{3},r;y^{3},t)

\displaystyle=3\mathcal{L}(z,r;y,t).

In other words, with notation as in the proof of Lemma
5.3
we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}^{p,r}_{1}(z)+\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\hat{q},r}_{1}(z)=\max_{w\in[-m-1,m+1]}\mathcal{L}^{p,r}_{1}(w)+\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\hat{q},r}_{1}(w),\qquad\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\hat{q},r}_{1}(z)=\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\hat{q},r}_{3}(z).\end{split}

(53)

Now, fix
\delta>0
. By the modulus of continuity for the extended landscape (Lemma
2.14
), if (
53
) holds for some
p=(x,t),q=(y,s),r,z
satisfying (
52
), then for all small enough
\epsilon>0
the following event holds:

\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,z\in\epsilon^{2+\delta}\mathbb{Z}\cap[-m-1/2,m+1/2],\\
t\in\epsilon^{3+\delta}\mathbb{Z}\cap[-m-1/2,m+1/2],\\
r\in\epsilon^{3+\delta}\mathbb{Z}\cap(s+1/(2m),t-1/(2m))\end{subarray}}S_{-m-1,m+1}(\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(y,t),r},\mathcal{L}^{(x,s),r},z,\epsilon).

(54)

By Theorem
2.9
, for all choices of
x,y,z,r,t
in the union in (
54
) there are random vectors
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}(x,y,r,t),\mathbf{w}^{\prime}=\mathbf{w}^{\prime}(x,y,r,t)
and a constant
c_{m}>0
such that letting
B,B^{\prime}
be independent Brownian bridges from
(-m-1,\mathbf{w})
to
(m+1,\mathbf{w}^{\prime})
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(S_{-m-1,m+1}(\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(y,t),r},\mathcal{L}^{(x,s),r},z,\epsilon))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(S_{-m-1,m+1}(\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(y,t),r},\mathcal{L}^{(x,s),r},z,\epsilon),\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(y,t),r}\in\operatorname{NI})

\displaystyle\leq c_{m}\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{P}(S_{-m-1,m+1}(B,B^{\prime};z,\epsilon),B\in\operatorname{NI}\mid\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime})]

\displaystyle\leq c_{m}\epsilon^{14}\mathbb{E}e^{c_{m}\|(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\|_{\infty}}.

Here the fact that the constant in the first inequality only depends on
m
uses that
x,y,z,r,t\in[-m-1/2,m+1/2],|r-t|,|r-s|\geq 1/(2m)
. The second inequality is Lemma
5.6
. Finally,
\mathbb{E}e^{c_{m}\|(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\|_{\infty}}\leq c_{m}^{\prime}
by the tail bounds in Theorem
2.9
.3, so the probability of (
54
) is
O(\epsilon^{2-5\delta})
as
\epsilon\to 0
and hence
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}_{m,s})=0
.
∎

6
Networks and their dimensions

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.1
and parts
1
and
2
of Theorem
1.2
. The major part of this section is devoted to carefully understanding how to cut weighted geodesic stars away from geodesic networks, and how to paste weighted geodesic stars together via an intermediate forest. See Section
1.1
for a rough outline. This section contains three preliminary parts before we proceed to the main proofs in Section
6.4
: a part on graph theory basics, a short section on constructing forests in
\mathcal{L}
, and a section containing two technical lemmas about the Airy sheet.

6.1
Graph theory

Directed embeddings of directed graphs.
Suppose that
G=(V,E)
is a finite, loop-free planar directed graph with no directed cycles and no vertices of both in-degree
1
and out-degree
1
. We call
G
a
candidate graph
.
A map
\Phi:V\cup E\to\{\text{subsets of }\mathbb{R}^{2}\}
is a
directed embedding
of
G
if it satisfies the following conditions:

•

\Phi(v)
is a point for all
v\in V
.

•

For every edge
e=(u,v)\in E
, there is a continuous function
\gamma_{e}:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\Phi(e)=\mathfrak{g}\gamma|_{(s,t)}
, and
\Phi(u)=\bar{\gamma}(s),\Phi(v)=\bar{\gamma}(t)
.

•

\Phi(a)\cap\Phi(b)=\emptyset
for all
a\neq b\in V\cup E
.

We call
\Phi(G):=\bigcup_{a\in E\cup V}\Phi(a)
the image of
G
under
\Phi
. Now suppose that
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the image of some candidate graph
G
; we claim that
G
is unique up isomorphism. Indeed, the vertex set
V
is given by all points
v\in\Gamma
such that there is no open set
O\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
containing
v
for which the intersection
\Gamma\cap O
is given by
\{(\pi(s),s):s\in(a,b)\}
for some
a<b
and a continuous function
\pi
. Edges correspond to the connected components of
\Gamma\setminus V
with the direction inherited from the time coordinate, yielding a graph
G=(V,E)
. For all
(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, the network graph
G(p;q)
can be constructed from the geodesic network
\Gamma(p;q)
in this way. We will also use this map to associate other subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to directed graphs.

Ordered forests and interior forests.
We say that a candidate graph
G=(V,E)
is a
(k,\ell)
-
ordered forest
if:

•

G
is a forest (as an undirected graph).

•

V
has
k
source vertices
p_{1},\dots,p_{k}
, all of which have degree
1
.

•

V
has
\ell
sink vertices
q_{1},\dots,q_{\ell}
, all of which have degree
1
.

•

G
has a directed embedding into
\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
with
p_{i}\mapsto(i,0),q_{j}\mapsto(j,1)
for all
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,j\in\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket
.

Setting some notation, let
\partial V^{-}=\{p_{1},\dots,p_{k}\},\partial V^{+}=\{q_{1},\dots,q_{\ell}\},\partial V=\partial V^{-}\cup\partial V^{+}
, and
V^{\circ}=V\setminus\partial V
. We call a
(i,j)\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket
an
admissible pair
if there is a path from
p_{i}
to
q_{j}
in
G
, and write
\operatorname{Adm}(G)\subset\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket
for the set of admissible pairs.

If
\Gamma(p;q)
is a geodesic network from
p
to
q
with network graph
G
, then by virtue of rule
3
in Theorem
1.1
, if we remove a small ball around
p
and
q
we are left with an ordered forest, which we call an interior forest for
G
. More precisely, for a graph
G=(V,E)\in\mathscr{G}
with source and sink
p,q
, we say that a graph
G^{\prime}
is an
interior forest
for
G
if there is a directed embedding
\Phi
of
G
into
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that:

•

\Phi(p)\in\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,0),\Phi(q)\in\mathbb{R}\times(1,\infty)
, and
\Phi(v)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,1)
for all
v\in V\setminus\{p,q\}
.

•

\Phi(G)\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1])
is a directed embedding of
G^{\prime}
.

While the graph structure of
G^{\prime}
is unique up isomorphism, the ordering on its source and sink vertices may not be.

Vertex restrictions for
G\in\mathscr{G}
.
Next, we prove a simple lemma restricting the size of the vertex set for a graph
G\in\mathscr{G}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
G=(V,E)\in\mathscr{G}
with source degree
\deg(p)=k\in\llbracket 1,3\rrbracket
and sink degree
\deg(q)=\ell\in\llbracket 1,3\rrbracket
. Then

2+|k-\ell|\leq|V|\leq k+\ell

and

d(G):=12-\frac{|V|+k^{2}+\ell^{2}}{2}=11-|F|-{k\choose 2}-{\ell\choose 2}\geq 0.

(55)

Here
|F|
denotes the number of faces in any planar embedding of
G
.

Proof.

First, since all vertices in
V\setminus\{p,q\}
have degree
3
we have
2|E|=k+\ell+3(|V|-2)
. Therefore by Euler’s formula
|F|+|V|=|E|+2
for planar graphs, we have

2|F|=k+\ell+|V|-2.

(56)

This implies the equality in (
55
