). Next, if we remove
p
and
q
from
G
and the
k+\ell
edges incident to
p
and
q
, then we are left with a forest. Therefore

|E|-k-\ell+3\leq|V|,

which by the previous formula for
|E|
implies that
|V|\leq k+\ell
and also gives the inequality in (
55
).

Finally, in a directed embedding of
G
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, if
e_{1},\dots,e_{k}
are the edges exiting from
p
, listed from left to right, then each of the pairs
(e_{k},e_{1}),(e_{1},e_{2}),\dots,(e_{k-1},e_{k})
must bound a different face since any two directed paths exiting
p
eventually meet. Therefore
|F|\geq k
, and so by (
56
) we have
2+k-\ell\leq V
. By symmetry,
2+\ell-k\leq V
as well.
∎

Weighted forests and slide-invariant affine sets.
We finish this section with a lemma that will characterize the requirements on the weight of a pair of geodesic stars connected through a given interior forest.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
G=(V,E)
be a
(k,\ell)
-ordered forest. Let
Z:E\to\mathbb{R}
and for
(i,j)\in\operatorname{Adm}(G)
, let
d_{Z}(p_{i},q_{j})
be the
Z
-weight of the unique path in
G
from
p_{i}
to
q_{j}
. Now let
W(Z)
be the set of all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
such that

x_{i}+d_{Z}(p_{i},q_{j})+y_{j}=x_{i^{\prime}}+d_{Z}(p_{i^{\prime}},q_{j^{\prime}})+y_{j^{\prime}}

(57)

for all
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\operatorname{Adm}(G)
. Then:

•

W(Z)
is a slide-invariant affine set.

•

Let
e^{i}_{p},e^{j}_{q}
be the unique edges exiting
p_{i}
and entering
q_{j}
in
G
, and let
E^{*}=\{e^{1}_{p},\dots,e^{k}_{p},e^{1}_{q},\dots,e^{\ell}_{q}\}
. Let
F:E(G)\to\mathbb{R}
be a function which is
0
outside of
E^{*}
. Then

W(Z+F)=W(Z)-(F(e^{1}_{p}),\dots,F(e^{k}_{p}),F(e^{1}_{q}),\dots,F(e^{\ell}_{q})).

•

W(Z)
has linear dimension
c(G)+1
, where
c(G)
is the number of components in
G
.

Proof.

The first bullet is immediate from definition. For the second bullet, simply observe that

d_{Z+F}(p_{i},q_{j})=F(e^{i}_{p})+d_{Z}(p_{i},q_{j})+F(e_{q}^{j}).

We come to the final bullet point. For a component
J
of
G
, let

I_{p}(J)=\{i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket:p_{i}\in J\},\qquad\qquad I_{q}(J)=\{j\in\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket:q_{j}\in J\}.

Claim:
For any component
J
of
H(G)
, the set of all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{I_{p}(J)}\times\mathbb{R}^{I_{q}(J)}
such that (
57
) holds for all admissible pairs
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in I_{p}(J)\times I_{q}(J)
has linear dimension
2
.

Proof:
Without loss of generality, we may assume
I_{p}(J)=\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket,I_{q}(J)=\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
for some
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
; the other cases have the same proof after relabeling.
First, let
A
be the undirected graph whose vertex set is
\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket\cup\{1^{\prime},\dots,n^{\prime}\}
and whose edges are pairs
(i,j^{\prime})
with
(i,j)\in\operatorname{Adm}(G)
.
Note that
A
must be connected, and so
A
has at least
m+n-1
edges
(i_{1},j_{1}^{\prime}),\dots,(i_{m+n-1},j_{m+n-1}^{\prime})
. Next, the
m+n-2
linear equations

x_{i_{u}}+d_{Z}(p_{i_{u}},q_{j_{u}^{\prime}})+y_{j_{u}^{\prime}}=x_{i_{u+1}}+d_{Z}(p_{i_{u+1}},q_{j_{u+1}^{\prime}})+y_{j_{u+1}^{\prime}},\qquad u\in\llbracket 1,m+n-2\rrbracket,

are linearly independent. Therefore the set of all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{I_{p}(J)}\times\mathbb{R}^{I_{q}(J)}
satisfying (
57
) has dimension at most
(m+n)-(m+n-2)=2
.

Next, create a graph
J^{\prime}
from
J
by adding in a vertex
p_{*}
and connecting it to all the vertices
p_{i},i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and a vertex
q_{*}
and connecting all the vertices
q_{i}
to it. Put weights
x_{i}
on the edges
(p_{*},p_{i})
and weights
y_{i}
on the edges
(q_{i},q_{*})
.
The graph
J^{\prime}
is still planar. Indeed, from the definition of
(k,\ell)
-ordered forests, we can find a directed embedding of
J
into
\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
where
p_{i}\mapsto(i,0),q_{j}\mapsto(j,1)
. We can easily extend this to an embedding of
J^{\prime}
into
\mathbb{R}\times[-1,2]
which maps
p_{*}\mapsto(0,-1),q_{*}\mapsto(0,2)
. In this embedding:

•

There is a unique face containing
p_{*},q_{*}
, and this face is unbounded.

•

For all
i\in\llbracket 2,m\rrbracket
, there is a unique bounded face
f_{i}
such that the edges
(p_{*},p_{i-1}),(p_{*},p_{i})
are both on the boundary of
f_{i}
.

•

For all
j\in\llbracket 2,n\rrbracket
there is a unique bounded face
f_{j}^{\prime}
such that the edges
(q_{j-1},q_{*}),(q_{j},q_{*})
are both on the boundary of
f_{j}^{\prime}
.

Note that all the
f_{i},f_{j}^{\prime}
are distinct since otherwise the original graph
J
would not be connected. Moreover,
f_{i},i\in\llbracket 2,m\rrbracket,f_{j}^{\prime},j\in\llbracket 2,n\rrbracket
constitute all bounded faces of
J^{\prime}
since
J
is a tree.

Now, equation (
57
) holds for all admissible
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
if the sum of weights along any undirected cycle in
J^{\prime}
equals
0
, where we add an edge weight with a negative sign if that edge is traversed backwards. Since the cycles around the bounded faces
f_{i},i\in\llbracket 2,m\rrbracket,f_{j}^{\prime},j\in\llbracket 2,n\rrbracket
span the entire cycle space of
J^{\prime}
, it suffices to check that the sum of weights around these cycles is
0
. Now, for any
i\in\llbracket 2,m\rrbracket
the sum of edges around the face
f_{i}
only involves the variables
x_{i-1},x_{i}
from the set
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\}
, and for any
j\in\llbracket 2,n\rrbracket
, the sum of edges around the face
f_{j}^{\prime}
only involves the variables
y_{j-1},y_{j}
. Therefore given any choice of
(x_{1},y_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
we can recursively define
x_{i},y_{j}
for
i\geq 2,j\geq 2
to get a solution to the set of equations (
57
) that holds for all admissible
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
. Hence this solution set has dimension at least
2
.

\blacksquare

Now let
J^{1},\dots,J^{c(G)}
be the components of
G
. By the above claim, the set
W^{*}(Z)
of all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
that satisfy (
57
) whenever
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
are admissible and in the same block
I_{p}(J_{a})\times I_{q}(J_{a})
has dimension

2c(G).

Moreover,
W^{*}(Z)
is invariant under the map

(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mapsto(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y}+\mathbf{w})

(58)

for any vectors
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
where the indices of
\mathbf{z}
are constant on each of the blocks
I_{p}(J_{a})
and the indices of
\mathbf{w}
are constant on each of the blocks
I_{q}(J_{a})
.
In order for
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
to satisfy (
57
) for all admissible
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
, making an arbitrary choice of an admissible
(i_{a},j_{a})
in
I_{p}(J_{a})\times I_{q}(J_{a})
for all
a\in\llbracket 1,c(G)-1\rrbracket
, it is enough to impose the following additional
c(G)-1
constraints:

x_{i_{a-1}}+d_{Z}(p_{i_{a-1}},q_{j_{a-1}})+y_{j_{a-1}}=x_{i_{a}}+d_{Z}(p_{i_{a}},q_{j_{a}})+y_{i_{a}},\quad a\in\llbracket 2,c(G)\rrbracket.

By using the invariance (
58
), we can see that this reduces the dimension of the solution set by exactly
c(G)-1
, yielding the desired result.
∎

Corollary 6.3
.

In the context of Lemma
6.2
, suppose that
G=(V,E)
is an interior forest for some
G^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})\in\mathscr{G}
. Then
c(G)=(k+\ell-|V^{\prime}|)/2+1
, so in the final bullet we have
\dim W(Z)=(k+\ell-|V^{\prime}|)/2+2
.

Proof.

We have
|V^{\prime}|=|V|-k-\ell+2
by the construction of
G
from
G^{\prime}
. Also,
c(G)=|V|-|E|
since
G
is a forest. Finally, since all vertices in
V^{\circ}
have degree
3
, and the
k
source and
\ell
sink vertices have degree
1
, we have
2|E|=3|V|-2(k+\ell)
. Combining these three formulas gives the result.
∎

6.2
Planting forests

Consider
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}_{<}
and a set
S\subset\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket
. Let
G^{0,1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},S)
be the unique candidate graph that can be direct-embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
as the set

\Gamma^{0,1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},S):=\bigcup_{(i,j)\in S}\Gamma(x_{i},0;y_{j},1).

(Here recall that
\Gamma(x_{i},0;y_{i},1)
is the geodesic network from
(x_{i},0)
to
(y_{i},1)
). We also write
G^{s,t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},S),\Gamma^{s,t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},S)
if the start and end times are
s,t
.
The goal of this short section is to show that we can realize certain ordered forests as networks of the form
G^{0,1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},S)
. This is a necessary step for proving existence of geodesic networks and lower bounds in Theorem
1.2
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a
(k,\ell)
-ordered forest such that all vertices in
V^{\circ}
have degree
3
and let
\alpha>0
. Then there exist (random) vectors
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{<},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Q}^{\ell}_{<}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
such that:

1.

(Forest Condition)
G^{0,1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\operatorname{Adm}(G))=G
.

2.

(Weight Condition) For all
(i,j)\in\operatorname{Adm}(G)
we have

a_{i}+\mathcal{L}(x_{i},0;y_{j},1)+b_{j}\in(0,1),

and for all
(i,j)\in\operatorname{Adm}(G)^{c}:=(\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket)\setminus\operatorname{Adm}(G)
we have

a_{i}+\mathcal{L}(x_{i},0;y_{j},1)+b_{j}<-\alpha.

Figure 9
:
The construction in Lemma
6.4
. The idea of the lemma is that we can find vectors
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
such that if we add in only geodesics from
(x_{i},0)
to
(y_{j},1)
for
(i,j)\in\operatorname{Adm}(G)
, then the resulting forest graph is given by
G
. The weight condition implies that if we pad the landscape on the strips
(-\epsilon,0)
and
(1,1+\epsilon)
with paths of weights
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}
then the paths between
(x_{i},-\epsilon)
and
(y_{j},1+\epsilon)
will be of comparable weight when
(i,j)\in\operatorname{Adm}(G)
, but highly inferior
(i,j)\notin\operatorname{Adm}(G)
. The forthcoming Lemma
6.5
will allows us to create this padding.

See Figure
9
for an illustration of the lemma.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, when
x,y\in\mathbb{Q}
we write
\pi^{x,y}
for the unique geodesic from
(x,0)
to
(y,1)
. We also assume that none of the points
(x,0),(y,1)
with
x,y\in\mathbb{Q}
are
2
-star points; this is an almost sure event since
\operatorname{Star}_{2}
is a lower-dimensional set (Theorem
1.5
). Finally, for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{<},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Q}^{\ell}_{<}
and
S\subset\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket
we write
S_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}=\{(x_{i},y_{j}):(i,j)\in S\}
. Condition
2
in the lemma can be equivalently phrased in terms of
\operatorname{Adm}(G)_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}
and
\operatorname{Adm}(G)^{c}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}
.

Case 1:
G
is a tree.
We will prove the result in this case by induction on
k+\ell
. The base case when
k=\ell=1
holds almost surely with
x_{1}=0,y_{1}=0,a_{1}=-\mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)
and
b_{1}=1/2
.
Now fix
m\geq 3
, suppose the result holds when
k+\ell=m-1
, and consider a
(k,\ell)
-ordered tree
G
where
k+\ell=m
.

Since
|V|=|E|+1=(\sum_{v\in V}\deg(v))/2+1
for a tree, source and sink vertices in
G
have degree
1
, and all interior vertices have degree
3
, we must have that
|V^{\circ}|=m-2=k+\ell-2
. Note that
m-2\geq 1
. Since all source and sink vertices connect to an interior vertex, the pigeonhole principle implies that at there is a vertex
v\in V^{\circ}
that connects to two vertices
v_{1},v_{2}
in
\partial V
. Without loss of generality, we assume
v
has two outgoing edges and one incoming edge, and that one of the outgoing edges is to
v_{1}
, so necessarily
v_{1}\in\partial V^{+}
.
We let
v_{3}
be the third vertex connected to
v
by an edge. We now define a
(k,\ell-1)
-ordered tree
G^{-}
as follows. There are two cases:

a.

First suppose
v_{1},v_{2}\in\partial V^{+}
. Define
G^{-}
by removing the vertices
v,v_{1}
and the edges
(v,v_{1}),(v,v_{2}),(v_{3},v)
, and adding in a new edge
(v_{3},v_{2})
.

b.

Suppose
v_{1}\in\partial V^{+},v_{2}\in\partial V^{-}
. In this case we may assume that
v_{3}\notin\partial V^{+}
since otherwise we are in case
1
after switching the labels on
v_{2},v_{3}
. Then the three edges incident to
v
are
(v_{2},v),(v,v_{1}),(v,v_{3})
.
Define
G^{-}
by removing the vertices
v,v_{1}
and the edges
(v_{2},v),(v,v_{1}),(v,v_{3})
, and adding in a new edge
(v_{2},v_{3})
.

In the example in Figure
9
, we can create
G^{\prime}
from
G
by removing either of the blue edges: the short edge corresponds to Case 1a and the long edge to Case 1b.
In both cases we have
\partial V^{+}(G^{-})=\partial V^{+}\setminus\{v_{1}\}
and
\partial V^{-}(G^{-})=\partial V^{-}
.
Now, in both of the above cases, by the inductive hypothesis we can find vectors
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{<},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Q}^{\ell-1}_{<},\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell-1}
such that conditions
1
and
2
in the lemma hold for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},G^{-}
.
Let
\epsilon>0
be small enough so that there are no interior vertices in
\Gamma^{0,1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\operatorname{Adm}(G^{-}))\cap(\mathbb{R}\times(\epsilon,1-\epsilon))
.

Case 1a:
v_{1},v_{2}\in\partial V^{+}
.
Let
y_{i}
be such that
(y_{i},1)
is identified with
v_{2}
in
\Gamma^{0,1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\operatorname{Adm}(G^{-}))
. Let

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{b}}

\displaystyle=(b_{1},\dots,b_{i},b_{i},b_{i+1},\dots,b_{\ell-1})\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell},\quad\;\text{and}\;

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}[y]

\displaystyle=(y_{1},\dots,y_{i},y,y_{i+1},\dots,y_{\ell-1})\in\mathbb{Q}^{\ell}_{<}

for
y\in(y_{i},y_{i+1})
. Then

\operatorname{Adm}(G)_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}[y]}=\operatorname{Adm}(G^{-})_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}\cup\{(x_{i},y):(x_{i},y_{i})\in\operatorname{Adm}(G^{-})_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}\}.

By continuity of
\mathcal{L}
and condition
2
for
G^{-},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
, there exists
\epsilon_{1}\in(0,1)
such that if
0<y-y_{i}<\epsilon_{1}
, then condition
2
in the lemma holds for
G,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}[y],\mathbf{a},\hat{\mathbf{b}}
. Moreover, Proposition
3.5
.2 implies that there exists
\epsilon_{2}>0
such that for all rational points
y
with
0<y-y_{i}<\epsilon_{2}
, we have
\pi^{x_{j},y}|_{[0,1-\epsilon]}=\pi^{x_{j},y_{i}}|_{[0,1-\epsilon]}
for all
j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
. Therefore by condition
1
for
G^{-},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
we have

G^{0,1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}[y],\operatorname{Adm}(G))=G

whenever
0<y-y_{i}<\epsilon_{2}
, so for
y\in(y_{i},y_{i}+\epsilon_{1}\wedge\epsilon_{2})\cap\mathbb{Q}
, the quadruple
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}[y],\mathbf{a},\hat{\mathbf{b}}
satisfies the lemma.

Case 1b:
v_{1}\in\partial V^{+},v_{2}\in\partial V^{-}
.
Let
x_{i}
be such that
(x_{i},0)
is identified with
v_{2}
. We claim
i\in\{1,k\}
. Indeed, because of how
v_{2}
was formed, we know that in a directed embedding of
G
into
\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
, that there is a face touching both
(x_{i},0)
and the line
\mathbb{R}\times\{1\}
. This can only happen if
i\in\{1,k\}
, since otherwise
G
would be disconnected.
Without loss of generality, assume
i=k
, and for
y>y_{\ell-1}
let
\mathbf{y}[y]=(y_{1},\dots,y_{\ell-1},y)
and

\mathbf{b}[y]=(b_{1},\dots,b_{\ell-1},-a_{k}-\mathcal{L}(x_{k},0;0,y)+1/2).

In this case

\operatorname{Adm}(G)_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}[y]}=\operatorname{Adm}(G^{-})_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}\cup\{(x_{k},y)\}.

Then for all large enough
y
, the landscape shape theorem (Theorem
2.5
) and condition
2
for
G^{-},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}
guarantees that condition
2
holds for
G,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}[y],\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}[y]
.
For condition
1
, Theorem
2.5
again guarantees that for large enough rational
y
we have
\pi^{x_{k},y}(r)>\pi^{x_{i},y_{j}}(r)
for all
r\in[\epsilon,1]
and
(i,j)\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,\ell-1\rrbracket
. On the other hand, since
x_{k}
is not a
2
-star point,
\pi^{x_{k},y}(r)=\pi^{x_{k},y_{\ell-1}}(r)
for some
r>0
, and hence
O(\pi^{x_{k},y},\pi^{x_{k},y_{\ell-1}})=[0,\delta]
for some
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
. Therefore for large enough rational
y
we have

G^{0,1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}[y],\operatorname{Adm}(G))=G,

and so for large enough rational
y
, the quadruple
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}[y],\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}[y]
satisfies the lemma.

Case 2:
G
is a forest.
Let
G_{1},\dots,G_{m}
be the components of
G
, listed so that if
p_{j_{1}}\in G_{i},p_{j_{2}}\in G_{i+1}
, then
j_{1}<j_{2}
. Let
k_{i},\ell_{i}
be such that each
G_{i}
is a
(k_{i},\ell_{i})
-tree. Then for each
G_{i}
there exist
\mathbf{x}^{i}\in\mathbb{Q}^{k_{i}}_{<},\mathbf{y}_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}^{\ell_{i}}_{<},\mathbf{a}^{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{i}},\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{i}}
such that with positive probability both conditions in the lemma hold for
\mathbf{x}^{i},\mathbf{y}^{i},\mathbf{a}^{i},\mathbf{b}^{i}
. Then by the landscape shape theorem (Theorem
2.5
) and since
\mathcal{L}
is strong mixing with respect to the spatial shift (Remark
2.3
), there exists a random vector
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}_{<}
such that the conditions of the lemma hold for

\displaystyle(\mathbf{x}^{1}+z_{1}{\bf 1}^{k_{1}},\dots,\mathbf{x}^{m}+z_{m}{\bf 1}^{k_{m}}),

\displaystyle\quad(\mathbf{y}^{1}+z_{1}{\bf 1}^{\ell_{1}},\dots,\mathbf{y}^{m}+z_{m}{\bf 1}^{\ell_{m}}),

\displaystyle\quad(\mathbf{a}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}^{m}),

\displaystyle\quad(\mathbf{b}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{b}^{m}).

Here
{\bf 1}^{n}=(1,\dots,1)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
∎

6.3
Tools for the Airy sheet

In order to cut and paste together geodesic stars and interior forests, we need two technical lemmas about the Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}
. Both of these are proven using the Airy line ensemble representation. The first lemma plants good regions in the Airy sheet and will allow us to locate geodesics.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{\gamma}
be an Airy sheet of scale
\gamma>0
.
For
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<},\epsilon,\beta>0,\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
let
T(\mathcal{S};\mathbf{x},\beta,\mathbf{w},\epsilon,\gamma)
be the event where:

1.

For all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\mathcal{S}_{\gamma}(x,y)+\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{\gamma^{3}}\leq\beta\log^{2}(3+|x|/\gamma^{2}+|y|/\gamma^{2})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathbf{1}(|y-x_{i}|<\epsilon).

2.

\mathcal{S}_{\gamma}(x,x_{i})\in[w_{i},w_{i}+\beta]
for all
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
and
|x-x_{i}|\leq\gamma^{2}
.

Then there exists
c_{k}^{\prime}>0
such that for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<},\mathbf{w}\in(c_{k}^{\prime}\gamma,\infty)^{k},

\beta>c_{k}\gamma,\epsilon>0,
we have
\mathbb{P}(T(\mathcal{S};\mathbf{x},\beta,\mathbf{w},\epsilon,\gamma))>0
.

Proof.

First, by Airy sheet rescaling (Lemma
2.2
) it suffices to prove the lemma when
\gamma=1
.
By translation invariance of
\mathcal{S}
, we may assume
x_{1}>1
.
First, as long as
c_{k}>0
is large enough and
\delta\in(0,1)
is small enough given
\mathbf{x}
, the following events hold simultaneously with probability at least
1/2
:

(I)

|\mathcal{S}(x,y)+(x-y)^{2}|\leq(c_{k}/2)\log^{2}(3+|x|+|y|)
for any
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. This uses the landscape shape theorem (Theorem
2.5
).

(II)

\mathcal{S}(x,x_{i})\in[-c_{k}/4,c_{k}/4]
for all
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,|x-x_{i}|\leq 1
. This uses spatial stationarity of
\mathcal{S}
(Lemma
2.2
.2).

(III)

Let
x_{0}=0
. Then for all
i,j\in\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket
and all
(x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
|x-x_{i}|\leq 1,|y-x_{j}|\leq 1
and
\|(x,y)-(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq 2\delta
we have

|\mathcal{S}(x,y)-\mathcal{S}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})|\leq\|(x,y)-(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\|_{2}^{1/3}.

This uses the modulus of continuity, Proposition
2.4
.

Let
A(\mathbf{x},\delta)
denote the event where these conditions hold, and let
\mathfrak{A}
be coupled to
\mathcal{S}
as in Definition
2.11
. Next, let
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
be small enough so that the intervals
(x_{i}-\delta,x_{i}+\delta)
are all disjoint and assume
w_{i}\geq c_{k}/2
for all
i
. Define
f^{\delta}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by the following rules:

•

f^{\delta}(x)=0
for
x\notin U(\mathbf{x},\delta):=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}(x_{i}-\delta,x_{i}+\delta)
.

•

f^{\delta}(x_{i})=w_{i}+c_{k}/2
for all
i
.

•

f^{\delta}
is linear on each of the intervals
[x_{i}-\delta,x_{i}],[x_{i},-x_{i}+\delta]
.

Define
\mathfrak{A}^{\delta}=(\mathfrak{A}_{1}+f^{\delta},\mathfrak{A}_{2},\mathfrak{A}_{3},\dots)
.
Since
f^{\delta}
is a non-negative Lipschitz function,and equals
0
outside of the compact set
[x_{1}-\delta,x_{k}+\delta]
, by the Brownian Gibbs property for
\mathfrak{A}
(Lemma
2.8
) applied to the set
\{1\}\times[x_{1}-\delta,x_{k}+\delta]
, the law of
\mathfrak{A}^{\delta}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the law of
\mathfrak{A}
for every fixed
\delta
.
Therefore letting
\mathcal{S}^{\delta}
be defined from
\mathfrak{A}^{\delta}
as in Definition
2.11
, the law of
\mathcal{S}^{\delta}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the law of
\mathcal{S}
. We claim that for
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
small enough and
\beta>c_{k}
, on the event
A(\mathbf{x},\delta)
, the event
T(\mathcal{S}^{\delta};\mathbf{x},\epsilon,\beta,\mathbf{w},1)
holds.

Verifying Condition
1
.
Let
x\geq 0,y\in\mathbb{R}
. For any parabolic path
\pi
from
(x,\infty)
to
(y,1)
, we have

\displaystyle\|\pi\|_{\mathfrak{A}^{\delta}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}^{\delta}(x,x_{1}-\delta)+\lim_{z\to-\infty}\left(\|\pi|_{[z,y]}\|_{\mathfrak{A}^{\delta}}-{\mathfrak{A}^{\delta}}[(z,\pi(z))\to(x_{1}-\delta,1)]\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}(x,x_{1}-\delta)+\lim_{z\to-\infty}\left(\|\pi|_{[z,y]}\|_{\mathfrak{A}^{\delta}}-\mathfrak{A}[(z,\pi(z))\to(x_{1}-\delta,1)]\right)

\displaystyle=\|\pi\|_{\mathfrak{A}}+\|\pi|_{[x_{1}-\delta,y]}\|_{\mathfrak{A}^{\delta}}-\|\pi|_{[x_{1}-\delta,y]}\|_{\mathfrak{A}}.

(59)

Here the first equality uses the comment after (
19
), and the remaining equalities use that
\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{A}^{\delta}
outside of of
\{1\}\times U(\mathbf{x},\delta)
. Now, if
y\notin U(\mathbf{x},\delta)
then
\|\pi|_{[x_{1}-\delta,y]}\|_{\mathfrak{A}^{\delta}}\leq\|\pi|_{[x_{1}-\delta,y]}\|_{\mathfrak{A}}
, and if
y\in[x_{i}-\delta,x_{i}+\delta]
then
\|\pi|_{[x_{1}-\delta,y]}\|_{\mathfrak{A}^{\delta}}\leq\|\pi|_{[x_{1}-\delta,y]}\|_{\mathfrak{A}}+w_{i}+c_{k}/2
. Therefore for
x\geq 0,y\in\mathbb{R}
, by Lemma
2.12
(i) we have

\mathcal{S}^{\delta}(x,y)\leq\mathcal{S}(x,y)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(w_{i}+c_{k}/2)\mathbf{1}(|x-x_{i}|<\delta).

(60)

Similar reasoning for
x\leq 0
using the Airy line ensemble
\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}(y):=\mathfrak{A}(-y)
yields the same bound for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
. Combined with the bound in condition (I) for
\mathcal{S}
, this yields condition
1
of the lemma.

Verifying Condition
2
.
By the quadrangle inequality (
9
) for the Airy sheet, using that
x_{1}>1
, for any
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
and
|x-x_{i}|\leq 1
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\delta}(x,x_{i})

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{S}^{\delta}(x,x_{i}-\delta)+\mathcal{S}^{\delta}(0,x_{i})-\mathcal{S}^{\delta}(0,x_{i}-\delta)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}(x,x_{i}-\delta)+\mathcal{S}(0,x_{i})+w_{i}+c_{k}/2-\mathcal{S}(0,x_{i}-\delta)

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{S}(x,x_{i})+w_{i}+c_{k}/2-\delta^{1/3}\geq w_{i}.

(61)

Here the equality uses that
\mathcal{S}(0,\cdot)=\mathfrak{A}_{1}(\cdot)
,
\mathcal{S}^{\delta}(0,\cdot)=\mathfrak{A}^{\delta}_{1}(\cdot)
and that
\mathcal{S}(x,w)=\mathcal{S}^{\delta}(x,w)
for
x\geq 0,w\leq x_{1}-\delta
by Definition
2.11
. The second inequality uses condition (III) for the event
A(\mathbf{x},\delta)
, and the final inequality uses condition (II). This yields the lower bound in condition
2
in the lemma. On the other hand, (
60
) gives that
\mathcal{S}^{\delta}(x_{i},x_{i})\leq\mathcal{S}(x_{i},x_{i})+w_{i}+c_{k}/2
, which is bounded above by
w_{i}+c_{k}
by (II), giving the upper bound in condition
2
.
∎

The next lemma gives a weak anti-concentration estimate for certain functions of the Airy sheet.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
I_{1}=[a_{1},b_{1}],\dots,I_{k}=[a_{k},b_{k}]
be compact intervals with
b_{i}<a_{i+1}
for all
i\in\llbracket 1,k-1\rrbracket
, and let
f_{i}:I_{i}\to\mathbb{R},i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
be continuous functions. Similarly let
I_{1}^{\prime}=[a_{1}^{\prime},b_{1}^{\prime}],\dots,I_{k}^{\prime}=[a_{k}^{\prime},b_{k}^{\prime}]
be compact intervals with
b_{i}^{\prime}<a_{i+1}^{\prime}
for all
i\in\llbracket 1,k-1\rrbracket
, and let
g_{i}:I_{i}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R},i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
be continuous functions. Then the vector
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with

X_{i}=\max_{x\in I_{i},y\in I_{i}^{\prime}}f_{i}(x)+\mathcal{S}(x,y)+g_{i}(y)

has a Lebesgue density.

Proof.

By translation invariance of
\mathcal{S}
, we may assume
a_{1},a_{1}^{\prime}>0
. First, by induction it is enough to show that for all
j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, conditional on the random variables
X_{1},\dots,X_{j-1}
, the distribution of
X_{j}
almost surely has a Lebesgue density.
Let
\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{S}
be coupled as in Definition
2.11
. By Lemma
2.12
(iii) we have

\displaystyle X_{j}

\displaystyle=\max_{x\in I_{j},y\in I_{j}^{\prime},n\in\mathbb{N}}f_{j}(x)+\mathfrak{A}[(x,\infty)\to(b_{j-1}^{\prime},n)]+\mathfrak{A}[(b_{j-1}^{\prime},n)\to(y,1)]+g_{j}(y).

It is important that this is a maximum, rather than a supremum. We can then write
X_{j}=\max_{n\in\mathbb{N}}W_{n}+Z_{n}
, where
where

W_{n}=\max_{x\in I_{j}}f_{j}(x)+\mathfrak{A}[(x,\infty)\to(b_{j-1}^{\prime},n)],\qquad Z_{n}=\max_{y\in I_{j}^{\prime}}\mathfrak{A}[(b_{j-1}^{\prime},n)\to(y,1)]+g_{j}(y).

Therefore it is enough to show that conditional on
X_{1},\dots,X_{j-1}
,
W_{n}+Z_{n}
has a Lebesgue density for all
n
. From Definition
2.11
, we see that
\sigma(X_{1},\dots,X_{j-1},W_{n})\subset\sigma(\mathfrak{A}|_{(-\infty,b_{j-1}^{\prime}]})
, so it is enough to show that conditional on
\sigma(\mathfrak{A}|_{(-\infty,b_{j-1}^{\prime}]})
,
Z_{n}
has a Lebesgue density. By the Brownian Gibbs property applied to the set
K:=\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times[b_{j-1}^{\prime},b_{j}^{\prime}+1]
,
given
\mathfrak{A}|_{(-\infty,b_{j-1}^{\prime}]}
we have

\mathfrak{A}|_{K}\ll\mathfrak{A}|_{K}+f^{U}

where
U
is a uniform random variable on
[0,1]
independent of
\mathfrak{A}
, and
f^{U}:K\to\mathbb{R}
is the function whose components
f^{U}_{1},\dots,f^{U}_{n}
are all identical, piecewise linear functions with pieces defined on
[b_{j-1}^{\prime},a_{j}^{\prime}],[a_{j}^{\prime},b_{j}^{\prime}],[b_{j}^{\prime},b_{j}^{\prime}+1]
, and satisfy

f^{N}_{\ell}(b_{j-1}^{\prime})=f^{N}_{\ell}(b_{j}^{\prime}+1)=0,\quad f^{N}_{\ell}(a_{j}^{\prime})=f^{N}_{\ell}(b_{j}^{\prime})=U,\qquad\ell\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket.

Since last passage commutes with shifts by a common function, for
y\in I_{j}^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{A}+f^{U})[(b_{j-1}^{\prime},n)\to(y,1)]=\mathfrak{A}[(b_{j-1}^{\prime},n)\to(z,1)]+U

and so
Z_{n}\ll Z_{n}+U
conditional on
\sigma(\mathfrak{A}|_{(-\infty,b_{j-1}^{\prime}]})
, which yields the result.
∎

6.4
Cut and paste: the proofs of Theorem
1.1
and parts
1
and
2
of Theorem
1.2

The proofs of Theorem
1.1
and parts
1
and
2
of Theorem
1.2
are divided into two parts: an upper bound (Proposition
6.7
) based on cutting networks into components stars connected by an interior forest, and a lower bound (Proposition
6.8
) based on pasting these pieces back together.

Proposition 6.7
.

For all
G\in\mathscr{G}
, a.s. we have

\dim_{1:2:3}(N_{\mathcal{L}}(G))\leq d(G),

and if
d(G)=0
, then
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G)
is at most countable.

To prove Proposition
6.7
, the basic idea is to cut up a potential geodesic network
\Gamma(p;q)
into a pair of geodesic stars at
p
and
q
and an interior forest for
G
. We can then appeal to Lemma
6.2
and Corollary
6.3
to show that the weight vector of the geodesic stars at
p
and
q
must lie in some
(k+\ell-|V|)/2+2
dimensional linear subspace
W
. The set of pairs
(p;q)
that support such stars has dimension at most
d(G)
by Proposition
4.4
.
Making this argument rigorous is a fairly delicate process. Indeed, we must ensure that we only need a bound for countably many distinct linear subspaces
W
; this relies on coalescence properties. Moreover, each of these subspaces cannot have a strong dependence on the weight of the geodesic stars; for this we will use Lemma
6.6
. These difficulties are the reason for the rather involved setup that comprises the first half of the proof.

Proof.

Fix
G=(V,E)\in\mathscr{G}
, with source vertex
p
of degree
k
and a sink
q
of degree
\ell
. Without loss of generality assume
k\leq\ell
.
We can write
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G)=N_{\mathcal{L}}^{1}(G)\cup N_{\mathcal{L}}^{2}(G)
, where
(p;q)\in N_{\mathcal{L}}^{1}(G)
if
G(p;q)=G
and
(p;q)\in N_{\mathcal{L}}^{1}(G)
if
G(p;q)=G^{T}
. By time-reversal symmetry of
\mathcal{L}
, it suffices to prove the proposition with
N_{\mathcal{L}}^{1}(G)
in place of
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G)
.

Figure 10
:
An example of a pair
(p;q)\in N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\mathbf{r},n,\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},S)
. Here
G
is the two-vertex graph
\{p,q\}
with a double-edge from
p
to
q
, and
S=\{(1,1),(2,2)\}
.

Step 1: Division into countably many parts.
We do this in two steps. First, for a rational vector
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<}
we write
\delta(\mathbf{a})=\min\{a_{i}-a_{i-1}:i\in\llbracket 2,k\rrbracket\}
. We can write

N_{\mathcal{L}}^{1}(G)=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{Q}^{2}_{<},n\in\mathbb{N}\text{ s.t. }r_{1}+3/n<r_{2}-3/n\\
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{<},\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{Q}^{\ell}_{<},S\subset\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket\end{subarray}}N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\mathbf{r},n,\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},S),

where
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\mathbf{r},n,\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},S)
is the set of all
(p;q)=(x,s;y,t)\in N_{\mathcal{L}}^{1}(G)
satisfying the following conditions:

1.

p\in(-n,n)\times(-\infty,r_{1}-3/n],q\in(-n,n)\times[r_{2}+3/n,\infty)
.

2.

\Gamma(p;q)\subset(-n,n)^{2}
and no interior vertices of
\Gamma(p;q)
are contained in the time intervals
(s,r_{1}+3/n)
and
(r_{2}-3/n,t)
.

3.

For
r\in(s,r_{1}+3/n)
, let
\mathbf{v}^{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<}
be the vector whose coordinates are the spatial coordinates of elements of
\Gamma(p;q)\cap(\mathbb{R}\times\{r\})
. For
r\in(r_{2}-3/n,t)
similarly define
\mathbf{v}^{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}_{<}
.
Then

\max_{r\in\{r_{1}-1/n,r_{1}+1/n\}}\|\mathbf{v}^{r}-\mathbf{a}\|_{\infty}<\delta(\mathbf{a})/4,\qquad\max_{r\in\{r_{1}-1/n,r_{1}+1/n\}}\|\mathbf{v}^{r}-\mathbf{b}\|_{\infty}<\delta(\mathbf{b})/4.

4.

There is a geodesic from
p
to
q
through the points
(v^{r_{1}}_{i},r_{1})
an
(v^{r_{2}}_{j},r_{2})
if and only if
(i,j)\in S
. Note that by point
2
, we could equivalently state this with
r_{1},r_{2}
replaced by any
r_{1}^{\prime}\in(s,r_{1}+3/n),r_{2}^{\prime}\in(r_{2}-3/n,t)
.

See Figure
10
for an illustration of much of the definition.
By countable stability, it suffices to prove the Hausdorff dimension and countability bounds in the proposition for
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\sigma_{0})
for each fixed
\sigma_{0}:=(\mathbf{r},n,\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},S)
. We will break up
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\sigma_{0})
once more before proceeding to the bound.

By Proposition
3.4
(i), for
i\in\{1,2\}
, there are finitely many points

z^{-}_{i,1}<\dots<z^{-}_{i,\ell^{-}(i)}
such that
(z^{-}_{i,j},r_{i}-2/n)
lies on a geodesic between points
p^{\prime}\in(-n,n)\times\{r_{i}-3/n\}
and
q^{\prime}\in(-n,n)\times\{r_{i}-1/n\}
. Similarly, there are finitely many points

z^{+}_{i,1}<\dots<z^{+}_{i,\ell^{+}(i)}
such that
(z^{+}_{i,j},r_{i}+2/n)
lies on a geodesic between points in
(-n,n)\times\{r_{i}+1/n\}
and
(-n,n)\times\{r_{i}+3/n\}
. For
(p;q)\in N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\sigma_{0})
, for
i=1,2
, we can write

\mathbf{v}^{r_{i}\pm 2/n}=(z^{\pm}_{i,j}:j\in I_{i}^{\pm}[p,q])

where
I_{i}^{\pm}[p,q]
is a subset of
\mathbb{N}
consisting of
k
elements if
i=1
and
\ell
elements if
i=2
.
Next, for all
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, the point
(v^{r_{1}-2/n}_{i},r_{1}-2/n)
is an interior point on the unique geodesic from
p
to
(v^{r_{1}-1/n}_{i},r_{1}-1/n)
. Therefore by Proposition
3.5
, there is a
rational
point
Q_{1}[p,q](i)\in(-n,n)
such that
(v^{r_{1}-2/n}_{i},r_{1}-2/n)
is an interior point on a geodesic from
p
to
(Q_{1}[p,q](i),r_{1}-1/n)
.
We can similarly define rational points
Q_{2}[p,q](j)\in(-n,n)
such that
(v^{r_{1}+2/n}_{j},r_{2}+2/n)
is an interior point on a geodesic from
(Q_{2}[p,q](j),r_{2}+1/n)
to
q
for all
j\in\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket
. See Figure
11
for an illustration of these definitions.

Therefore we can divide
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\sigma_{0})
into countably many pieces
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\sigma_{0},I,Q)
where:

•

I=(I_{1}^{-},I_{1}^{+},I_{2}^{-},I_{2}^{+})
is any vector consisting of four subsets of
\mathbb{N}
with
|I_{1}^{\pm}|=k,|I_{2}^{\pm}|=\ell
.

•

Q=(Q_{1},Q_{2})\in[\mathbb{Q}\cap(-n,n)]^{k}_{<}\times[\mathbb{Q}\cap(-n,n)]^{\ell}_{<}
.

•

If
(p;q)\in N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\sigma_{0},I,Q)
then
I_{i}^{\pm}=I_{i}^{\pm}[p,q]
and
Q_{i}=Q_{i}[p,q]
for
i=1,2
.

It suffices to prove the proposition for
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\sigma)
for each fixed
\sigma=(\sigma_{0},I,Q)
. From this point forward, we assume that
I^{\pm}_{1}=\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,I^{\pm}_{2}=\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket
; the proof in the remaining cases is the same up to relabeling. We may also assume that
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\sigma)
is non-empty, since otherwise the result holds trivially.

Figure 11
:
Some of the objects relevant to the definition of
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G,\sigma)
. In this example,
I^{-}_{1}[p,q]=\{1,4\}
. The blue paths are the start of the geodesic network
\Gamma(p;q)
, the black paths are geodesics from time
r_{1}-3/n
to
r_{1}-1/n
, and the red paths are parts of the geodesics from
p
to
Q_{1}[p,q](1),Q_{1}[p,q](2)
.

Step 2: From
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\sigma)
to weighted stars.
To relate
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\sigma)
to the existence of weighted stars at
p,q
, we use Lemma
6.2
. First, for
(i,j)\in S
, if
(p;q)\in N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\sigma)
then

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}(p;q)=\mathcal{L}(p;z^{-}_{1,i},r_{1}-2/n)+\mathcal{L}(z^{-}_{1,i},r_{1}-2/n;z^{+}_{2,j},r_{2}+2/n)+\mathcal{L}(z^{+}_{2,j},r_{2}+2/n;q).\end{split}

(62)

Using the conditions in
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\sigma)
on
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},Q
we can rewrite the right-hand side above. First, for
i=1,2
let

\mathcal{S}^{i}(x,y)=\mathcal{L}(x,r_{i}-1/n;y,r_{i}+1/n)

Then, for
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,j\in\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket
define functions

\displaystyle F_{1,i}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(z^{-}_{1,i},r_{1}-2/n;x,r_{1}-1/n)-\mathcal{L}(z^{-}_{1,i},r_{1}-2/n;Q_{1}(i),r_{1}-1/n),

\displaystyle G_{1,i}(y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(y,r_{1}+1/n;z^{+}_{1,i},r_{1}+2/n)

\displaystyle F_{2,j}(y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(y,r_{2}+1/n;z^{+}_{2,j},r_{2}+2/n)-\mathcal{L}(Q_{2}(j),r_{2}+1/n;z^{+}_{2,j},r_{2}+2/n)

\displaystyle G_{2,j}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(z^{-}_{2,j},r_{2}-2/n;x,r_{2}-1/n)

and let

\displaystyle X_{i}

\displaystyle=\max_{x,y\in[a_{i}-\delta(\mathbf{a})/4,a_{i}+\delta(\mathbf{a})/4]}F_{1,i}(x)+\mathcal{S}^{1}(x,y)+G_{1,i}(y),

\displaystyle Y_{j}

\displaystyle=\max_{x,y\in[b_{j}-\delta(\mathbf{b})/4,b_{j}+\delta(\mathbf{b})/4]}G_{2,j}(x)+\mathcal{S}^{2}(x,y)+F_{2,j}(y).

Then by metric composition, if
(p,q)\in N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\sigma)
, then (
62
) equals

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(p;Q_{1}(i),r_{1}-1/n)+X_{i}+\mathcal{L}(z^{+}_{1,i},r_{1}+2/n;z^{-}_{2,j},r_{2}-2/n)+Y_{j}+\mathcal{L}(Q_{2}(j),r_{2}+1/n;q).

Now, set
\mathbf{z}^{+}_{1}=(z^{+}_{1,1},\dots,z^{+}_{1,k}),\mathbf{z}^{-}_{2}=(z^{-}_{2,1},\dots,z^{-}_{2,\ell})
and

G^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})=G^{r_{1}+2/n,r_{2}-2/n}(\mathbf{z}^{+}_{1},\mathbf{z}^{-}_{2},S);

here notation is as in Section
6.2
. Note that
S=\operatorname{Adm}(G^{\prime})
, since otherwise
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\sigma)
would necessarily be empty.
Denote by
p_{1},\dots,p_{k},q_{1},\dots,q_{\ell}
the source and sink vertices of
G^{\prime}
. There is a weight function

Z:E^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}

such that

\mathcal{L}(z^{+}_{1,i},r_{1}+2/n;z^{-}_{2,j},r_{2}-2/n)=d_{Z}(p_{i},q_{j})

for all
(i,j)\in S
.
Moreover, let
e^{i}_{p}
be the unique edge in
E^{\prime}
emanating from
p_{i}
, and let
e^{j}_{q}
be the unique edge connecting to
q_{j}
, and let
f_{X,Y}:E^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}
be the function with
f_{X,Y}(e^{i}_{p})=X_{i}
for
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
,
f_{X,Y}(e^{j}_{q})=Y_{j}
for
j\in\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket
, and
f_{X,Y}(e)=0
for all other
e\in E^{\prime}
. Then

d_{Z+f_{X,Y}}(p_{i},q_{j})=X_{i}+\mathcal{L}(z^{+}_{1,i},r_{1}+2/n;z^{-}_{2,j},r_{2}-2/n)+Y_{j}.

Hence by Lemma
6.2
, the weight vector

(\mathcal{L}(p;Q_{1}(1),r_{1}-1/n),\dots,\mathcal{L}(p;Q_{1}(k),r_{1}-1/n)),\mathcal{L}(Q_{2}(1),r_{2}+1/n;q),\dots,\mathcal{L}(Q_{2}(\ell),r_{2}+1/n;q))

lies in some
(c(G^{\prime})+1)
-dimensional affine space
W=W(Z+f_{X,Y})
. By the definition of
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\sigma)
,
G^{\prime}
is an interior forest for
G
so
c(G^{\prime})+1=(k+\ell-|V|)/2+2
by Corollary
6.3
.
Moreover, there must also exist geodesic stars from
p
to
(Q_{1},r_{1}-1/n)
and from
(Q_{2},r_{2}+1/n)
to
q
. In summary, using the notation of introduced at the beginning of Section
4
we have shown that

N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\sigma)\subset\operatorname{Star}(Q_{1},r_{1}-1/n;Q_{2},r_{2}+1/n;W).

From here we will complete the proof using either Proposition
4.4
(for the dimension bound) or Lemma
4.9
(for the countability claim when
d(G)=0
).

Step 3: The dimension bound.
First, observe that if
Z+f_{X,Y}
were independent of the function

\Theta(\cdot):=\operatorname{Star}(Q_{1},r_{1}-1/n;Q_{2},r_{2}+1/n;\cdot),

(63)

then by Proposition
4.4
and the fact that
\dim(W(Z+f_{X,Y}))=2+(k+\ell-|V|)/2
we would have that almost surely,

\displaystyle\dim_{1:2:3}(N_{\mathcal{L}}(G))

\displaystyle\leq\dim_{1:2:3}(\Theta(W(Z+f_{X,Y}))

\displaystyle\leq 12+\frac{k+\ell-|V|}{2}-\frac{k(k+1)}{2}-\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{2}=d(G).

However, there is a subtlety because
Z+f_{X,Y}
is not independent of
\Theta
. To deal with this, we use Lemma
6.6
to show that the law of
(\Theta,Z+f_{X,Y})
is absolutely continuous with respect to the law of a pair of independent random variables.
First, observe that
\Theta

is measurable given the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{1}
generated by
\mathcal{L}
restricted to the following strips:

(\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,r_{1}-1/n])^{2}\cap\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow},\qquad(\mathbb{R}\times[r_{2}+1/n,\infty))^{2}\cap\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}.

(64)

Next, the weight function
Z
(which depends on the identities of the random points
z^{+}_{1,i},z^{-}_{1,j}
) is measurable given the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{2}
generated by
\mathcal{L}
restricted to the set

(\mathbb{R}\times[r_{1}+1/n,r_{2}-1/n])^{2},

and hence is independent of
\Theta
. Next, the functions
F_{1,i},F_{2,j},G_{1,i},G_{2,j}
are measurable given
\sigma(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2})
. Therefore since
\mathcal{S}^{1},\mathcal{S}^{2}
are independent of each other and of
\sigma(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2})
, by Lemma
6.6
, given
\sigma(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2})
, the vector
(X_{1},\dots,X_{k},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{\ell})
has a Lebesgue density. Therefore if
(N,N^{\prime})=(N_{1},\dots,N_{k},N_{1}^{\prime},\dots,N_{\ell}^{\prime})
is a standard normal vector, independent of all else, the law of
(\Theta,Z+f_{X,Y})
is absolutely continuous with respect to the law of
(\Theta,Z+f_{N,N^{\prime}})
which is the desired result.

Step 4: Countability when
d(G)=0
.
First, by noting that
k,\ell\leq 3
and using Lemma
6.1
, we see that the only way to have
d(G)=0
is if
k=\ell=3,|V|=6
. Therefore
\dim W=2
. Since
W
is slide-invariant, recalling the map
L_{3}
from Lemma
4.9
, we have that

W=L_{3}^{-1}(\mathbf{b})\times L_{3}^{-1}(\mathbf{c})

for some
\mathbf{b},\mathbf{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Hence

\displaystyle|N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\sigma)|

\displaystyle\leq|\operatorname{Star}(Q_{1},r_{1}-1/n;Q_{2},r_{2}+1/n;L_{3}^{-1}(\mathbf{b})\times L_{3}^{-1}(\mathbf{c}))|

\displaystyle\leq|\operatorname{Star}(Q_{1},r_{1}-1/n;L_{3}^{-1}(\mathbf{b}))\times\operatorname{Star}_{R}(Q_{2},r_{2}+1/n;L_{3}^{-1}(\mathbf{c}))|.

Lemma
4.9
then gives that
|N_{\mathcal{L}}(G;\sigma)|\leq 135^{2}
, as desired.
∎

Proposition 6.8
.

For all
G\in\mathscr{G}
, a.s. we have

\dim_{1:2:3}(N_{\mathcal{L}}(G))\geq d(G),

and if
d(G)=0
, then
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G)
is infinite.

Together with Proposition
6.7
and the results of Section
5
, Proposition
6.8
yields Theorem
1.1
and parts
1
and
2
of Theorem
1.2
. See Figure
12
for a sketch of the proof.

Figure 12
:
Elements of the construction in the proof of Proposition
6.8
. The basic idea is that on the set
S
(shaded in green) we plant an interior forest from
(\mathbf{x},0)
to
(\mathbf{y},1)
by an application of Lemma
6.4
. On the yellow sets
T_{\gamma}^{1},T_{\gamma}^{2}
, we use Lemma
6.5
to plant small favourable regions around the points
x_{i},y_{j}
, indicated in purple. Geodesics moving through the strip
\mathbb{R}\times(-\gamma,1+\gamma)
will use these regions, and by a coalescence argument will also use essentially follow paths in the planted interior forest. Given this construction, we will look for pairs
(p;q)\in(-\infty,-\gamma)\times\mathbb{R}\times(1+\gamma,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
which support geodesic stars whose weights are chosen so that every path through the planted forest has equal weight. Proposition
4.1
will yield a lower bound on the dimension of such pairs.

Proof.

Fix
G=(V,E)\in\mathscr{G}
.
By ergodic properties of
\mathcal{L}
(Remark
2.3
), it is enough to show that with positive probability,

\dim_{1:2:3}(N_{\mathcal{L}}(G))\geq d(G)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad N_{\mathcal{L}}(G)\neq\emptyset.

(65)

We will construct an event where (
65
) holds. We first construct events for
\mathcal{L}
restricted to each of the five strips

\displaystyle S:=(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1])^{2}

\displaystyle\cap\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow},\qquad T^{\gamma}_{1}:=(\mathbb{R}\times[-\gamma,0])^{2}\cap\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow},\quad T^{\gamma}_{2}:=(\mathbb{R}\times[1,1+\gamma])^{2}\cap\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow},

\displaystyle R^{\gamma}_{1}

\displaystyle:=(\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,-\gamma])^{2}\cap\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow},\quad R^{\gamma}_{2}:=(\mathbb{R}\times[1+\gamma,\infty))^{2}\cap\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow},

where
\gamma>0
is a small parameter whose value we set later.
Note that
\mathcal{L}
is independent on each of these five strips, so random elements defined on one strip do not affect the distribution on any other. To set these events, let
G^{\prime}
be any interior forest for
G
, and let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{<},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Q}^{\ell}_{<}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
be defined as in Lemma
6.4
for the forest
G^{\prime}
with
\alpha=1000
. Note that these random variables only depend on
\mathcal{L}|_{S}
.

Our events will be constructed in terms of small positive parameters
\delta,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime},\gamma
and large positive parameters
\beta,\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}
that may depend on
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
. The relationships between these parameters are given throughout the constructions in (
66
), (
67
), and (
68
).

Event
1
:
\mathcal{L}|_{S}
.
Let
A(\delta,\epsilon^{\prime})
denote the event where the following conditions hold:

(I)

We have
x_{i}-x_{i-1}>\delta
and
y_{j}-y_{j-1}>\delta
for
i\in\llbracket 2,k\rrbracket,j\in\llbracket 2,\ell\rrbracket
. Moreover,
|x_{i}|,|a_{i}|,|b_{i}|,|y_{i}|
are bounded above by
\delta^{-1}
for any
i
.

(II)

In the embedding of
G^{\prime}
as the set

\Gamma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},G^{\prime}):=\bigcup_{(i,j)\in\operatorname{Adm}(G^{\prime})}\Gamma(x_{i},0;y_{j},1),

no vertices are sent to the strips
\mathbb{R}\times(0,\delta]
and
[1-\delta,1)\times\mathbb{R}
.

(III)

For every
(i,j)\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket
, let
\pi^{i,j}
denote the unique geodesic from
(x_{i},0)
to
(y_{j},1)
. Then if
|x-x_{i}|<\epsilon^{\prime}
and
|y-y_{j}|<\epsilon^{\prime}
, then
any geodesic
\pi
from
(x,0)
to
(y,1)
satisfies
[\delta,1-\delta]\subset O(\pi,\pi^{i,j})
.

Conditions (I) and (II) hold with positive probability for all
\delta<\delta_{0}
. Condition (III) holds for all
\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon^{\prime}_{0}(\delta)
by Proposition
3.5
(ii) and the continuity of
\mathcal{L}
.
Therefore
\mathbb{P}A(\delta,\epsilon^{\prime})>0
for all

\delta<\delta_{0},\qquad\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon^{\prime}_{0}(\delta).

(66)

Event
2
:
\mathcal{L}|_{R^{\gamma}_{1}},\mathcal{L}|_{R^{\gamma}_{2}}
, and a shape constraint.
Define the event
B(\gamma,\delta,\beta,\beta^{\prime},\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
as follows:

1.

If
k=3
, then

\mathcal{L}(0,-\gamma-1;x_{i},-\gamma)-\mathcal{L}(0,-\gamma-1;x_{2},-\gamma)>200,

for
i=1,3
. Similarly, if
\ell=3
, then

\mathcal{L}(y_{i},1+\gamma;0,2+\gamma)-\mathcal{L}(y_{2},1+\gamma;0,2+\gamma)>200,

for
i=1,3
. These conditions guarantee that
(-100,100)^{3}\subset S^{-}(\mathbf{x},-\gamma)
if
k=3
and
(-100,100)^{3}\subset S^{+}(\mathbf{y},1+\gamma)
if
\ell=3
, where
S^{-}(\mathbf{x},-\gamma),S^{+}(\mathbf{y},1+\gamma)
are as in Proposition
4.1
.

2.

Define the compact sets

K^{1}_{\beta}=[-\beta,\beta]\times[-1-\gamma,-\beta^{-1}-\gamma],\qquad K^{2}_{\beta}=[-\beta,\beta]\times[1+\beta^{-1}+\gamma,2+\gamma]

and let
K_{\beta}=K^{1}_{\beta}\times K^{2}_{\beta}
.
For every slide-invariant affine set
W\subset\mathbb{R}^{k+\ell}
with dimension
(k+\ell-|V|)/2+2
and satisfying

W\cap(-100,100)^{k+\ell}\neq\emptyset,

we have

\displaystyle\dim_{1:2:3}[\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},-\gamma;\mathbf{y},1+\gamma;g(W))\cap K_{\beta}\geq d(G),

where
g(W)
is the smallest slide-invariant set containing
W\cap(-100,100)^{k+\ell}
.

3.

For all points
p\in K^{1}_{\beta}
,
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, and
x
with
|x-x_{i}|\leq\epsilon^{\prime}
, any geodesic from
p
to
(x,-\gamma)
overlaps with the unique geodesic
\tau^{i}
from
(0,-2)
to
(x_{i},-\gamma)
at time

. Similarly, for all points

,

and

with

, any geodesic from
(y,1+\gamma)
to

overlaps with the unique geodesic
\sigma^{i}
from
(y_{i},1+\gamma)
to
(0,2)
at time
1+\gamma+\epsilon
. The vectors
\tau(-\gamma-\epsilon),\sigma(1+\gamma+\epsilon)
are included in Figure
12
.

4.

For any point
p\in K^{1}_{\beta}
(or
q\in K^{2}_{\beta}
) the function
\mathcal{L}_{p}(y):=\mathcal{L}(p;y,-\gamma)
(or
\mathcal{L}_{q}(y):=\mathcal{L}(y,1+\gamma;q)
) is Hölder-
1/3
with constant
\beta^{\prime}
on the interval
[-\delta^{-1},\delta^{-1}]
. Moreover, for
u=(x,t;y,t+s)\in R^{\gamma}_{1}\cup R^{\gamma}_{2}\cup S
we have the general shape estimate

\left|\mathcal{L}(u)+\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{s}\right|\leq\beta^{\prime}s^{1/3}\log^{4/3}\left(\frac{2(\|u\|_{\infty}+2)^{3/2}}{s}\right)\log^{2/3}(\|u\|_{\infty}+2).

Given arbitrary
\delta,\gamma
, we claim that
A(\delta,\epsilon^{\prime})\cap B(\gamma,\delta,\beta,\beta^{\prime},\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
holds with positive probability whenever
\mathbb{P}A(\delta,\epsilon^{\prime})>0
and

\beta>\beta_{0},\qquad\beta^{\prime}>\beta^{\prime}_{0}(\beta,\delta),\qquad\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}(\beta),\qquad\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon_{0}^{\prime}(\epsilon,\beta).

(67)

Condition on the values of
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
. Under this condition, point
1
holds with positive probability by the Brownian Gibbs property (Lemma
2.8
). Point
2
holds with positive probability given
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
and point

for large enough

by Proposition

. The condition (

) is simplified because

if

and

if

. Point

holds almost surely for all small enough

and
\epsilon^{\prime}
small enough given

since almost surely, there are no rational
2
-stars points (Theorem
1.5
) and the local compactness of the overlap topology (Proposition

). Given the event
A(\delta,\epsilon^{\prime})
and Conditions
1,2,3,

condition
4
holds almost surely for large enough

by Theorem
2.5
. Note that by independence and stationarity of time increments that there is no dependence on
\gamma
in (
67
).

Event
3
:
\mathcal{L}|_{T^{\gamma}_{1}}
and
\mathcal{L}|_{T^{\gamma}_{2}}
.
Define

\mathcal{S}^{1}_{\gamma}(x,y)=\mathcal{L}(x,-\gamma;y,0),\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\gamma}(x,y)=\mathcal{L}(y,1;x,1+\gamma),

and with notation as in Lemma

let

C(\gamma,\epsilon^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime})=T(\mathcal{S}^{1}_{\gamma};\mathbf{x},1,\mathbf{a}+\beta^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{1},\epsilon^{\prime},\gamma^{1/3})\cap T(\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\gamma};\mathbf{y},1,\mathbf{b}+\beta^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{1},\epsilon^{\prime},\gamma^{1/3}).

Lemma
6.5
and landscape rescaling guarantees that given

, the event

holds with positive probability for all

\gamma<\gamma_{0},\qquad\beta^{\prime\prime}>1.

(68)

Final Step: Constructing the networks.
We work on the event

A(\delta,\epsilon^{\prime})\cap B(\gamma,\delta,\beta,\beta^{\prime},\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})\cap C(\gamma,\epsilon^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime})

which holds with positive probability under conditions (
66
), (
67
), and (
68
).
We may also assume that the parameters have the relative sizes

(\beta^{\prime\prime})^{-1}\ll\gamma\ll\epsilon^{\prime}\ll\epsilon\ll\delta\wedge\beta^{-1}\wedge(\beta^{\prime})^{-1}.

(69)

First, for every
(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, let
\mathcal{L}^{i}_{j}(p;q)
be the supremum of
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}}
over paths
\pi
from
p
to
q
satisfying

\max(|\pi(-\gamma)-x_{i}|,|\pi(0)-x_{i}|)\leq\epsilon^{\prime},\qquad\max(|\pi(1)-y_{j}|,|\pi(1+\gamma)-y_{j}|)\leq\epsilon^{\prime}.

(70)

We write
\mathcal{L}^{i}(p;q)
if we only impose the first inequality and
\mathcal{L}_{j}(p;q)
if we only impose the second. We will only use these definitions when the relevant times
-\gamma,0,1,1+\gamma
are in the domain of
\pi
. First, by the assumptions (
69
), the shape conditions for
\mathcal{S}^{1}_{\gamma},\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\gamma}
imposed by
C(\gamma,\epsilon^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime})
, and the shape condition in
4
, for any
(p;q)\in K_{\beta}
we have

\mathcal{L}(p;q)=\max_{i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,j\in\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket}\mathcal{L}^{i}_{j}(p;q).

(71)

Next, by upper semi-continuity of path length, the supremum
\mathcal{L}^{i}_{j}(p;q)
is always attained by at least one path
\pi^{i,j}_{p,q}
. Moreover,
\pi^{i,j}_{p,q}
is a geodesic on each of the five intervals

[s,-\gamma],\quad[-\gamma,0],\quad[0,1],\quad[1,1+\gamma],\quad[1+\gamma,t].

In particular, if
(p;q)\in K_{\beta}
, then by points (III),
3
we have

\displaystyle\pi^{i,j}_{p,q}(-\gamma-\epsilon)=\tau^{i}(-\gamma-\epsilon),\qquad

\displaystyle\pi^{i,j}_{p,q}(1+\gamma+\epsilon)=\sigma^{j}(1+\gamma+\epsilon)

(72)

\displaystyle\pi^{i,j}_{p,q}(\delta)=\pi^{i,j}(\delta),\qquad

\displaystyle\pi^{i,j}_{p,q}(1-\delta)=\pi^{i,j}(1-\delta),

(73)

and so
\mathcal{L}^{i,j}(p;q)
is equal to

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}(p;\bar{\tau}^{i}(-\gamma-\epsilon))&+\mathcal{L}^{i}(\bar{\tau}^{i}(-\gamma-\epsilon);\bar{\pi}^{i,j}(\delta))+\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}^{i,j}(\delta),\bar{\pi}^{i,j}(1-\delta))\\
&+\mathcal{L}_{j}(\bar{\pi}^{i,j}(1-\delta);\bar{\sigma}^{j}(1+\gamma+\epsilon))+\mathcal{L}(\bar{\sigma}^{j}(1+\gamma+\epsilon);q).\end{split}

(74)

Now, define

\displaystyle X_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}^{i}(\bar{\tau}^{i}(-\gamma-\epsilon);\bar{\pi}^{i,j}(\delta))-\mathcal{L}(\bar{\tau}^{i}(-\gamma-\epsilon);x_{i},-\gamma)-\mathcal{L}(x_{i},0;\bar{\pi}^{i,j}(\delta)),

\displaystyle\qquad Y_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{j}(\bar{\pi}^{i,j}(1-\delta);\bar{\sigma}^{j}(1+\gamma+\epsilon))-\mathcal{L}(y_{j},1+\gamma;\bar{\sigma}^{j}(1+\gamma+\epsilon))-\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}^{i,j}(1-\delta),y_{j},1).

Then by points
3
and (III) again we can rewrite (
74
) to get

\mathcal{L}^{i,j}(p;q)=\mathcal{L}(p;x_{i},-\gamma)+X_{i}+\mathcal{L}(x_{i},0;y_{j},1)+Y_{j}+\mathcal{L}(y_{j},1+\gamma;q).

(75)

Now, observe that by the Hölder continuity condition in
4
and the shape conditions for
\mathcal{S}^{1}_{\gamma},\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\gamma}
imposed by
C(\gamma,\epsilon^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime})
, as long as
\epsilon^{\prime}
is set small enough given
\beta^{\prime}
we have

X_{i}\in[a_{i}+\beta^{\prime\prime}-1,a_{i}+\beta^{\prime\prime}+2],\qquad Y_{j}\in[b_{j}+\beta^{\prime\prime}-1,b_{j}+\beta^{\prime\prime}+2],

(76)

and so from the definition of
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}
we have

X_{i}+\mathcal{L}(x_{i},0;y_{j},1)+Y_{j}\in(-2+2\beta^{\prime\prime},4+2\beta^{\prime\prime})

(77)

whenever
(i,j)\in\operatorname{Adm}(G^{\prime})
and

X_{i}+\mathcal{L}(x_{i},0;y_{j},1)+Y_{j}<-900+2\beta^{\prime\prime}

(78)

otherwise. Our next aim is to appeal to Lemma
6.2
. Let
p_{1},\dots,p_{k},q_{1},\dots,q_{\ell}
denote the source and sink vertices in
G^{\prime}
. We can define a weight map
Z:E(G^{\prime})\to\mathbb{R}
so that

d_{Z}(p_{i},q_{j})=X_{i}+\mathcal{L}(x_{i},0;y_{j},1)+Y_{j}

for all
(i,j)\in\operatorname{Adm}(G^{\prime})
. Indeed, we can first weight all edges according their lengths in the embedding of
G^{\prime}
as the set
\Gamma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},G^{\prime})
, and then add
X_{i}
to the weight of the edge emanating from
p_{i}
and
Y_{j}
to the weight of the edge coming into
q_{j}
for all
i,j
. By Lemma
6.2
and Corollary
6.3
, the set
W(Z)
defined in that lemma has dimension
(k+\ell-|V|)/2+2
. Moreover, by (
77
),
W(Z)\cap(-100,100)^{k+\ell}\neq\emptyset
so by point
2
we have

\displaystyle\dim_{1:2:3}[T(Z)]\geq d(G),\quad\text{ where }\quad T(Z):=\operatorname{Star}(\mathbf{x},-\gamma;\mathbf{y},1+\gamma;g(W(Z)))\cap K_{\beta}.

For
(p;q)\in T(Z)
, by (
75
) all of the values
\mathcal{L}^{i}_{j}(p;q)
are equal to a common value
L(p;q)
for
(p;q)\in\operatorname{Adm}(G^{\prime})
. Moreover, by (
78
) we have
\mathcal{L}^{i}_{j}(p;q)<L(p;q)
for
(i,j)\notin\operatorname{Adm}(G^{\prime})
. Hence by (
71
), for all
(p;q)\in T(Z)
we have

\mathcal{L}(p;q)=L[p,q]=\mathcal{L}^{i}_{j}(p;q)

for all
(i,j)\in\operatorname{Adm}(G^{\prime})
. Now, by (
72
), the geodesic network
\Gamma(p;q)
contains as a subset

\bigcup_{(i,j)\in\operatorname{Adm}(G^{\prime})}\Gamma(\bar{\pi}^{i,j}(\delta),\bar{\pi}^{i,j}(1-\delta)).

By point (II), this set is a directed embedding of
G^{\prime}
. Next, by (
72
)
\Gamma(p;q)
contains all of the points

\bar{\tau}^{i}(-\gamma-\epsilon),i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,\qquad\bar{\sigma}^{j}(1+\gamma+\epsilon),j\in\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket.

Since there are geodesic
k
- and
\ell
-star from
p
to
(\mathbf{x},-\gamma)
and from
(\mathbf{y},1+\gamma)
to
q
, respectively, point
3
implies that there are geodesic
k
- and
\ell
-star from
p
to
\bar{\tau}(-\gamma-\epsilon)
and from
\bar{\sigma}(1+\gamma+\epsilon)
to
q
. These geodesic stars are also contained in
\Gamma(p;q)
. Hence
\Gamma(p;q)
contains a directed embedding of
G
, so
G
is a subgraph of
G(p;q)
. In summary, we have shown that with positive probability:

\dim_{1:2:3}\bigcup_{H\in\mathscr{G}:G\subset H}(N_{\mathcal{L}}(H))\geq d(G),\qquad\bigcup_{H\in\mathscr{G}:G\subset H}(N_{\mathcal{L}}(H))\neq\emptyset.

(79)

Now, if
G
is such that
d(G)=0
, then the only graph
H\in\mathscr{G}
containing
G
as a subgraph is
G
itself, so (
79
) implies (
65
). If
d(G)>0
, then for any graph
H\neq G
in
\mathscr{G}
with
G\subset H
we have
d(G)<d(H)
. Therefore combining (
79
) with the upper bound in Proposition
6.7
yields (
65
).
∎

7
Dense geodesic networks

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.2
.3, which classifies the dense and nowhere dense networks in
\mathcal{L}
. It is easier to show that if a graph
G\in\mathscr{G}
cannot be disconnected by removing a single edge, then
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G)
is nowhere dense. This follows from the next lemma.

Lemma 7.1
.

Suppose that
G=(V,E)\in\mathscr{G}
and that there is no edge
e\in E
such that the graph
G^{\prime}=(V,E\setminus\{e\})
is disconnected (as an undirected graph). Then almost surely, the closure
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muN_{\mathcal{L}}(G)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
of
N_{\mathcal{L}}(G)
satisfies
\dim_{1:2:3}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muN_{\mathcal{L}}(G)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)\leq 7
.

Proof.

Suppose that
G
cannot be disconnected along a single edge. Then we claim there are two edge-disjoint directed paths in
G
connecting the source and sink vertices
p
and
q
. Indeed, consider any directed embedding
\Phi(G)
of
G
, and consider the leftmost and rightmost paths
\pi,\tau
from
p
to
q
in this embedding. If these paths meet at a point away from the vertices
p,q
then they must meet on an edge since all interior vertices in
G
have degree
3
. Removing this edge would necessarily disconnect
G
, so the paths
\pi,\tau
must be disjoint away from
p,q
, as desired.

Therefore if
(p_{n},q_{n})\in N_{\mathcal{L}}(G)
is a sequence converging to some
(p;q)
then there are at least two disjoint geodesics
\pi^{n},\tau^{n}
from
p_{n}
to
q_{n}
for all
n
. Each of the sequences
\pi^{n},\tau^{n}
is precompact in the overlap topology on geodesics by Proposition
3.5
, so there is a subsequential overlap limit
(\pi,\tau)
of
(\pi^{n},\tau^{n})
and both
\pi,\tau
are geodesics from
p
to
q
. Since overlap convergence preserves disjointness,
\pi
and
\tau
are disjoint. Therefore
(p;q)\in N_{\mathcal{L}}(G^{\prime})
for some
G^{\prime}\in\mathscr{G}
with source and sink degree at least
2
. By Theorem
1.2
,
\dim(N_{\mathcal{L}}(G^{\prime}))\leq 7
, yielding the lemma.
∎

Now, for every pair
k,\ell\in\llbracket 1,3\rrbracket
, it is not hard to check that there is exactly one graph
G\in\mathscr{G}
that can be disconnected by a single edge. This follows from the full enumeration in Section
8
, though it is also an easy exercise for the reader. We call this graph
G_{k,\ell}
. All six choices of
G_{k,\ell}
with
k\leq\ell
are enumerated in Figure
3
. The goal of the lemmas in the remainder of this section is to show that each of these networks is dense. For these lemmas, we write
B_{\epsilon}(u)
for the Euclidean ball of radius
\epsilon>0
centered at
u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
be any fixed point, and let
\epsilon>0
. Then almost surely, there are points
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in B_{\epsilon}(p)
such that
G(p_{k},q)=G^{k,1}
for
k=1,2,3
.

To prove Lemma
7.2
, we need a simple fact about Brownian motions.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
W:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
be any function whose law is absolutely continuous with respect to that of a
2
-sided Brownian motion on
[-1,1]
. For every
\delta>0
, define
W^{\delta}:[-\delta^{-2},\delta^{-2}]\to\mathbb{R}
by
W^{\delta}(y)=\delta^{-1}B(\delta^{2}y)
, and for
\alpha>0
let
T_{\delta}(W^{\delta},\alpha)
be the event where

|x|^{2/3}>W_{\delta}(x)+\alpha,\qquad x\in[-\delta^{-2},-\alpha^{-1}]\cup[\alpha^{-1},\delta^{-2}].

(80)

Then for any fixed sequence
\delta_{n}\to 0
and any fixed
\alpha>0
, almost surely we have

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}(T_{\delta_{n}}(W^{\delta_{n}},\alpha))>0.

Proof.

First, since the claim in the lemma is an almost sure statement, it suffices to prove it when
W
is a Brownian motion. Extend
W
to a
2
-sided Brownian motion on all of
\mathbb{R}
, and let
\tilde{T}(W^{\delta},\alpha)
be the event where (
80
) holds for all
x\in\mathbb{R},|x|\geq\alpha^{-1}
. Now, Brownian motion is strong mixing with respect to the Brownian scaling
W\mapsto W^{\delta}
, so

\mathbb{P}(\tilde{T}(W,\alpha))=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}(\tilde{T}(W^{\delta_{n}},\alpha))\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}(T_{\delta_{n}}(W^{\delta_{n}},\alpha)).

Finally, it is easy to check that
\mathbb{P}(\tilde{T}(W,\alpha))>0
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
7.2
.

Fix
k\in\llbracket 1,3\rrbracket
. By landscape rescaling, we may assume
(p;q)=(0,0;0,1)
. Also, almost surely, no rational points are
2
-stars by Theorem
1.5
. We assume this during the proof. Define

\mathcal{L}^{\delta}(x,s;y,t)=\delta^{-1}\mathcal{L}(\delta^{2}x,\delta^{3}s;\delta^{2}y,\delta^{3}t),\qquad\mathfrak{A}^{\delta}(y)=\delta^{-1}[\mathcal{L}(\delta^{2}y,0;0,1)-\mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)].

For
s<0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, suppose there is a unique argmax
M_{x,s}
of the function

f_{x,s}(y):=\mathcal{L}^{\delta}(x,s;y,0)+\mathfrak{A}^{\delta}(y).

Then all
\mathcal{L}
-geodesics from
(\delta^{2}x,\delta^{3}s)
to
(0,1)
go through the point
(\delta^{2}M_{x,s},0)
. Since
(\delta^{2}M_{x,s},0)
is an interior geodesic point and
(0,1)
is not a
2
-star, there is a unique geodesic from
(\delta^{2}M_{x,s},0)
to
(0,1)
since otherwise we could find a geodesic bubble, contradicting Lemma
3.3
.3. Therefore if

G_{\mathcal{L}^{\delta}}(x,s;M_{x,s},0)=G_{\mathcal{L}}(\delta^{2}x,\delta^{3}s;\delta^{2}M_{x,s},0)=G^{k,1}

then
G_{\mathcal{L}}(\delta^{2}x,\delta^{3}s;0,1)=G^{k,1}
as well. Here
G_{\mathcal{L}^{\delta}},G_{\mathcal{L}}
indicate network graphs in
\mathcal{L}^{\delta},\mathcal{L}
.
Now, fix
\delta>0
. Since each
\mathcal{L}^{\delta}
is a directed landscape, with positive probability the following two events occur simultaneously. Here
c,c^{\prime},\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}>0
are absolute constants.

1.

There exists a (random)
p_{\delta}=(x_{\delta},s_{\delta})\in[-c,c]\times[-c,-\gamma]
such that
(p_{\delta};0,0)\in N_{\mathcal{L}^{\delta}}(G^{k,1})
. Moreover, there exists
y_{\delta}\in\mathbb{R}
such that if
\pi
is any geodesic from
p_{\delta}
to a point in
(-\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})\times\{0\}
, then
\pi(-\gamma)=y_{\delta}
.

2.

For all
y\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\left|\mathcal{L}^{\delta}(p_{\delta};y,0)+\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{|s_{\delta}|}\right|\leq c^{\prime}\log^{2}(2+|y|).

To see why point
1
above holds with positive probability for large
c
and small
\gamma
, observe that
N_{\mathcal{L}^{\delta}}(G^{k,1})
is almost surely non-empty (Theorem
1.1
) and that if
u\in N_{\mathcal{L}^{\delta}}(G^{k,1})
then necessarily
v\in N_{\mathcal{L}^{\delta}}(G^{k,1})
for all rational
v
in a small ball around
u
(this follows from Proposition
3.5
and the fact that no rational points are
2
-stars). Together with the symmetries of
\mathcal{L}
, these points imply that
N_{\mathcal{L}^{\delta}}(G^{k,1})
contains a point of the form
(p;0,0)
with
p\in[-c,c]\times[-c,-\gamma]
with positive probability, and that all geodesics from
p
to
(0,0)
coincide at time
-\gamma
at some location
y_{0}
.
A second application of Proposition
3.5
.2 implies that for small enough
\gamma^{\prime}>0
, any geodesic
\pi
from
p
to a point in
(-\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})\times\{0\}
also satisfies
\pi(-\gamma)=y_{0}
. The second point holds with positive probability given the first point by the landscape shape theorem (Theorem
2.5
) as long as
c^{\prime}
is sufficiently large.

Now, let
S_{\delta}
be the event where the two points above hold. Since
\mathcal{L}
is ergodic with respect to
1:2:3
rescaling (Remark
2.3
) we can find a random sequence
\delta_{n}\to 0
such that
S_{\delta_{n}}
holds for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover, by Theorem
2.5
, we can find
C>0
such that

|\mathfrak{A}^{1}(y)+y^{2}|\leq C\log^{2}(2+|y|)

for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore by point
2
above, for all large enough
n
, any argmax of the function
f_{p_{\delta_{n}}}
is attained on the set
[-\delta^{-2},\delta^{-2}]
. Moreover, by Lemma
2.8
the law of the function
\mathfrak{A}^{1}|_{[-1,1]}-\mathfrak{A}^{1}(0)
is absolutely continuous with respect to a
2
-sided Brownian motion. By independence of landscape time increments,
\mathfrak{A}^{1}
is also independent of the choice of the sequence
\delta_{n}
, and all the functions
y\mapsto\mathcal{L}^{\delta_{n}}(p_{\delta_{n}};y,0)
. Therefore by Lemma
7.3
, almost surely for all
\alpha>0
, there are infinitely many values of
n\in\mathbb{N}
for which
T_{\delta_{n}}(\mathfrak{A}^{\delta_{n}},\alpha)
holds. Combining this with point
2
above, we find that there is a subsequence
Y\subset\mathbb{N}
such that the argmax of the function
f_{p_{\delta_{n}}}
is attained on the set
(-\gamma,\gamma)
for all
n\in Y
.

Next, since
\mathfrak{A}^{\delta_{n}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to a
2
-sided Brownian motion on
[-\gamma,\gamma]
, and is independent of the function
y\mapsto\mathcal{L}^{\delta_{n}}(p_{\delta_{n}};y,0)
, almost surely the function
f_{p_{\delta_{n}}}
has a unique argmax on
[-\gamma,\gamma]
for all
n
. Therefore for all
n\in Y
,
f_{p_{\delta_{n}}}
has a unique global argmax
M_{p_{\delta_{n}}}
, attained in
(-\gamma,\gamma)
. Now, by the second part of point
1
for the event
S_{\delta_{n}}
, a path is a geodesic from
p_{\delta_{n}}
to
(M_{p_{\delta_{n}}},0)
if and only if it can be written as a concatenation
\pi|_{[s_{\delta},-\gamma]}\oplus\tau
, where
\pi
is a geodesic from
p_{\delta_{n}}
to
(y_{\delta_{n}},-\gamma)
and
\tau
is a geodesic from
(y_{\delta_{n}},-\gamma)
to
(M_{p_{\delta_{n}}},0)
. The choice of
\tau
is unique by Lemma
3.3
.3, and so

G_{\mathcal{L}^{\delta_{n}}}(p_{\delta_{n}};M_{p_{\delta_{n}}},0)=G_{\mathcal{L}^{\delta_{n}}}(p_{\delta_{n}};y_{\delta_{n}},-\gamma)=G_{\mathcal{L}^{\delta_{n}}}(p_{\delta_{n}};0,0)=G^{k,1}.

Hence
G_{\mathcal{L}}(\delta^{2}x_{\delta_{n}},\delta^{3}s_{\delta_{n}};0,1)=G^{k,1}
for all
n\in Y
, yielding the result.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
u=(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
be any fixed point, and let
\epsilon>0
. Then almost surely, there are points
u_{k,\ell}\in B_{\epsilon}(u)
for
k\leq\ell\in\llbracket 1,3\rrbracket
such that
G(u_{k,\ell})=G^{k,\ell}
for
i=1,2,3
.

Theorem
1.2
.3 follows from combining Lemma
7.1
and Lemma
7.4
.

Proof.

By landscape rescaling, it suffices to prove the lemma with
p,q=(0,0;0,1)
. Almost surely, there is a unique geodesic
\pi
from
p
to
q
. Now, by Proposition
3.5
.2, for all small enough
\delta>0
, the following three statements hold:

1.

If
(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})\in B_{\delta}(u)
and
\tau
is a geodesic from
p^{\prime}
to
q^{\prime}
, then
[1/4,3/4]\subset O(\pi,\tau)
.

2.

If
(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})\in B_{\delta}((p,\bar{\pi}(1/2)))
and
\tau
is a geodesic from
p^{\prime}
to
q^{\prime}
, then
1/4\in O(\pi,\tau)
.

3.

If
(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})\in B_{\delta}((\bar{\pi}(1/2),q))
and
\tau
is a geodesic from
p^{\prime}
to
q^{\prime}
, then
3/4\in O(\pi,\tau)
.

Now, by Lemma
7.2
almost surely for every rational point
w\in B_{\delta/2}(\bar{\pi}(1/2))
we can find points
(p_{k},q_{\ell})\in B_{\delta/2}(u)\cap B_{\epsilon}(u)
such that
G(p_{k},w)=G^{k,1},G(w,q_{\ell})=G^{1,\ell}
. By point
2
above, all geodesics from
p_{k}
to
w
must merge in the time interval
(-\infty,1/4]
and must equal
\pi(1/4)
at time
1/4
. Therefore
G(p_{k},\bar{\pi}(1/4))=G^{k,1}
. Similarly, point
3
yields that
G(\bar{\pi}(3/4),q_{\ell})=G^{1,\ell}
. By point
1
, geodesics from
p_{k}
to
q_{\ell}
are exactly paths of the form
\tau_{p}\oplus\pi|_{[1/4,3/4]}\oplus\tau_{q}
where
\tau_{p}
is a geodesic from
p_{k}
to
\bar{\pi}(1/4)
and
\tau_{q}
is a geodesic
\bar{\pi}(3/4)
to
q_{\ell}
. Therefore
G(p_{k},q_{\ell})=G^{k,\ell}
, as desired.
∎

8
Geodesic Botany

In this final section, we enumerate the networks in
\mathscr{G}
. The proof of the enumeration is straightforward but somewhat tedious. The reader may find it more useful and interesting to simply peruse the figures of each of the
27
networks contained at the end of the section.

Proposition 8.1
.

We have
|\mathscr{G}|=27
. Moreover, letting
\mathscr{G}_{k,\ell}\subset\mathscr{G}

be the set of networks with
\{\deg(p),\deg(q)\}=\{k,\ell\}
, we have

|\mathscr{G}_{1,1}|=|\mathscr{G}_{1,2}|=|\mathscr{G}_{1,3}|=1,\qquad|\mathscr{G}_{2,2}|=3,\qquad|\mathscr{G}_{2,3}|=6,\qquad|\mathscr{G}_{3,3}|=15.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we may assume
\deg(p)=k\leq\deg(q)=\ell
since any
G\in\mathscr{G}
is transpose-isomorphic to a graph with these properties.
We will also repeatedly appeal to the Lemma
6.1
bound:

2+|k-\ell|\leq|V|\leq k+\ell.

(81)

Case 1.
\mathscr{G}_{1,\ell}
for
\ell=1,2,3
: one graph for each
\ell
.
By (
81
), any
G\in\mathscr{G}_{1,\ell}
has
1+\ell
vertices. Therefore:

•

Any
G\in\mathscr{G}_{1,1}
only has the two vertices
p
and
q
, and so the only element of
\mathscr{G}_{1,1}
is the path from
p
to
q
, see Figure
13
(a).

•

Any
G\in\mathscr{G}_{1,2}
has one interior vertex
v
in addition to
p,q
. The only way to obtain a graph in
\mathscr{G}
on these vertices with
k=1,\ell=2
is to connect
v
once to
p
and twice to
q
, see Figure
13
(b).

•

Any
G\in\mathscr{G}_{1,3}
has
4
vertices
\{p,q,v_{1},v_{2}\}
. Either
(p,v_{1})
or
(p,v_{2})
must lie in
E
since otherwise either
v_{1}
or
v_{2}
is a source. Up to isomorphism we may assume that
(p,v_{1})\in E
. Note that no other edges connect to
p
. Then
(v_{1},v_{2})\in E
since otherwise
v_{2}
would be a source. The edge
(v_{1},v_{2})
appears only once and
(v_{2},v_{1})
never appears, since otherwise
G\setminus\{p,q\}
would have a cycle. Therefore the third edge containing
v_{1}
is
(v_{1},q)
. We have examined all possible edges except
(v_{2},q)
; this edge must appear twice resulting in a unique graph
G
, see Figure
13
(c).

Case 2.
\mathscr{G}_{2,2}
:
3
total graphs.
By (
81
), every graph in
\mathscr{G}_{2,2}
has
2,3,
or
4
vertices. There is only
one
graph when
|V|=2
, where the edge
(p,q)
appears twice, see Figure
14
(a). There are no graphs on
3
vertices since then the total degree of
G
would be odd (
2+2+3=7
). Now suppose that
G=\{p,q,v_{1},v_{2}\}\in\mathscr{G}_{2,2}
. Since the set
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
has at most four edges connecting it to
\{p,q\}
but
\deg(v_{1})+\deg(v_{2})=6
, the vertices
v_{1},v_{2}
must be connected by an edge. The remaining four edges must go between the set
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
and the set
\{p,q\}
, and these remaining four edges must hit each of these vertices twice. Up to isomorphism, there are
two
possibilities: each of the edges
(p,v_{1}),(v_{1},q),(p,v_{2}),(v_{2},q)
are included once or the edges
(p,v_{1})
and
(v_{2},q)
are each included twice, see Figure
14
(b, c). This gives a total of
1+2=3
graphs in
\mathscr{G}_{2,2}
.

Case 3:
\mathscr{G}_{2,3}
.
By (
81
), every graph in
\mathscr{G}_{2,3}
has
3,4,
or
5
vertices. There is only
one
graph on three vertices: where we take the unique graph
G\in\mathscr{G}_{1,2}
and add an extra edge
(p,q)
, see Figure
15
(a). There are no graphs on four vertices since in this case the sum of degrees would be odd. Now suppose that
G=\{p,q,v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}\in\mathscr{G}_{2,3}
. Since there are at most
2+3=5
edges between the sets
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}
and
\{p,q\}
but
\deg(v_{1})+\deg(v_{2})+\deg(v_{3})=9
, there must be at least two edges in the set
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}^{2}
. Note also that there cannot be more than two edges, as this would create an undirected cycle. Therefore there are exactly two edges contained in
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}^{2}
, exactly five edges between the sets
\{p,q\}
and
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}
, and
(p,q)
is not an edge.
Up to relabeling the vertices, there are three possibilities for the pair of edges in
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}^{2}
:

\{(v_{1},v_{2}),(v_{1},v_{3})\},\{(v_{1},v_{2}),(v_{2},v_{3})\},\quad\;\text{and}\;\quad\{(v_{1},v_{2}),(v_{3},v_{2})\}.

In the
\{(v_{1},v_{2}),(v_{1},v_{3})\}
case, we must include edges
(p,v_{1}),(v_{2},q),(v_{3},q)
since otherwise either
v_{1}
is a source or
v_{2}
or
v_{3}
is a sink. The remaining two edges must have one connection to
v_{2}
and one connection to
v_{3}
. Since
\deg(p)=2
, we can either include the edges
(p,v_{2})
and
(v_{3},q)
again, or include
(p,v_{3})
and
(v_{2},q)
again. These choices are isomorphic and hence yield
one
graph, see Figure
15
(b).

In the
\{(v_{1},v_{2}),(v_{2},v_{3})\}
case, we must include edges
(p,v_{1})
and
(v_{3},q)
. There are three remaining edges to add to the graph, connecting between the sets
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}
and
\{p,q\}
. Since exactly one of these leaves from
p
, and the other two edges go to
q
this leaves
three
possible choices, see Figure
15
(c)-(e).

In the
\{(v_{1},v_{2}),(v_{3},v_{2})\}
case, we must include edges
(p,v_{1}),(p,v_{3})
and
(v_{2},q)
. Since
\deg(p)=2
and
\deg(q)=3
, there is only
one
way to complete the graph: by including the edges
(v_{3},q)
and
(v_{1},q)
, see Figure
15
(f).
Summarizing the whole analysis of
\mathscr{G}_{2,3}
gives a total of
1+1+3+1=6
graphs.

Case 4a:
\mathscr{G}_{3,3}
with
|V|<6
:
4
total graphs.
By (
81
),
|V|\in\llbracket 2,6\rrbracket
if
G\in\mathscr{G}_{3,3}
, and
|V|
is even since otherwise
G
would have odd total degree. There is
one
graph when
|V|=2
given by including
(p,q)

3
times, see Figure
16
(a). Now consider
|V|=4
and let
V=\{p,q,v_{1},v_{2}\}
. Suppose first that
(p,q)
is an edge. In this case, we can remove this edge to get a graph
G\in\mathscr{G}_{2,2}
with
|V|=4
: by our argument enumerating
\mathscr{G}_{2,2}
there are
two
such graphs, see Figure
16
(b, c).

Next suppose that
(p,q)
is not an edge. In this case, there are six edges between
\{p,q\}
and
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
so
(v_{1},v_{2})
is not an edge. Therefore
(p,v_{1}),(v_{1},q),(p,v_{2}),(q,v_{2})
must all be included at least once. The remaining two edges must hit all vertices exactly once, so the only option is to double up either
(p,v_{1})
and
(v_{2},q)
, or
(p,v_{2})
and
(v_{1},q)
. These are isomorphic options, so we only get
one
graph, see Figure
16
(d).

Case 4b:
\mathscr{G}_{3,3}
with
|V|=6
:
11
total graphs.
Let
G\in\mathscr{G}_{3,3}
have vertices
\{p,q\}\cup V^{\circ}
where
V^{\circ}=\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\}
. Since
\sum_{i=1}^{4}\deg(v_{i})=12
and there are at most
6
edges connecting
\{p,q\}
to
V^{\circ}
, there are at least
3
edges in the set
(V^{\circ})^{2}
. Since including any more than three edges in
(V^{\circ})^{2}
would create a cycle, there must be exactly three edges in this set, three edges in the set
\{p\}\times V^{\circ}
, and three edges in the set
V^{\circ}\times\{q\}
. The pair
(p,q)
is not an edge.

We classify graphs in
\mathscr{G}_{3,3}
with
|V|=6
by focusing first on the induced graph on
V^{\circ}
. Up to transpose-isomorphism, there are five possibilities:

1.

Path 1.
(v_{1},v_{2}),(v_{2},v_{3}),(v_{3},v_{4})
.

2.

Path 2.
(v_{1},v_{2}),(v_{2},v_{3}),(v_{4},v_{3})
.

3.

Path 3.
(v_{1},v_{2}),(v_{3},v_{2}),(v_{3},v_{4})
.

4.

Star 1.
(v_{1},v_{2}),(v_{1},v_{3}),(v_{4},v_{1})
.

5.

Star 2.
(v_{1},v_{2}),(v_{1},v_{3}),(v_{1},v_{4})
.

There are no graphs
G\in\mathscr{G}
that extends Star 2, since in this case
v_{1}
has three outgoing edges and so cannot accommodate an incoming edge.

Path 1:
5
Extensions.
In this case,
(p,v_{1})
and
(v_{4},q)
must be edges, since otherwise either
v_{1}
would be a source or
v_{4}
would be a sink. There are four remaining edges to place, each of which involves exactly one of
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\}
. Two of these edges go to
q
, and the other
2
come from
p
, leaving
{4\choose 2}=6
possibilities. Among this set of six possibilities, all are planar and two choices lead to transpose-isomorphic graphs:

•

The graph obtained by adding
(p,v_{1}),(p,v_{4}),(v_{2},q),(v_{3},q)
is isomorphic to the transpose of the graph obtained by adding
(p,v_{2}),(p,v_{3}),(v_{1},q),(v_{4},q)
.

There are no other transpose-isomorphisms, see Figure
17
(e.g. each of the
5
graphs in that figure has a different number of paths from
p
to
q
).

Path 2:
3
Extensions.
In this case,
(p,v_{1}),(p,v_{4})
and
(v_{3},q)
must be edges. We have three edges left to add between
\{p,q\}
and
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\}
, which must use
v_{1},v_{2},v_{4}
once each,
p
once and
q
twice. We have three choices, depending on where we connect
p
, see Figure
18
. These options are not transpose-isomorphic. To see this, note that the graphs where we connect
p
to
v_{1}
or
v_{4}
have a double edge, whereas the graph where we connect
p
to
v_{2}
does not. Also, connecting
p
to
v_{1}
results
6
paths going from
p
to
q
, whereas connecting
p
to
v_{4}
results in
5
paths.

Path 3:
2
Extensions.
In this case,
(p,v_{1}),(p,v_{3}),(v_{2},q)
and
(v_{4},q)
are necessary edges. We then need two edges between
\{p,q\}
and
\{v_{1},v_{4}\}
that use all vertices exactly once. The two possibilities are
(p,v_{1}),(v_{4},q)
and
(p,v_{4}),(v_{1},q)
. These are non-isomorphic options: in the second graph there is a path from
p
to
q
of length
2
, whereas no such path exists in the first graph.

Star 2:
1
Extension.
In this case,
(v_{2},q),(v_{3},q),
and
(p,v_{4})
must be edges so that
v_{2},v_{3}
are not sinks and
v_{4}
is not a source. We need to complete the graph with two more edges going out of
p
, one edge entering
q
, and one edge incident to each of
v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}
. Without loss of generality, we can therefore include
(p,v_{2})
as an edge since up to this point, the roles of
v_{2}
and
v_{3}
are interchangeable.

There are then two ways to finish the graph: either with
(p,v_{4})
and
(v_{3},q)
, or with
(p,v_{3})
and
(v_{4},q)
. The first of these options results in a graph in
\mathscr{G}_{3,3}
. The second of these options breaks the planarity constraint: in this case, the underlying undirected graph is the complete bipartite graph
K_{3,3}
, with
\{p,q,v_{1}\}
and
\{v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\}
being the two parts.

In summary, there are
5+3+2+1=11
total graphs in
\mathscr{G}_{3,3}
with
|V|=6
.
∎

Corollary
1.4
follows from the main theorem and examining the figures below.
